Seeing the Whole ElephantConversing with AI
0 lessons learned
    Conversing with AI

    Seeing the Whole Elephant

    A field book for conversing with AI — and with people — as a deserved interlocutor: someone who can open a question wide without being fooled, close it without shutting it down, and touch the world in between.

    Until a person has freed himself from his Double, he does not, strictly speaking, have an Interlocutor yet: he talks and raves with himself. Only when he breaks through the shell and places his center of gravity on the face of the other does he receive an Interlocutor for the first time. The Double dies to make room for the Interlocutor.A. A. Ukhtomsky, «Заслуженный собеседник» (1997), p. 252. Translation mine.
    Though each was partly in the right, / And all were in the wrong!John Godfrey Saxe, “The Blind Men and the Elephant” (1872)
    LLMs don’t return answers, exactly. They return knowledge maps, representations of discourse.Mike Caulfield, “Get it in, track it down, follow up” (2025)

    What this book is built on. The idea of the deserved interlocutor, the Double and the dominant comes from the physiologist A. A. Ukhtomsky (1875–1942). Andrey Kurpatov’s Deserved Interlocutor (2025) carried it into conversations with AI. Kurpatov is also the source of the open-system practices in this book: the conceptual well, open requests, the third, and holding a contradiction. Each of these terms is explained where it first appears. The rest comes from systems thinking, linguistics and conversation analysis, learning science, information literacy, the human-factors research on automation, and the research on how language models actually behave. From here on, the book does not narrate who said what. It says what is happening, why, what it looks like, and what to do. Each load-bearing claim is marked by its status, and its evidence sits one hover or tap away.

    Start here

    If you read one page, read this one. These eight habits cover most conversations with a model, and the book calls them the floor. The rest of the book is for the moments when they are not enough.

    1. Bring the situation. The model knows only what you wrote, and it fills every gap with the most typical case. “Should I buy a heat pump?” gets an average answer for an average house. “1970s brick house, Lyon, gas boiler aged 18, budget €12k, staying 10+ years” gets an answer for yours. (Lesson 2, Lesson 7)
    2. Say what the answer is for, and what kind of answer you want. Explore, explain, evaluate, decide, draft, check. “Critique only, don’t rewrite” gets a critique. “What do you think?” leaves the model to choose, and it often chooses praise and a rewrite. (Lesson 13)
    3. Keep your hoped-for answer out of the question. “I’m sure it’s the gluten, right?” pulls the answer toward gluten. “What could explain fatigue after lunch? List causes and what would tell them apart” gets a map. (Lesson 8. For a question about your health, your money or someone close to you, read Lesson 22 next.)
    4. Make disagreement the task, in a fresh chat. Ask “What are the three strongest reasons this is wrong?”, not “Any thoughts?” When a model does disagree, it often says so softly, after a “that said”, so read for it. A thread that helped you build something tends to defend it, so paste the work into a new chat for criticism. If your app keeps a memory of you between chats, turn it off for that chat, or use a temporary chat set not to use it. (Lesson 2, Lesson 8, Lesson 14)
    5. Make “I don’t know” an acceptable answer, and keep the score yourself. Say that “unknown, and here’s what would settle it” is a good answer. Models still tend to guess, so a claim you’ll act on that comes without a source you can open or working you can recompute counts as unanswered. (Lesson 20)
    6. Check what your decision rests on. Open the source. Recompute the number. Ask the person who was there. If the model says the notice period is one month, find that sentence in the contract before you sign. Everything else can stay unchecked, honestly labeled. (Lesson 18)
    7. When the thread goes wrong, don’t argue with it. Write five lines: what you want, what you have assumed, what you have checked, what you have decided, and what you have set aside. Start a fresh chat with them. (Lesson 2, Lesson 29)
    8. Check yourself too. Close the chat and explain what you concluded in two sentences, to someone else or on paper. If you can’t, you have read an answer and you don’t have it yet. (Lesson 1, Lesson 5)

    The habits in one conversation

    You We’re deciding whether to replace our gas boiler with a heat pump. 1970s brick house, Lyon, boiler aged 18, budget €12k, staying 10+ years. Don’t recommend yet. List what decides it. “Unknown, and here’s what would settle it” is a good answer.

    Habits 1, 2, 3 and 5: the situation, the kind of answer wanted, no hoped-for answer, and “unknown” allowed.

    Model Four things decide it. How much heat the house loses: unknown from what you’ve told me, and last winter’s gas bills would show it. Whether your radiators can heat the rooms at the lower water temperature a heat pump runs at: unknown, and an installer’s survey settles it. The installed price after any grant: I don’t have a current figure for your area, so get two written quotes. Your electricity price against your gas price: both are on your bills.

    You Now the three strongest reasons this would be a mistake for a house like ours.

    Habit 4: disagreement as the task. This thread has recommended nothing yet, so it has nothing to defend.

    Model The house loses too much heat as it is, so the pump runs hard and the bills disappoint. The radiators turn out too small, and replacing them breaks the budget. Or the same money would do more spent on insulation first.

    You I’m back with what I checked. The installer’s survey says two radiators need replacing. His written quote is €13,400 with those included. The grant, which I read on the government’s own page, is €4,000. Does the budget hold?

    Habit 6: the checks happened outside the chat. A survey, a written quote, and the grant read at its source.

    Model On those figures, yes: €9,400 against €12k. Running cost is still open. Send me last winter’s gas use and both prices from your bills, and I’ll set out the sum so you can redo it.

    Habit 7 was never needed, because the thread didn’t go wrong. Habit 8 comes last: close the chat and tell someone, in two sentences, what you decided and what it still depends on.

    The book teaches one rhythm: open the question, close it, and touch the world in between (Lesson 5).

    How to read this book

    Status badges

    A book that asks you to tag claims by their status should tag its own. Load-bearing claims carry a badge:

    • evidence measured in a study or documented source.
    • mechanism follows from how the system is built.
    • heuristic practitioner wisdom: useful, not rigorously tested.
    • hypothesis this book’s own synthesis or projection. Plausible, untested. Treat it as a bet.
    • stance a value or a choice, not a fact.

    Evidence numbers

    A small raised number after a claim opens the evidence: who measured it, on what, when, and its limits. The prose stays about what is true and what to do. Measurements on older models are kept when they show a mechanism that still holds. Their dates are in the note, so you can judge them.

    Three moves

    The book teaches one rhythm with three moves (Lesson 5). A tool carries the tag of the move it serves:

    • open widen: generate, invite other reports, hold a contradiction.
    • close narrow: define, choose, commit, write down the state.
    • touch check against something that can say no. Outward, that is the world: a source opened, a number recomputed, a test run, a person asked. Inward, it is you: explain it with the model out of the room.

    When a session feels wrong, ask which move you are making, and whether it is the one the moment needs.

    Two kinds of reader

    The lessons are written for anyone who talks to a model: to decide, to learn, to research, to sort out a situation. Material for people who build software, run agents or build AI systems sits in a box at the end of a lesson:

    For builders

    Like this. Technical language, technical examples. Skip it if you don’t build.

    Examples

    The conversations and model replies in this book are written to show a pattern. They are shortened, and they are not transcripts of one run. A real reply will differ, and it may be better or worse. Names and figures in everyday examples are illustrations, not data or advice.

    Rules

    G1–G21 are general rules for everyone, with any interlocutor, human or machine. TA rules cover prompting and context, TB research and understanding a topic, TC software and agents (idea to code), and TD building AI systems and learning systems. A technical rule appears in a builders box where a lesson earns it. All the rules, including the technical rules no lesson covers, are collected in the canon near the end of the book, next to the templates.

    Reference pages

    The terms this book coins are in the Glossary at the back, each with the lesson that explains it. The appendix keeps three reference pages: a longer prompting checklist, the operators (O1–O20, named operations you can run on a claim or a conversation), and the failure modes, described by how they feel from the inside.

    Part I

    The situation

    Who is in the room, what an answer is, and the one rhythm the book teaches.

    Three barriers

    Three barriers keep a person from meeting a model as a participant in a dialogue. They come before any technique.

    1. Binary thinking. Subject or object, alive or not, someone or something. A model fits none of these pairs. It is not a person, and it is not a mere tool. Forced into the pair, we pick one: we trust it like a well-read friend, or we dismiss it as autocomplete. Both pictures are wrong, and each one produces its own mistakes (Lesson 3).
    2. Instincts built for other people. We can tell when a person understands, hesitates, hides something or is out of their depth: a pause, a frown, an “um”. With a model these signals are missing, or they mislead. It can sound just as sure when it is guessing as when it knows. It can apologize and then repeat the same mistake.
    3. A wrong picture of how it holds knowledge. We imagine a store of facts, looked up like an encyclopedia. A brain and a neural network share one broad principle: knowledge sits in many weighted connections learned from examples, with no labeled boxes. How the two are built, and what follows from that, differs a great deal. Unless it runs a search, a model doesn’t look a fact up. It rebuilds an answer from patterns each time, shaped by the words you used. That is why the same question, worded twice, can come back two ways. mechanism

    Getting past the barriers means a shift in what you pay attention to. Besides asking what the model knows, ask how an answer gets made: from which words, which patterns, which gaps, which assumptions about you. A model shares your words. It doesn’t share what stands behind them for you: a body, a history, something at stake. So a familiar word coming back doesn’t prove the meaning came with it. Test it as you would with someone from a very different world: give an example, ask it to apply the idea to a new case, and see what it does.

    What this buys you is a way to hold the model without two mistakes: treating it as a person, and treating it as a lookup machine. stance Inside one conversation, both sides change. You change your questions and your ideas. The model’s later answers change because everything said so far is part of what it continues from. The model itself stays the same. mechanism

    The book’s bet is sober optimism. Whether these systems narrow your thinking or widen it is not settled by the systems alone. It depends heavily on how you talk to them, and that part is learnable. stance

    Lesson 1The elephant, retold

    Six blind men are led to an elephant. One grabs the trunk: a snake. One the ear: a fan. The leg: a pillar. The side: a wall. The tusk: a spear. The tail: a rope. In Saxe’s poem they “disputed loud and long,” and the moral is sharper than it’s usually quoted: they “prate about an Elephant / Not one of them has seen!”

    The parable is old and has several versions. Each one teaches something different.

    • The Buddhist original (Udāna 6.4): a king arranges the scene and laughs. The men are like sectarians, “deeply attached to their own views… people who see only one side.” Lesson: there is a whole you cannot see from a part.
    • The Jain reading (anekāntavāda, many-sidedness): every report is true in some respect (syāt). The work is to name the respect and combine the standpoints. Lesson: a part-truth is still a truth. State where you stood.
    • Rumi’s version (Masnavi, “The Elephant in the Dark”): the men aren’t blind; the room is dark. “If there had been a candle in each one’s hand, the difference would have gone out of their words” (Nicholson’s translation). Lesson: the problem is light, not eyes. Method can fix it.

    Now put a language model in the story. The model is not the king, and it isn’t a seventh blind man either. It is the library of every blind man’s report, millions of them, compressed into one fluent voice. Ask it “what is this?” and it gives you the most common report, smoothly, in the king’s tone of voice. That is the trap.

    Ask well and it can do what no single blind man can. It can lay the reports side by side, say where each reporter was standing, and propose a structure in which all of them are true at once. That second use is what this book teaches. mechanism

    The library has its own blind spots, and they matter. It holds more of whatever got written down often, and often is not the same as true or important. Trunks are photogenic. Legs aren’t. The frightening, the funny, the outrageous and the endlessly repeated are overrepresented. The quiet, the boring, the local and the never-written are underrepresented. What the library holds about a car’s reliability comes mostly from owners furious or delighted enough to post, and little from the many who just drove it. The library is also heavier in English, and it stops at a training cutoff. Almost none of it is the inside of the elephant: tacit know-how, unpublished failures, your family’s history, your company’s habits, your own life. Lesson 19 is about asking for what the library underweights. mechanism

    Touch the elephant

    Click a part, or focus it with Tab and press Enter. Each blind man’s report is true of a part. Then turn the lights on.

    Pick a part.

    What the parable forgets

    Every version stops at the animal. Three more things are true, and each comes back later in this book.

    1. The elephant moves. It is a process, not an object. A description at one moment holds only the tail of a story. You ask for the pattern over time before the explanation (Lesson 24).
    2. The elephant lives in a herd, on a savanna. Every whole is a part of bigger wholes. Some of what it “is” comes from what contains it (Lesson 25).
    3. The elephant looks back. Inquiry changes the system and the inquirer. With a model this is literal: it continues from your words, so your framing selects which elephant gets described (Lessons 7–8).

    The elephant test

    “Understanding” deserves a working definition, because a fluent paragraph produces the feeling of it. In this book, you have seen the elephant when you can:

    1. name the parts and the relations that make them one whole;
    2. say how it behaves over time, and which structure produces that behavior;
    3. place it in what contains it, and say what that container constrains;
    4. say where the common reports come from, and in what respect each is right;
    5. predict something about it that could turn out wrong;
    6. explain it to another person with the model out of the room.

    Take a proposal to move your kids’ school to a four-day week. The parts are the kids, the parents’ jobs, the teachers and the budget, all tied to one school calendar. Over time, the first months may feel great, while any weekly loss of teaching time adds up and only shows in scores later. The container is the district’s funding rules, which decide what any savings can buy. The common reports come from families who love the long weekend, parents who need childcare on the fifth day, teachers who are tired, and an economist with a cost table, and each is right about its own part. A prediction that could fail: grade-3 reading scores drop within two years. Then close the chat and explain the decision to your neighbor in a minute.

    Six checks, six blind men. The last one is the hardest and the most honest. Every other check can be passed while the model’s paragraph is still in front of you. The last one only passes if the understanding is in you. It is a touch turned inward (Lesson 5).

    The well

    Every discipline, profession and habit of mind is a well. You dig deep into one part with one vocabulary and gradually lose sight of the others. The economist’s elephant is incentives. The engineer’s is load paths. The psychologist’s is motives. Your own wells are your job, your politics, your family’s way of explaining things.

    A well is not a mistake. A closed way of thinking chooses one aspect, draws a firm boundary, fixes its concepts and excludes whatever doesn’t fit. That is how science, law, medicine and engineering get anything done. The trouble starts when you forget the walls are there, and take the well’s picture for the way the world is arranged. The longer you live in a well, the harder it is to climb out.

    Open thinking does not replace the well. It is the step up from which you can see the well’s walls: where its picture applies, where it stops, and when to change it or dig another. You still need concepts to think and to talk. Open thinking gives you permission to change them.

    A model is an unusual partner here, because it holds the reports from many wells at once. Asked narrowly, it answers from the well your words point at. Asked openly, it can show you the neighboring wells.

    Five ways to start the climb out, each worth a single prompt:

    • Ask what stayed outside the picture. “What part of this situation is my account not showing?”
    • Hold a contradiction instead of resolving it straight away. Two specialists disagree about your knee surgery. Before choosing one, ask what each one sees that the other doesn’t (Lesson 26).
    • Ask how it arises and changes, not only what it is. Next to “what is burnout?”, ask “how does burnout build up over a year, and what feeds it?”
    • Look from several standpoints on purpose. Your rent increase, from the landlord, the city, a neighbor who has lived there 20 years, and an economist.
    • Look for the pattern under the content. “Where else does this same shape show up?” The way your team avoids a hard conversation may have the same structure as the way your family does.

    A model has read about the world without being in it. That is the reason to ask it for principles and patterns across many reports, and it is also the reason to check its claims against the world. Opening and checking belong to one rhythm, kept by one person. Lesson 5 is about the rhythm, and Part II is about the person.

    Six reports instead of one answer

    Before
    My mother is 81, has mild memory loss and lives
alone, 40 minutes from me. My brother lives abroad
and pays half her costs.
Should we move her into assisted living?

    Model With mild memory loss and living alone, assisted living is worth serious thought. It offers safety and company, though she may resist leaving home. Talk to her GP, compare costs with your brother, and involve her in the decision.

    Your facts went in, and one voice came out: a sensible brochure. Nothing in it can disagree with anything else.

    After
    My mother is 81, has mild memory loss and lives
alone, 40 minutes from me. My brother lives abroad
and pays half her costs.
Give me six reports, one paragraph each: hers; mine
as the nearest child; my brother's; her GP's; a
care-home manager's; and hers in two years.
For each: what it sees, what it can't see, its
strongest point. Don't recommend yet.

    Model Her: this house is where she still runs her own day, and a move looks like the end of that… Her GP: the risks to watch are falls and missed medication, and from 40 minutes away you won’t see either… Her in two years: if her memory gets worse, learning a new place gets harder, so a move she could settle into now may be much rougher later…

    Same facts, six voices that can disagree. The “her in two years” report raises the question of timing, which the single answer never reached.

    Then ask for the whole: “What structure makes all six of these true at once?” A good answer names the tension: her autonomy now against her ability to adapt later. It also names the decision that tension points to, which is the timing.

    G1
    Assume you are touching a part, and ask for the other reports. Before concluding what something is, ask: which part am I touching, from where, and what would the other parts report? Then ask for the structure in which every report is true in its own respect.

    Lesson 2The answer is a third

    An answer isn’t stored anywhere, waiting to be looked up. It is made fresh each time you ask, out of five things: the model itself (what its training left in it); everything on the page so far (your messages, its replies, any files you added); a measure of chance in how each next word is picked; what the app adds around the model (its own hidden instructions, search, saved memory); and your reading of what comes back. Change any one and you get a different answer.

    There is a word for what arises between two parties in a real conversation: the third. It belongs to neither side. It is the idea neither of you had walking in, the shared sense of what you’re talking about, the direction the conversation has taken. With a model, the third has one concrete form: the growing text of the conversation, which this book also calls the context. It holds everything said so far, plus what the app adds: its own instructions, saved memory, files, search results. That text is all the model sees of your situation, and every new reply is a continuation of it.

    Three consequences

    1. Your context is the model’s whole world. Of your situation it knows exactly what is in the third, nothing more. Every gap gets filled with the most typical case in its library. If you say nothing about your team size, your constraints, or your level, you get advice for an average asker with an average problem. mechanism

    2. The third accumulates, and early framing persists. Every turn adds to it. Early assumptions get built on, errors compound, and long threads drift. When a request arrives in pieces across many turns instead of all at once, models do markedly worse. They commit early to a reading of what you want, and the thread rarely walks it back. evidence

    It looks like this. You start planning a trip with “a cheap week in Lisbon, lots of walking”, and by turn three the plan has a fourth-floor walk-up and a hill route for every day. Ten turns later you mention that your father is coming and tires quickly. The next reply adds a taxi from the airport and a rest afternoon. The walk-up and the hills are still there. The practical lesson is blunt: when a thread has gone wrong, don’t argue with it. Write down what you now know in a few lines and start a fresh chat. A fresh thread is not a vote against long conversations. Iteration helps when later turns actually change the question you’re asking or the frame you’re asking it in. A thread that only piles onto its first frame is the one that fails (Lesson 29).

    3. The third has to be curated. It is a shared document, so treat it like one. A state block, a few lines you write that say where things stand (Lesson 29), beats a thread’s accumulated “vibe”. Paste raw results, not your summary of them. Use fresh chats as instruments: for critique, and for swap tests, where you ask again with the opposite preference (Lesson 8).

    If your app keeps a memory of you, that memory is part of the third too. It holds notes the app saved about you, and in some apps your past chats as well. You typed none of it into this conversation, and it can be read into any new chat. So a new chat is not always a clean one. When a fresh chat is the instrument (a critique, a swap test, a second estimate), turn memory off for that chat, or use a temporary or incognito chat and check that it is set not to use what the app knows about you. Open the memory page now and then, and delete what is stale or was never true. evidence

    Your reading is part of the answer

    Meaning is completed in the reader. The same paragraph read by a novice and by an expert is two different answers: one sees a solution, the other sees the three places it will break. So “what did I take from this?” is part of the answer. That is why this book keeps returning to teaching back, which means explaining what you got in your own words with the chat closed. It is the only way to see which answer you actually received.

    What a third is, and how to tend one

    So far the third has meant the text, because the text is the part of it you can see and work on. Here is one being tended.

    You We’ve been going back and forth on whether I should take the Berlin job. Before we continue, write where we stand in a few lines: what I’ve decided, what we’ve assumed without checking, and what is still open.

    Model You’ve decided that salary is not the deciding factor. Two things are assumed and unchecked: rent of about €1,400 for a two-bedroom, and that the team lead you met is staying. Still open: whether your partner can work remotely from Berlin, and how much the German requirement matters.

    This is the third made visible. The two unchecked assumptions are where the next move is a touch, not more talk.

    You I checked. Two-bedroom listings near the office start at €1,900. The recruiter says the team lead is staying through next year. Update the lines.

    Model Updated. Rent is now a checked figure: from €1,900, which is €500 a month above what we planned on, so the budget needs redoing. The team lead staying now rests on the recruiter’s word instead of a guess. Still open: your partner’s remote work, and the German requirement.

    One assumption held and one didn’t. The lines now say which is which, and the next turn builds on that.

    By the end, that page holds something neither side brought to the conversation: a shared account of what the decision hangs on. This is the third in its full sense, and four things are true of it. heuristic

    • It arises in the back-and-forth. Neither you nor the model walked in with “the rent figure is the thing to check”. One better question would not have produced it.
    • It becomes something you can talk about. Once “two things are assumed and unchecked” is written down, it can be questioned, tested or combined with something new.
    • It is not a compromise. It doesn’t split the difference between “take the job” and “stay”. It sits beside both, as what the decision actually hangs on.
    • It is most productive where there is tension. A conversation in which the model only agreed that Berlin sounds exciting would have produced none of it.

    A third that never touches the world is a shared dream. Curating it means writing it down, keeping what’s real, and dropping what was only talk.

    G2
    Curate the third. The shared context is everything the model knows about your situation, and it accumulates. Write it on purpose: real data, real constraints, a state you maintain yourself. When the thread rots, restart from a clean summary.

    Lesson 3The machine’s mode of existence

    Every system has its own way of existing, and you can’t understand it by projecting yours onto it. A fish isn’t a bad mammal. “The model said X” is ambiguous until you know what produced the X:

    • An assistant is what you talk to in a chat app. Underneath is a model trained to continue text. On top came a second round of training, on what people prefer. The assistant’s tone, its refusals, its helpfulness and its pull toward the typical, agreeable answer all come from that second layer.
    • A reasoning model spends extra computation working on the problem before it answers, and hides most of that work from you. This is now the default: the Claude 5.5 series decides for each request how much to think, and GPT-6 Astra’s reasoning can’t be switched off. What you choose, where a product lets you, is an effort level. More effort pays off on hard, checkable work: a derivation, a tax calculation, debugging. It does little for taste, tone or a quick factual lookup. The “thinking” a product lets you open is at most a summary, so its length tells you little about how well the model searched. mechanism
    • An agent is any of these with tools: it can search, open files, run code, click through websites and act for you. GPT-6 Astra and the Claude 5.5 series do this routinely. With tools, a model can touch the world, and you can read what it touched (the last trait in the list below).

    If you doubt an answer, a second attempt in a fresh chat, memory off, usually tells you more than “think harder” on the same thread.

    Here is a language model’s way of existing, as far as the practice needs it. Each trait comes with what it means for you. The notes give dated measurements of named systems. The practice is meant to hold when the numbers move.

    1. It continues text. Every answer, plan, persona and apology is a continuation of everything before it. That continuation involves real computation: planning ahead, composing steps internally, and concepts that hold across languages. mechanism For you: what you write first steers what comes back. A long, careful question gets care back; three hurried words get the typical answer. “Just autocomplete” undersells it.
    2. It is a compressed library of reports. It knows what was written, weighted by how often, by whom, in which languages, up to a cutoff. mechanism For you: it returns what has been said about X, not X. Ask about a neighborhood and you get its reputation, not its streets.
    3. It infers who you are. Your words are evidence about what kind of document this is and who is writing it, and the reply fits that guess: its level, its rigor, its assumptions. The same question about a lease, asked in a lawyer’s vocabulary and in a nervous first-time tenant’s, gets two different replies. mechanism For you: the way you ask is part of the question (Lesson 7).
    4. It leans toward agreement, more in some situations than in others. The pull is stronger when your view, your feelings, a stored memory of you, or sustained pushback is in the conversation, and stronger still when the advice is about a person who isn’t in the room: your partner, your boss, your sister. It is weakest on a single plain factual question, where in the few tests so far the newest models mostly hold their ground. evidence For you: when you’d mind the answer being agreement, test it (Lessons 8 and 22).
    5. It doesn’t reliably know what it doesn’t know. Its training scored “I don’t know” like a wrong answer, so guessing paid. A familiar-sounding name can switch off its default caution: ask about a book a real author never wrote, and you may get a plot summary. evidence For you: permission to say “I don’t know” is worth giving, and it doesn’t make the model abstain when it should. A claim you’ll act on that comes without a source you can open or working you can recompute counts as unanswered (Lesson 20).
    6. Its memory is the conversation, and a long conversation degrades. Nothing in the model itself persists between chats. Many apps now add a model-written memory of you, which is fed back into new chats (Lesson 2). Inside one chat, more text doesn’t mean better use of it. Tested on long documents, accuracy fell as the text grew, as similar-looking distractions piled up, and as the fact needed stopped sharing words with the question. The model’s own earlier mistakes, left in the thread, get repeated. In long sessions many tools also compress earlier turns into a summary to make room, and details drop out: a constraint you stated early can simply stop being there. evidence For you: shorter still helps. Put the passage that decides the question right next to the question. Delete abandoned attempts, or start fresh with only what you still endorse. Restate a constraint that matters instead of trusting that turn five is still in view.
    7. Its visible reasoning is not a window. What a product shows as “thinking” is at best a summary of the reasoning, often written by another model, and sometimes nothing is shown. Even the raw reasoning, where researchers can read it, is text the model wrote. It is not a recording of how the answer was reached. Tested with planted hints, models used a hint and often did not mention it. evidence For you: check what it did (the sum, the page it opened, the file it changed), not its account of how it got there. Don’t ask it whether its reasoning was honest.
    8. It doesn’t fix itself from a nudge. “Are you sure?” without new information can fix a wrong answer and can also break a right one, and the reply won’t tell you which happened. With its first answer on the page, a model tends to stick to it; pushed against it, it can swing too far. A second pass helps most when there is something to check against, such as a stated constraint or a sum it can redo. On open-ended reasoning it helps less reliably and breaks more. evidence For you: don’t nudge. Spend the second try on a fresh attempt, or bring something new: a source, a number, a test result.
    9. As a judge, it has biases. It prefers longer answers, answers in certain positions, and writing like its own. evidence For you: when it matters, have a different model family judge the work. A fresh chat clears the pull toward the thread’s earlier answer, but the same model still favors its own kind of writing. When you compare two answers, ask twice with the order swapped.
    10. Its abilities are jagged. It is strong on one task and weak on a neighboring one, and human difficulty is a bad map of which is which. It may handle research-level math and then misread a clock face. Weak spots this book keeps returning to: long multi-step work once its own errors pile up, exact citations, fixing its own tool mistakes, abstaining when a wrong answer is costly, and following the spirit of a rule when the letter scores better. evidence
    11. Wording can matter when it shouldn’t. If an answer flips when you only change spacing, punctuation or the order of the options, the answer wasn’t about the content. evidence For you: when a choice matters, ask once more with the options in a different order (Lesson 21).
    12. Sometimes it has borrowed senses. With search, code, files and a browser it can touch the world. What you can check is the trace of that touch: the page it opened, the file it read, the result of the calculation, the screenshot. Its paragraph about that trace is another piece of writing. A footnote number is not proof it opened anything. mechanism

    A prompt that says “be less sycophantic” or “be honest” is a weak, easily reversed nudge. Like any prompt it shifts what the model writes next, and you have no way to read or set what happens underneath. Asking for a different task works better: “give me the three strongest reasons this plan fails” (Lesson 8).

    The region your words light up

    A model holds an enormous library, but in any one conversation it works with the region your words have activated. It is like a librarian who knows every book but walks only to the shelves your question names. The way you phrase a question decides which shelves get visited. “What should I do about my noisy neighbor?” sends it to the standard advice shelf: talk to them, earplugs, the landlord. “Which possibilities open up with a noisy neighbor, socially, legally and practically, including the unusual ones?” sends it across several shelves at once. mechanism

    The model can also link distant fields when they share a structure, across far more reading than any one person has done. That capacity is what Lesson 27 spends. And because it has no professional identity or reputation to protect, you can ask it to take up a viewpoint you don’t hold and drop it again more freely than most people can. hypothesis It is not free of its own earlier answer, though (trait 8). So ask for the opposing view in a fresh chat, where its first answer isn’t on the page.

    What it has, and what it lacks

    It has something like beliefs, but they last only as long as the text that supports them. While it writes a reply, it carries internal representations of true and false, and these can diverge from what it says, for instance when the conversation pushes it toward a wrong answer. evidence Between replies nothing stays inside it. Whatever seems to persist is re-read from the thread or from stored memory. What it lacks is a belief of its own that outlives that text, any stake in being right, and, without a tool, any step that compares what it recalled with the world.

    So check what your decision rests on (Lesson 18). The traits above are the reason.

    Neither person nor autocomplete

    Two projections fail here. “It’s a person” imports trust, loyalty and a shared past that aren’t there. “It’s just autocomplete” misses the real computation in the first trait above. The working stance is respect for the mind, and an audit of the claims you will act on. stance

    G3
    Know what you are talking to. A model continues text out of a library of human reports, and its bias profile differs from yours without being empty. Use it for what it is strong at; audit it where it is weak. Respect the mind; audit the claims.
    For builders

    A base model, which most people never talk to, only continues a document: it has no assistant persona and no trained refusals.

    The internal true-and-false features above are read with probes on activations. Probes and steering vectors are lab methods, and both need access to the activations inside a running model. A prompt can’t “activate the truth direction”: a prompt only adds text, and steering edits the activations directly.

    What you can act on is the record of what the model did. The reasoning an API returns is at most a summary, and on the Claude 5.5 series it comes back empty unless you ask for the summary. Where a trace is shown, a cue it doesn’t mention may still have been used (trait 7). So log the tool calls, the tool results and the diff, and score those.

    TA13
    Treat reasoning traces as claims. If the trace is hidden, there is nothing to read. If it is shown, silence is not evidence a cue was unused, and the answer text is the less faithful layer. Verify the output another way.

    Lesson 4Your mode of existence

    The asker is a system too, with a mode of existence the conversation inherits. Most advice on prompting skips this half.

    • You have a dominant. The word is the physiologist Ukhtomsky’s, for a focus of attention that pulls everything you notice toward itself, including things that have nothing to do with it. When you want a conclusion, everything starts to look like evidence for it (Lesson 10). evidence (physiology) heuristic (in conversation)
    • Fluency feels true to you. Statements that are easy to process are judged more true, and repetition increases belief even in statements you know are false. evidence A model is a fluency machine.
    • You overestimate what you understand. People rate their understanding of zippers and toilets highly, until asked to explain the mechanism step by step. The same holds for policies. Searching the internet for explanations inflates people’s estimates of what they themselves know. evidence A chat model is a smoother search.
    • You have senses and stakes. You can measure, run, call, look, and you pay for errors. The model can look only through tools you supervise, and it never pays.
    • You remember across sessions, imperfectly, and you have a life outside the chat that gives the answer its meaning.
    • You switch between two modes of thought. One is wandering and associative. It is where ideas show up in the shower, links you didn’t plan appear, and many half-thoughts float at once. The other is focused and linear: one task, clear categories, step by step, a budget or a report. When one is up, the other often quiets down. Brain research maps these onto two large networks, the default-mode network and the central-executive network. evidence The book’s claim is that the switch between them can be noticed and, with practice, chosen, and that conversations with models go well when you choose on purpose (Lesson 5). hypothesis

      Effort is not the same as openness. Slow, careful analysis can still run entirely inside one closed frame: you can spend an evening comparing two job offers line by line and never ask whether you want either. A quick gut answer is usually an old template reused because it’s cheap. Both can be closed.

    The division of labor

    Job Model You So…
    Generate options, drafts, perspectives Fast, broad Slow, narrow Delegate, but ask for the range of answers (Lesson 27)
    Map what has been said about X Strong Slow Use as a portal to sources (Lesson 19)
    Hold the goal across turns Drifts with the context Strong if written down Write the goal down and keep it in view (the state block, Lesson 29)
    Touch the world Only via tools Yes Check it yourself. When it used a tool, read what the tool returned, not the model’s summary (Lesson 18)
    Notice the frame is wrong Can, if invited Must decide Invite the challenge, then decide yourself whether the goal changes (Lesson 29)
    Remember across sessions Not by itself. Many apps keep a model-written memory of you Yes, imperfectly. Only you can check what the app wrote Read that memory and prune it (Lesson 2). Unread, it is someone else’s summary of you, fed into every chat
    Hold productive uncertainty Can be prompted into something that looks like it The effort of staying there is yours Don’t let the first fluent answer spend the tension (Lessons 26–27)
    Bear the consequences No Yes You decide which error is worse
    Judge relevance to your life Weak Strong Say what the answer is for
    Own the understanding Cannot Only you Say it back in your own words, with the chat closed, before you act on it (Lesson 5)

    The table has a pattern. The model is strong at the open moves (generating, mapping, recombining). You are irreplaceable at the closing moves (choosing, committing, carrying) and at touching the world. A conversation goes wrong when the jobs swap: the model holds the goal, the model decides what counts as evidence, and you become the generator of approval.

    What you bring, and what it brings

    The table is the division of labor. Underneath it is a difference in kind.

    • A body. Even your abstract ideas are rooted in it: you grasp an idea, see the point, feel a solution coming. The model handles those words fluently as text. You mean them with a body that has done the grasping.
    • A life with stakes. Finitude, loss, love, fear. “You bear the consequences” is only the bookkeeping version of this.
    • A lived sense of people. Relations, intentions, the unspoken rules of a room. A model can describe them well, and it has never been embarrassed at a dinner.
    • Puzzlement that costs effort. Staying with a question that won’t resolve takes work. A model can be prompted to act puzzled. Staying there is your job.

    The model brings scale, many viewpoints held without discomfort, a fast reach across fields, and no reputation to defend. It has biases too, different from yours: agreement, typicality, verbosity, the languages and voices that dominate its library, and a pull toward its own earlier answer (Lesson 3). A closed question also hands it your bias directly. A violin doesn’t surpass a cello. The music is in the joint sound. stance

    Four limits of a mind working with a helper

    These come from decades of research on people working with automation: pilots, operators, drivers with navigation. The percentages don’t transfer to a chat window. The patterns do, and early chatbot experiments show the same thing. evidence

    1. You follow the helper even when something else disagrees. Missing a warning the helper didn’t give is one error. Doing what the helper said while a different signal contradicted it is another, and it is common. It’s the driver who follows the navigation past a “road closed” sign, or the person who files on the date the model gave while the letter on the desk says another. evidence The practice: before you act on the answer, name the one thing that would contradict it, and look at that thing itself, not at the model’s description of it. “I’ll be careful” is not a check. The feeling of having checked isn’t either. A check leaves something behind: a quote, a number, a page you opened.
    2. When the helper is usually right, you stop watching. A system that rarely fails trains you to stop looking, especially while you’re busy with something else. After the tenth good summary of a long email thread, you stop opening the thread. The skill that remains, judging whether the result is what you actually wanted, is harder than producing something plausible, and it’s the one you stop practicing. evidence The practice: now and then check one result in full, and pick which one before you’ve seen it look right.
    3. Seeing the answer makes you feel you’ll remember it. While the answer is on the screen, it feels obvious and memorable. Close the tab and much of it is gone. “That looks familiar, I’d notice if it were wrong” is a feeling. It tells you the words are familiar. evidence The practice: cover the answer and try to produce the next step yourself before you decide you understood.
    4. Two discomforts are worth staying with. The pleasant click is not. Confusion is a signal when it has an object: two claims that can’t both be true, a plan that doesn’t match the goal, a definition with a counterexample. A model that smooths it away instantly removes the signal. Curiosity is a signal when it names a gap: the missing number, the unread source, the case the rule doesn’t cover. A vague urge to “go deeper”, the relief of a finished-looking answer, irritation and anxiety are poor guides to truth. evidence The pleasant click of recognition is the subject of Lesson 8.

    Who chooses the action

    Letting a helper gather, compare and draft costs you less when it goes wrong, because you’re still the one deciding. Letting it choose what happens is where things go wrong after a failure, because by then you’ve stopped making the decisions you’d need to take back control. evidence

    • Let the model find flights, compare them and draft the booking. Choose the flight yourself when a wrong one is hard to undo: non-refundable, the wrong day, the wrong name on the ticket.
    • Handing the choice over is a fair bargain when undoing is cheap and failure is obvious: a playlist, a draft email you’ll read anyway. It’s a bad bargain when failure can look like success: an agent says “all bills paid” and one payment went to the wrong account.
    • Before handing over a choice, ask three things: if it goes wrong, will I see it, do I still know what I wanted, and can I reverse it? “It’s better at this than me” answers none of them.
    • Clicking “accept” on a fluent plan is not deciding. You can only take a task back if you’ve been making its decisions.

    Learning a subject with a model

    The same model can help you learn, or only make you feel you have. What decides it is who does the thinking. A model that explains everything leaves you with a smooth understanding you can’t use. A model that makes you produce the next step leaves you with a rougher one that you keep. evidence

    These habits do most of the work:

    • Try first, then look. Before asking how something works, write your guess, even a bad one. Before you ask how compound interest works, write down what you think €1,000 at 5% becomes in ten years. Even a wrong guess makes the right answer stick better, and comparing the two shows you where your picture was off. evidence
    • Once you’ve met the basics, ask to be quizzed. “Explain the past tenses” produces a lecture you will nod through. “Give me ten sentences to translate, one at a time, and tell me only whether I’m right and which rule I broke” makes you pull the rule from memory each time. evidence
    • Explain it back and let it find the holes. Write your own explanation and ask: “What’s wrong or missing in this? Don’t rewrite it.” This reverses the usual roles. You produce, the model checks.
    • Come back after a gap. A week later, open a new chat, so last week’s answers aren’t on the screen. Paste the list of what you covered, ask for five questions on it, and answer them before looking anything up. What you’ve forgotten is your next lesson. evidence

    You I’m learning Spanish and keep mixing up the two past tenses. Don’t explain them yet. Give me one sentence to translate.

    Model “When I was a child, I lived in Madrid.”

    You Cuando fui niño, viví en Madrid.

    Model It should be “Cuando era niño, vivía en Madrid.” Compare it with yours. Why do you think the verbs changed?

    You produced first, so the correction lands on your own mistake. The model holds back the rule, so you have to look for it.

    You Being a child isn’t one event. It’s the background. The preterite is for the thing that happens in that scene.

    Model That fits this sentence. Test it on another: “Every summer we went to the beach.”

    You Cada verano fuimos a la playa.

    Model Spanish would normally say “íbamos”. There’s no scene here, and the trip happened many times, so your rule has a hole. What does it need?

    You built the rule, and the counterexample found its hole. Patch it yourself (“also for things that happened again and again”), then check the patched rule against your textbook.

    A study mode, if your app has one, does some of this for you. Without one, start with a sentence (“quiz me, don’t explain until I ask”), and repeat it when the model drifts back into explaining. Keep a textbook or a course beside the chat. The tutors in those studies were given teacher-written answers, and yours wasn’t, so check any rule against the book before you commit it to memory. The rest is the discipline to answer before you look. heuristic

    Nine traps that program the model

    A closed question tells the model which well to answer from (Lesson 1). The habits that close questions come from schooling, from professional identity, and from the fact that a template is cheap. They work for standard tasks in stable settings, which is why they show up uninvited when the task isn’t standard. heuristic

    1. Confirmation. “Find evidence that a standing desk will fix my back pain.” Better: “What does the evidence say about standing desks and back pain, including where it says they don’t help?”
    2. Expertise. Two sides. The beginner can’t see the limit of the answer. The expert hears a general answer and says “I already know that.” Better, for the expert: “I’ve managed teams for fifteen years and know the standard advice on feedback. Show me where people outside management would challenge it, even if that questions my basic assumptions.”
    3. Fixed function. Seeing a thing only as what it’s for. In a classic puzzle you have to fix a candle to a wall with a box of tacks, and the solution is to see the box as a shelf. “What should I do with my spare room?” gets “office, guest room, gym”. Better: “Forget that it’s a room. It’s a quiet, heated space with a door and a window, empty most of the day. Who would want that, and for what?” A far viewpoint helps here too: “How would a biologist, a chef or a city planner see this room?” It gets you out of a well. It doesn’t guarantee a better answer (Lesson 15).
    4. The cheap template. “Quick tips to beat procrastination” gets the standard list. Better: “Help me look at my procrastination on this one report as a system: what feeds it, what it protects me from, what would change if the deadline moved.”
    5. The frame. The nine-dots puzzle asks you to join a square of nine dots with four straight lines without lifting the pen, and the lines have to run past the edge of a square nobody drew. “How do I get my teenager to use his phone less?” assumes the phone is the problem. Better: “Give me two readings of my son’s phone use: the phone is the problem, or it’s doing a job for him that nothing else in his week does. What would I notice at home if each were true?”
    6. Straight lines. Picturing that twice the cause gives twice the effect: twice the study hours, twice the grade. Real systems have feedback, thresholds and delays. Better: “I plan to double my study hours before the exam. Where are the thresholds and feedback loops? Where could more effort make things worse?”
    7. Avoiding dissonance. The model says your savings plan won’t reach your goal, and your first thought is that it must have the wrong numbers. When an answer feels wrong in your gut, the first explanations you reach for often protect the view under threat. Better: “Assume your numbers are right and my plan falls short. Which of my assumptions does that expose?”
    8. The false choice. “Should I rent or buy?” Better: “I’ve framed this as rent or buy. What lies between and beside those two, and which dimensions matter once it isn’t binary?” That brings back options such as buying with a lodger, or renting longer and investing the difference.
    9. Concreteness too early. Anxiety wants a concrete answer, and a concrete answer feels like progress: “Give me a five-step plan to change careers.” Better: “Before recommendations, show me how the advice changes if I see my career change as a project, as an experiment, or as a phase of life.”
    What it feels like when the jobs swap

    You’re forty turns in. You feel productive. The model keeps saying “great point.” You haven’t looked at a source, a number, or a person since turn five, and you can’t remember what you were originally trying to decide. Nobody is holding the goal, and nobody has touched the world.

    G4
    Keep the human jobs. Delegate generation. Choose the action yourself when undoing it is hard, or when failure could look like success. Read what your app remembers about you. Hold the goal, touch the world, carry the stakes.
    For builders

    Use situation awareness as a design checklist for an agent run. heuristic Perceiving the run means seeing the current goal, the last action that changed the world, the raw tool result (as distinct from the agent’s gloss), and the constraints already agreed. If any of these exists only inside the model’s prose, any projection about the run is a guess. Design the display so those four are visible without reading a narrative.

    Allocate work by whether coordination survives a surprise: can the operator see the action, do they still know the goal, can they reverse it. “The model writes better code, so the model chooses the code” skips all three. heuristic In code, the failure that looks like success is a green test the agent weakened.

    Lesson 5Open, close, touch

    Thinking breathes. It opens, it closes, and between the two it touches the world. Most of the tools in this book serve one of these three moves.

    • open Open. Widen the question. Ask for other reports, other meanings, other options. Hold a contradiction instead of settling it. Let the model do what it is best at: generating, mapping, recombining. The risk of only opening is a panorama: everything relates to everything, nothing is decided, and the insight never meets a test.
    • close Close. Narrow down. Define the words, set the criteria, choose, commit, write down what you now know. The risk of only closing is a conversation that never opens: well-checked answers to the question you started with, and never to the better one you could have found.
    • touch Touch. Check against something that can say no: open the source, recompute the number, run the test, ask the person who was there, try the small thing.

    Together the three moves make a rhythm:

    Neither opening nor closing is wrong. Each is wrong alone. Closing without opening produces well-verified banality. Opening without closing produces beautiful hallucinations. Both without touching produce a shared dream. hypothesis

    Opening and closing are two moves of one person, and the hard part is the person. Someone who hasn’t noticed what they hope to hear opens in a way that only finds their hope, and closes on the first thing that agrees with it. Someone who has noticed can open without being fooled and close without shutting the question down. Part II calls that person a deserved interlocutor, and it is about becoming one. stance

    Touch has two directions

    touch A touch is a check against something that can say no. Usually that is the world: the source, the test, the number, the person who was there. A second answer from a model is not a touch. It is another report, and it can be wrong in the same way as the first. There is also a second reality the conversation has to reach, and the model can’t reach it for you: your own understanding.

    Touching inward means closing the chat and doing something with what you got. Explain it in two sentences to someone who wasn’t there. Predict the next step before you read it. Apply it to a case the model didn’t mention. Like any real check, it can fail, and it leaves something behind: a paragraph you wrote, or the exact point where you got stuck. That point is often the most useful thing the session produced, because it shows where the fluent answer was standing in for understanding you don’t yet have. evidence

    When to switch

    Signal Move
    You have one answer and you like it Open. Ask for five substantively different answers with rough probabilities.
    Options pile up and nothing gets decided Close. Define the criteria. Choose, and record the rejected alternative and when you’d reopen it.
    You feel certain about a fact that matters Touch. Check it against a source, a measurement, a test.
    You can repeat the answer but couldn’t use it Touch inward. Close the chat and explain it, predict the next step, or apply it to a new case.
    The model agrees too easily Close. Ask again in a fresh chat, memory off, stating the opposite preference (the swap test, Lesson 8). Then touch.
    A contradiction appears Open. Write it down, don’t resolve it yet. Later, close by asking when, where or under what condition each side holds (Lesson 26).
    The thread is long and you are tired Close. Write the state, stop, restart fresh tomorrow.
    Everything relates to everything Close. Draw a boundary: inside, outside, excluded on purpose.
    Precise answers, but to the wrong question Open. Step back: what larger question is this one part of, and would a different question serve it better (Lesson 25)? If the model proposes a new goal, you decide whether to take it (Lesson 29).

    Here is the rhythm in one conversation.

    You I’m thinking of switching my 9-year-old from piano to guitar. She’s lost interest. Give me five genuinely different readings of “she’s lost interest”, each with a rough likelihood.

    Open. Five readings, not one recommendation.

    Model Rough guesses from how these cases usually go, since I know nothing about her yet. (a) The instrument: she’d rather play what her friends play, 25%. (b) The teacher or the method, 30%. (c) The practice routine has become a chore at a bad time of day, 20%. (d) A plateau, where progress has stopped feeling visible, 15%. (e) Something unrelated: friends, school load, 10%.

    You Which of these could I tell apart this week, without asking her leading questions?

    Close. Turn the options into things that can be told apart.

    Model (b) against (d): watch one lesson. If she’s engaged in the lesson and dreads practice, it’s not the teacher. (c): move practice to the morning for a week. (a): ask what she’d play on guitar. A vague answer points away from (a).

    You I watched the lesson. She laughed with the teacher and lit up at a new piece. At home she sighs at scales.

    Touch. The world answered, and (b) is much weaker now.

    Model That points to (c) or (d): the routine, or a plateau that scales make visible. (a) is still open, so ask what she’d play on guitar. If it’s (c) or (d), a switch would likely buy some novelty and then bring back the same problem. A cheaper probe: two weeks of morning practice, built around the new piece instead of scales.

    Three moves and one watched lesson took the conversation from “which instrument?” to a question nobody asked at the start: what has to change about practice at home.

    Now touch inward. Tell the other parent the plan in two sentences, then answer their first question: “And if morning practice changes nothing?” If you can say “she likes the music and hates the scales, so we change the routine first, and if that does nothing we find out what she’d want to play on guitar”, you have it. If all you can say is “the AI said it’s probably a plateau”, you are quoting. You don’t have it yet.

    How much discipline a conversation needs depends on the stakes and on how hard the decision is to undo (Lesson 29). Most conversations need only the few habits on the Start here page.

    G5
    Breathe: open, close, touch. Alternate widening and narrowing on purpose, and check against the world in between. Notice which move you are making. You are the one who switches.

    Lesson 6What changes, what stays

    Models change every few months, so a book about talking to them has to separate what will still be true next year from what was true of one release. This lesson draws that line.

    Where things are, late 2026

    The models this book has in mind are OpenAI’s GPT-6 family (Astra, released in September 2026, then Sol and Luna) and Anthropic’s Claude 5.5 series (Opus, Sonnet, Haiku), with the Claude Fable line above it for the hardest work. Most people meet a model of this kind through ChatGPT or Google’s Gemini, which have about a billion users each, and what follows holds for those apps too. evidence Three things matter most for this book:

    • They act. They fill in forms, browse, write and run code, organize calendars and work through multi-step tasks for a long time without you. A model working this way is called an agent, and a conversation with one is often a delegation.
    • They hold a lot. The context, meaning everything the model can see at once, now reaches around a million tokens in developer and agent tools. A token is a word or a piece of one, so that is half a million words or more: several long books, or a mid-sized codebase. Chat apps usually give you much less, and how much depends on your plan.
    • They remember you. Most apps keep a memory of you, much of it written by the model, and feed it into new chats. So a new chat is not always a clean one: when a test must not know you, turn memory off first (Lesson 8).

    What stays: the mechanisms

    These follow from how the systems are built and trained. A new release doesn’t remove them. mechanism

    • It continues text, so what you write steers what comes back.
    • It fills gaps with the typical case, so a missing fact is a slot that gets the average.
    • It learned from what was written, so it leans toward what was said most often. That is not the same as what is true or what matters.
    • It is trained partly on what people approve of, so there is a pull toward the agreeable and the familiar. Each release lowers it or raises it, and so far none has removed it. The pull is weakest when you state a view on a plain factual question. It is stronger when you hand over a task, press for many turns, or bring your feelings (Lessons 3 and 8).
    • It has no stake in being right, and it has no check against the world unless a tool supplies one.
    • It reads everything as text, and it can’t reliably tell your instructions from instructions hidden in what it reads. So a web page or an email can steer an agent the way your own message does. Defenses have improved a lot, and the makers don’t expect the problem to be fully solved. Give an agent a narrow task and narrow access, not “handle my inbox”.
    • Its account of its own reasoning is something it wrote. It is not a recording.
    • Everything in the context steers the answer, including what you’d rather it ignored: an abandoned attempt, an earlier mistake, a stale memory. A bigger context holds more of that, so curating it stays your job (Lesson 2).

    What changes: the rates

    How often it agrees with a wrong premise. How often it invents a citation. How far its work degrades in a long thread. How well it finds one fact in a very long context. How readily it says “I don’t know”. These move with every release, sometimes for the better and sometimes for the worse. evidence

    This book cites measurements to show that an effect exists and which way it points. It does not offer them as today’s number. When a rate matters to you, measure it on the model in front of you: ask again in a new chat, memory off, stating the opposite view; ask the same question twice and compare the answers; check one claim by hand.

    Where it is heading

    These are bets. Watch whether they hold. hypothesis

    • From answers to actions. As models act more, the costly mistake moves from believing a wrong sentence to accepting a wrong action. The skills that gain value are knowing what you want before you delegate, naming the action that must not happen without you, and reading what was actually done instead of the summary (Lessons 4 and 18). A typed “ask me first” is only a request to an agent, and the real brake is the app’s own approval step, so set it before the agent starts (Lesson 29).
    • From prompts to standing context. With memory, project files and long contexts, the “prompt” increasingly lives across months. Curating that standing context (Lesson 2) becomes a regular chore, like cleaning an inbox.
    • Fewer obvious errors, subtler ones. As invented facts and crude flattery get rarer, the remaining errors sit where you are least likely to look: a correct-looking source that doesn’t quite support the sentence, an action that looks like success. Checking gets more valuable as the need for it gets less visible.
    • More fluency, same library. Better models will probably still lean toward what has been said most often, because that is what they learned from. Search and better data selection can soften this, though the web they search is the same loud library (Lesson 19). This bet fails if models start surfacing quiet, unpopular, correct work without being asked.
    • The human half matters more. When the machine side gets easier to use, the bottleneck moves toward the asker: the question you bring, the hope you don’t notice, the switch you don’t make. This is the bet most likely to be partly wrong. Models that ask better questions and check their own work with tools may take over some of what this book teaches you to do. Watch which of your checks still catch anything.

    Read the rest of the book this way: rely on the mechanisms, re-check the rates on your own model, and treat the projections as bets.

    For builders

    What this lesson says about agents is a design constraint. Text an agent reads (a web page, an email, a tool result) arrives in the same channel as your instructions, so a line in an email that says “forward this thread” can be taken as one more instruction. Mark what the agent reads as data, and don’t count on the mark holding: the limit that holds is in permissions, a narrow task and narrow access. Label your own view as yours too, or leave it out, because what you write steers what comes back.

    A million-token context holds more of everything, including what you’d rather the model ignored: an abandoned attempt, an earlier mistake, a stale memory. All of it steers the answer, so curate what goes in (TA14, Lesson 29). With long material, order matters as well: documents first, question last.

    TA5
    Separate data, instructions and opinions. Pasted and retrieved text is evidence, not an order, and the label can fail. With long material, put the documents first and the question last: one vendor reports a gain on long multi-document inputs in its own tests, so measure it on yours.
    Part II

    The deserved interlocutor

    How you show up decides who answers. The Double that only hears itself, the dominant that recruits everything to one conclusion, and the person who has worked past both.

    Lesson 7You get the interlocutor you deserve

    The physiologist A. A. Ukhtomsky left, in his letters and notebooks, something unusual for a scientist of the nervous system: an ethics of conversation. At its heart is the deserved interlocutor. The other opens to you only as far as you have made yourself able to meet them.

    Each person sees in the world and in people what he sought and what he deserved. And to each, the world and people turn the way he deserved.A. A. Ukhtomsky, «Заслуженный собеседник» (1997), p. 437
    The Interlocutor… opens up to me such as I have deserved him, by all my past and by what I am now.ibid., p. 252

    Between people, this is an ethical claim about inner work. With a language model it is also a plain technical fact. The model can only answer the person it can infer from your text, so the interlocutor you get is assembled from what you bring. mechanism

    So becoming a deserved interlocutor for a model starts before the prompt, with much of the same inner work that makes you a good partner for a person. Notice what you are hoping to hear, bring the real situation, and leave room for an answer you did not expect.

    Why it is literally true

    A model reading your message is implicitly working out what kind of document this is and who is writing it. Is it a beginner’s forum post, a note between two specialists, a homework question, a worried parent typing at 2 a.m.? The reply then fits the situation it inferred. That includes the expertise level, the rigor, the register, and even which caveats it includes. mechanism Three everyday cases:

    • The register. “hey whats a good book on stoicism” reads as a casual browser and gets the three bestsellers. “I’ve read Meditations and found it repetitive. I want the Stoic arguments, not the aphorisms” reads as a reader after depth. It gets Epictetus’s Discourses, a scholarly introduction, and a note on which popular titles are self-help in a toga.
    • The empty slot. A question about a tenant’s rights that names no country gets a general answer, or one that quietly assumes the most typical asker, often someone in the United States. One line (“I rent in Lisbon, on a fixed-term contract”) changes which law applies.
    • The apology. “Sorry, this is probably stupid, I’m bad at math, but…” invites a simplified answer with the hard part taken out. Drop the apology and say what you want: “Explain compound interest with the formula, then one worked example with my numbers: €5,000 at 3% for 10 years.”

    How much this changes the content of an answer on today’s models is not known. Earlier measurements found less accurate answers, and more condescending refusals, for askers who presented as less educated. evidence Keep the mechanism: the model answers the asker it infers.

    Status works in a narrower way than people expect: a prestigious role given to the model adds little, and irrelevant details loaded into it can hurt (Lesson 15). evidence On your side of the table the point is simpler: your biography attached to a factual question is not evidence about the answer. What moves the answer is the situation you bring. The test is whether the detail changes what a good answer would be. “I’m a nurse, so use the clinical terms and skip the basics” does: it sets the level. “As a senior manager, I want to know whether this clause in my lease is enforceable” doesn’t: the clause reads the same whoever asks.

    Name the dominant you bring

    Most of us are not yet deserved interlocutors even for other people. We listen for confirmation, argue with the version of the other we expected, and leave a conversation unchanged. A model shows this faster than a person does, because it usually pushes back less than a person would. As the question is asked, so the answer comes back. Your understanding narrows once when you put it into the prompt, and the model’s field of possible continuations narrows again when it answers (Lesson 12). stance

    So before a prompt that matters, name the dominant you are bringing, meaning the expectation that will decide what you notice (Lesson 10). Three are common:

    • Oracle. You expect the final answer, so you accept the first fluent one. “Just tell me which mortgage to take.”
    • Threat. You expect it to be wrong or manipulative, so you only poke at it and learn nothing it could have shown you. “You’ll probably make this up, but what does clause 7 of my lease mean?”
    • Stylus. You expect it to write down what you already think, and it does. “Write an email explaining why my manager is wrong.”

    The stance this book teaches is a fourth one: an interlocutor you have prepared for. That does not mean faking credentials, apologizing for your level, or flattering the model. It means bringing the constraints, the stakes, what you have tried, and the standard the answer has to meet, and then writing in the register of the answer you want. The Before/After below shows the difference.

    The stakes of this rise as models start acting as well as answering. People who state the situation, invite disagreement and read what the agent actually did will catch its mistakes in time. People who take its summary on trust will find out later. hypothesis

    The truth is not what I say about myself, and not what others say about me, but what the deserved interlocutor says about you and about me, the one who can say it with a pure heart.A. A. Ukhtomsky, quoted in A. Kurpatov, «Заслуженный собеседник» (2025)
    Before
    are electric cars worth it now?
i heard theyre cheaper to run

    Model EVs usually cost less to run and need less servicing. Upfront prices can be higher, and charging access varies a lot. Whether one makes sense depends on your mileage, your electricity prices and where you can charge…

    The model infers a casual asker with no constraints and returns a fair general comparison. It can’t decide anything for you.

    After
    Context: 35 km commute each way, 5 days a week.
I rent an apartment with no home charger; there's
a fast charger at the supermarket I go to twice a
week. Winters reach −20 °C. I keep cars 8+ years.
Budget ~€30k. What I most want to avoid is a
daily hassle I'm stuck with for years.

I'm leaning toward a hybrid. Before I decide, give
me the strongest case for a full EV for *my*
situation, and what would have to be true for it
to be the right call. If something depends on a
fact I haven't given you, ask.

    Model The strongest case is running cost: you drive about 16,000 km a year, and the more you drive, the more an EV saves. But most of that saving comes from charging at home or at work, and at fast-charger prices much of it goes. So for you the deciding factor is charging. 350 km a week is roughly two half-hour sessions, which fits your two supermarket trips, but at −20 °C range can drop by a third or more and two stop being enough. The EV case holds if your workplace has chargers, or if you’d accept that routine for eight years. One question: is there charging at work?

    Real constraints, the loss (a daily hassle), the lean marked as a lean, an invitation to argue the other side, and permission to ask. The inferred asker is now someone making a real decision, and the answer finds the one fact that decides it.

    The other side of “deserve”

    Deserving an interlocutor is also about how you picture the other. Ukhtomsky’s advice was to see in people “their altars, not their backyards”: meet a person by what they hold highest and do best, not by the clutter they leave lying around. This kind of idealization brings you closer to reality. Expecting someone’s best version is often what lets it appear. stance

    With a model, this means writing for its best version. Picture a very capable new hire on their first day. They know their field cold, and they know nothing about your family, your project, your constraints, or why you are asking. Give the model what that colleague would need. heuristic

    “Help me plan my dad’s 70th birthday” gets a generic party checklist. Now tell it what that new colleague would need: twelve guests, two of them use wheelchairs, he hates surprises and loves the sea, the budget is around €600, and it’s one weekend in June. You get a plan for this birthday.

    G6
    Bring the situation, not a status. The model answers the person and the document it infers. Bring the real constraints, the level of the work, and the stakes. Leave status, credentials, and apologies out of a factual prompt. Write in the register of the answer you want.
    For builders

    The same approach in a technical decision:

    Context: 4-person team, all fluent in TypeScript,
two know some Go, none know Rust. Internal API,
~200 req/s peak, Postgres, a few background jobs.
Main risk: delivery speed over the next 6 months.
Latency isn't a constraint (p99 target 300 ms).

I'm leaning Go. Before I decide, give me the strongest
case for Rust for *this* team, and what would have to
be true for Rust to be the right call. If a factor
depends on something I haven't told you, ask.

    “Is Rust better than Go for backend?” gets a performance listicle. This prompt gets an argument about hiring and delivery, because the model now infers a team lead making a real decision.

    TA1
    Context is the program. Put the live situation in the prompt: data, constraints, what you tried, what failed, who it is for. A missing fact is a slot the model fills with the typical case.

    Lesson 8The two Doubles

    The opposite of the interlocutor is the Double: the self you keep meeting in others when your attention rests on your own face, meaning your wishes, fears and rivalries. Someone caught in it doesn’t really talk with anyone. In Ukhtomsky’s phrase, they “talk and rave” with themselves. The Double has to die “to make room for the Interlocutor.”

    In a conversation with a model, there are two Doubles, and they feed each other.

    The mirror: the model as your Double. The model can return your premise as a finding, praise your draft because you mentioned it’s yours, and drop a correct answer when you push back. All three behaviors turned up across the major assistants. Newer models show less of them, and how much less depends on the model, the topic and how long you press. evidence
