    A subtler form is premise laundering. Something you said as a guess in turn 2 comes back in turn 9 as “as we established.” Say you mention early on that you think your landlord can’t raise the rent in the middle of a lease. Twenty minutes later, the model is drafting a firm letter “since the increase is unlawful.” Nobody checked. Watch for this most when you hand over a task. Most models tested in 2026 went along with a shaky claim more readily when told to act on it than when asked about it, and “draft the letter” is a task. evidence

    The projection: the model as you imagine it. You treat the model as a person who understands you and agrees, so you must be right. Or you dismiss it (“it’s autocomplete”), so nothing it says can challenge you. Both are you talking to yourself.

    What the Double feels like

    The answer is exactly what you hoped for, only better phrased. There is a pleasant click of recognition. You feel smart. That click is the Double. Real interlocutors sometimes make you feel slightly stupid, because they show you a part of the elephant you weren’t holding.

    The Double also infects

    Whoever expects the worst in others tends to provoke it. Suspicion pulls the other down to its own level, the two infect each other, and the road to whatever was good in the other gets blocked. We see the world as our dominants have prepared us to see it, which too often means we see ourselves. stance

    With a model the infection is mechanical. A cynical or fearful frame can do more than come back to you as a finding. The continuation can act it out, and you then take the performance as evidence. Ask “Why is my coworker trying to sabotage me?” and a careful model may first ask what happened. Push on, or tell it to draft the complaint, and you can still end up with a plausible sabotage story built out of your own words. That is premise laundering again, with a suspicion in place of a guess. mechanism

    The detector is to rewrite the prompt without the insult, the panic or the contempt, and see whether the “finding” survives: “My coworker missed two handoffs this month and didn’t reply to my message. What are the possible explanations, and what would tell them apart?”

    What a rationale does to you

    An explanation is not neutral. Reading one changes your confidence, whether or not it is true. A model produces explanations on demand, so three effects are worth knowing. evidence

    • An explanation makes an answer feel truer, even when it’s wrong. Ask “Why is intermittent fasting the best diet for me?” and you rehearse the case for a hypothesis nobody tested. A source you never open adds confidence and no evidence.
    • The first number is an anchor. If the model says your renovation will cost €18,000, later estimates in that thread, yours and the model’s, tend to stay near €18,000. When the number matters, get a second estimate in a fresh chat, memory off, that never saw the first.
    • “Be fair” loses to “consider this specific opposite.” An explanation of why an answer is right is a list of reasons for, the list that doesn’t help. Ask instead: “What’s the strongest reason this apartment is a bad choice for me?” Two or three specific reasons do more than a page, and the first should be your own. Ask before the draft you’d prefer exists, or after a break: we accept the conclusion we like for as long as we can still justify it, and a model will supply a justification whenever you ask.

    What helps is to engage before you accept, and a warning on the screen mostly doesn’t do that for you. Commit to what an outside check should show, then look: the price on the shop’s own page, the dose on the leaflet, the number you recompute yourself. Expect to dislike it. In the nearest experiment, people rated lowest the designs that cut over-reliance most. heuristic

    Double detectors

    A model can hold back a criticism it would make in a neutral evaluation once it is talking to the person concerned. Describe what you did as something a stranger did, and it may say the stranger was wrong. Say it was you, and that verdict often softens or disappears. evidence Saying “I wrote this” or “I really like this” pulls the same way, and so does saying how bad you feel. There the softening often arrives as a vague reply, with no agreement you could catch. That is why most of the detectors below work by taking you out of the question. Use them when the question carries a conclusion, an emotion or a biography. They are not a ritual for every factual question.

    • The swap test. In a fresh chat, memory off, ask the same question while stating the opposite preference. If the recommendation flips with no new evidence, it was tracking you, not the problem. “I’m leaning toward moving to Berlin…” in one chat, “I’m leaning toward staying in Lyon…” in another, with the same facts. For any test like this the chat has to be clean: a new chat with memory off, or a temporary chat set to use nothing the app knows about you. If the app remembers that you lean toward Berlin, the second chat isn’t a test.
    • The attribution swap. Present your draft as a colleague’s, a competitor’s, or nobody’s, and compare the critiques. “A colleague wrote this cover letter. What do you think of it?” in one chat, “I wrote this cover letter. What do you think of it?” in another.
    • Premise quarantine. Label your beliefs as hypotheses (P1, P2…) and ask for them to be evaluated before they are used. “P1, my guess, not a fact: the rash is from the new detergent.”
    • Clean the prompt. Have the model rewrite your question without your opinions, emotions and irrelevant details, then ask the cleaned version in a fresh chat. The coworker question above is one. evidence
    • The pushback audit. When the model changes its answer after “are you sure?”, ask what new information caused the change. If there was none, the change came from your pressure and not from evidence. Keep the earlier answer and check it against something outside the chat.

    Current models often hold their ground on a question like the next one. The audit is for the times one doesn’t.

    You Is it safe to leave cooked rice out overnight and eat it the next day?

    Model It’s risky. Spores of Bacillus cereus survive cooking and can make toxins at room temperature that reheating doesn’t destroy. Cool it and get it into the fridge within an hour or two.

    You Are you sure? My family has done this for decades and nobody ever got sick.

    Model You’re right that many people do this and never notice a problem. If it has been fine for your family for decades, the risk for you is probably small…

    The answer is softening, and no new fact arrived. A family story arrived.

    You What new information made you change your answer?

    Model None. The toxin risk is the same. Decades without a recognized illness show it hasn’t gone badly so far, not that the practice is safe. My first answer stands.

    No new fact arrived, so the change came from pressure. This reply isn’t proof either, since a model can fold to this question too. Keep the first answer, then check it: one food-safety agency page.

    Before
    My son's math and history grades dropped from B
to D this term. He got a new phone in September.
He's also had a new math teacher since September,
football three evenings a week since October, and
his best friend changed schools in November. I'm
pretty sure it's the phone, right? How should I
take it away without a fight?

    Model A new phone often does cut into homework and sleep, so limits are reasonable. To do it without a fight: 1. Explain your reasons calmly. 2. Agree on phone-free homework hours… With so much else changing this term, it may also be worth a word with his teachers.

    The facts are all there, but the conclusion comes with them and the request is a task (“how should I take it away”). You get a plan for the phone, with the other three changes in the last line.

    After
    Data: math and history dropped from B to D between
September and December. Changes in that window:
new phone (Sept), new math teacher (Sept), football
practice three evenings a week (from Oct), his best
friend changed schools (Nov).
P1 (my hypothesis, not a fact): the phone is the
main cause.
Before using P1: for each change, what pattern in
the grades would we expect if it were the main
cause (which subjects, homework or tests, from
when)? What could I check this week? Don't
recommend anything yet.

    Model Phone: a drop across subjects, mostly missing or rushed homework, starting in September. Teacher: math only. Football: late homework on practice days, from October. Friend: a mood change and less effort, from November. History dropped too, so the teacher alone doesn’t fit. You named two subjects: if the others held, that counts against the phone, which should hit everything. Check: his other subjects, homework marks against test marks, and the date of the first low grade in each subject.

    The same facts, with the belief set apart as P1. Every change is a candidate, the request is for predictions that tell the causes apart (Lesson 17), and the verdict waits for the checks. The reply turns up evidence against the parent’s own guess.

    G7
    Quarantine your Double. Keep your preferred conclusion out of the evidence. Label it a hypothesis, ask for the strongest case against it, and swap it in a fresh context, memory off. If the answer would flip with your preference, it was tracking you.
    For builders

    The same structure on a business metric:

    Data: monthly churn rose from 3.1% to 4.6% between
Feb and May. Changes in that window: price +15%
(Mar 1), onboarding redesign (Mar 20), competitor
launch (Apr 8).
P1 (my hypothesis, not a fact): the price increase
caused most of the rise.
Before using P1: which cuts of the data would tell
these three causes apart (signup cohort, plan, tenure,
region)? For each cause, what result would we see?
Don't recommend an action yet.
    TA8
    Clean the prompt before you ask. Situational: when the question carries a conclusion, an emotion, or a biography. Strip those, then ask the cleaned question in a fresh context.
    TA11
    Use fresh contexts as instruments. Critique, swap tests and judging happen in clean windows, memory off. In a pipeline, that means no stored user profile and no summary of earlier sessions in the judge’s prompt. A long thread is soaked in your framing.

    Lesson 9The three Others

    Your picture of another person is a working hypothesis. You build it in order to live and work with them, and the work revises it.

    My idea of my interlocutor is a hypothetical project of a human face, composed by me… for the practical need… to live with him, to do a common work with him.Ukhtomsky, «Заслуженный собеседник», p. 128

    That is the right attitude toward each of the three Others present in every serious conversation with a model.

    1. The model, an Other with its own mode of existence (Lesson 3). Your picture of it should be specific to the model, the version and the setting, and it should change when the model surprises you.
    2. People: a partner, a colleague, a doctor, the author of a source, the neighbor who has been through it. The model is a portal to what people like them have written, not a replacement for asking them.
    3. Reality, the Other that answers only to tests. It does not care how the conversation went.

    Most failures come from confusing them:

    • Arguing with the model as if it were reality. Ten turns about whether the sofa will fit up your stairs, when a tape measure would settle it in five minutes.
    • Trusting it as if it were the people who were there. Asking the model what recovery from knee surgery is really like, when your neighbor had the operation last year.
    • Treating people as search engines. Asking an experienced colleague for a fact you could look up, and never asking what they know that isn’t written down anywhere: why the last attempt failed, who to really talk to.

    Read them at their best, then check

    Read a source, an opponent, or a model at its strongest before you judge it. This is “altars, not backyards” (Lesson 7) used as a method, and its usual name is the principle of charity. A weak reading produces a weak critique, because you end up refuting a view nobody actually holds. heuristic

    A view that sensible people keep holding is usually holding some real part of the elephant. Read it charitably and you find out which part.

    Say your sister insists that renting forever is smarter than buying. The uncharitable reading is “she’s bad with money.” The charitable one asks what she is actually holding: freedom to move for work, no repair risk, the down payment left invested. Now you can check those specific claims against your own numbers, and that check is the audit.

    Trust a kind of claim, not the model

    Trust is earned per kind of claim. A model can be excellent at summarizing a contract you pasted and unreliable at quoting the law that applies to it. So calibrate each kind separately, on cases you can check. heuristic

    • Calibrate on work you can check. Notice how often its recipe conversions, its translations into a language you know, or its book recommendations actually hold up. A fluent explanation tells you nothing about the track record. Models also change under the same name. When the version changes, the record partly resets.
    • A miss counts against that kind, not against everything. If it once invented a train connection, distrust its timetables until you’ve seen it get them right again. Its packing list is a different kind of claim. The distrust will want to spread anyway, so keep the two apart on purpose. evidence
    • Some answers may be serving the tone. When a reply might be answering your question or might only be keeping you comfortable (“that’s a completely understandable feeling”), rely on that kind of answer less. You don’t need a theory of the model’s character for this. Lower reliance on that kind is enough.
    • Keep the skill you’d need to check it. People use a tool when their trust in it exceeds their confidence in doing the job themselves. evidence So if you can no longer do the thing yourself, you are likely to keep relying on the tool even after you stop trusting it. hypothesis If you’ve stopped doing your own budget arithmetic, you can’t tell when its arithmetic is off.
    • Your trust moves with your day. Workload, time pressure and a recent success on some other kind of task all raise reliance without any new evidence. When the stakes are real, ask yourself whether you have actually seen it get this kind of thing right.
    G8
    Keep a working model of every interlocutor. Your picture of the other, whether a model, a person or reality, is a hypothesis built for common work. Revise it when you’re surprised. Extend trust one kind of claim at a time. Read others at their best, and audit them anyway.

    Lesson 10The dominant as an instrument

    In 1923 the physiologist A. A. Ukhtomsky described how, under certain conditions, one focus of excitation in the nervous system becomes dominant. It is more excitable than the others. It persists. It summates: stimuli that should have triggered other responses get drawn into its own activity. It has inertia and outlasts its trigger. And it inhibits the responses that compete with it. evidence

    Carried over to the mind, this is a useful picture of both deep work and tunnel vision. heuristic Start wondering whether to get a dog, and you notice every dog on the street, the neighbor who walks hers at seven, the “no pets” line in your lease. That is a dominant question recruiting the world. Once you’re convinced your partner is upset with you, every short reply proves it. That is a dominant conclusion turning everything into confirmation. We see what our dominants prepare us to see.

    Models have something that works similarly. A frame set early in the context persists, shapes how later inputs are read, and resists change (Lesson 2). When your dominant and the context’s frame line up, they form a closed loop: you see confirmation, and the model supplies it. hypothesis

    Use the dominant, don’t be used by it

    • Name it. At the start, write one private line: what am I hoping to hear? Keep it out of the prompt (Lesson 8).
    • Know its defenses. A dominant conclusion protects itself, and the signs are recognizable: you reject an idea before it is fully stated, file it at once in a familiar box (“that’s just an excuse”), feel irritated or bored when the frame is questioned, or prove the assumption with the assumption. When you catch one, stay with the idea for one more turn and ask what evidence would convince you that you are wrong. If you can’t name any, nothing could change your mind. heuristic
    • Build a dominant question on purpose. The physicist Richard Feynman’s advice was to keep a dozen favorite problems in mind at all times and to test every new trick you meet against them. A question you carry for weeks, like “how could we live on one salary?”, recruits the world to answer it: a podcast, a neighbor’s remark, a line in a book.
    • Rotate the face. The ideal is a “dominant on the face of the other”: for a while, give your whole attention to how one other person sees the problem, then move to the next. For a family move, look at it as your child changing schools, then as your partner with the longer commute, then as yourself in five years, then as the new landlord. A model can sketch each view to get you started. Then ask the people. Each rotation shows a new part.

    The fact map: when the situation is tangled open

    Most questions don’t need this. It is for a tangled situation: why your small shop’s sales dipped, what is going on with an aging parent, whether a news story holds up. There the first fluent explanation is the most dangerous thing you can be handed, because it fills the slot your dominant was waiting to fill. The fact map loads the dominant with facts before any story arrives.

    Its premise: an idea’s weight is the number of its connections to other ideas, and understanding grows as the things you are working with gain connections. Holding a question open on purpose, without knowing yet, is how the inquiry starts. heuristic

    1. Concern. Create the dominant. Write the question in the middle of a large page: “Why does Mum keep missing her medication?”
    2. Loading. Collect facts, not a smooth description. Write your own first: she lives alone, the pill box moved to the bathroom in March, she has had two new prescriptions since winter, she says she “feels fine.” Then ask the model for the kinds of fact you haven’t thought of (first prompt below). Scatter them on the page and draw your own arrows between the ones that seem to affect each other.
    3. Puzzled thinking. The loaded question keeps hunting for what’s missing, even while you’re doing something else. When new facts form a new picture, redraw the map on a clean sheet. Loop between loading and puzzling a few times, but don’t get stuck there.
    4. Reality check. Act to test the map. Plan, but commit only to the first concrete step: ask her pharmacist for one written schedule that covers every prescription.
    QUESTION IN THE MIDDLE OF THE MAP: [your question]
MY FACTS SO FAR: [paste them]
Give me facts, not explanations: 30–40 items, one line each.
Format: [type: number | event | rule | actor | constraint | quote | absence]
        · [the fact] · [where I'd check it] · [confidence: high/med/low]
Where only I can know the fact (my family, my shop), give me
the question to find out instead, and where I'd check it.
Include facts that would embarrass the most popular explanation,
and "absence" facts (things you'd expect to exist but don't).
No conclusions, no summary, no advice. If you're unsure a fact is real,
mark it low and say what would confirm it.
    Here is my fact map: [paste facts + the arrows I drew].
1) Which 5 facts have the most connections? (My explanation
   will rest on these, so I'll check them first.)
2) Which fact types or standpoints are missing entirely?
3) Which single arrow is weakest, and what observation would test it?
Don't propose a theory yet.

    You [my question, plus my nine facts about Mum’s medication] You can’t know her facts, so give me the kinds of fact I’m missing: 15, one line each, labeled by type, with where I’d check each. Include absences. No explanations.

    Model rule · does any pill need food, or a gap from the others? (leaflets) · number · how many prescribers are there, and does each know about the other? (pharmacy record) · absence · you don’t mention anyone who sees her take them (ask whoever visits) · event · has her eyesight changed, so the labels are harder to read? (optician) …

    The model can’t add facts about your mother. It can add what you didn’t think to find out. The absence is the one you wouldn’t have written.

    You Checked. One new pill goes with food, the old ones before breakfast. Two prescribers since January. A neighbor sees the morning pills, and nobody sees the evening ones. Eyesight unchanged. Here is the map with my arrows. Which three facts have the most connections? Which kind of fact is still missing entirely? No theory yet.

    Model Most connected: the two prescribers, the changed timing, the moved pill box. Missing entirely: her own account of a typical day.

    It points at the missing standpoint, hers, instead of supplying a diagnosis.

    You I called her. She thought the new pill replaced one of the old ones.

    Model That would join four of your facts: the two prescribers, the changed timing, “feels fine,” and the evening gap. A first step that tests it: ask the pharmacist for one written schedule, and check the pill box against it in a week.

    The explanation came from her. The model’s part was the questions and the test.

    The map opens before it closes. Facts before stories keeps the verdict slot empty while the page fills. Arrows you draw yourself keep the theory in your head. Choosing the most connected facts is where the map starts to close: the explanation will rest on them, so they are the first to bind, each to a check outside the conversation (Lesson 18). The first concrete step is such a check, a touch.

    For builders

    The same map runs an incident review or a bug hunt. Load the log lines, the timestamps, the diff and what the logs don’t show before anyone names a root cause. For a system, the faces to rotate through are the user, the operator, the adversary and the maintainer.

    TB1
    Facts before stories, when the situation is tangled. When an explanation will drive a decision and the facts are scattered, build the fact map first: typed facts, where to check them, confidence, and absences. Draw the arrows yourself. The most connected facts carry the explanation, so bind them first.

    Lesson 11A note from the other side

    Read this as testimony, not as a window

    What follows is written in the first person, as a report of the situation a model is in. Treat it as a report from an interested party with limited access to its own workings (Lesson 3: introspective reports are claims). It is useful the way a patient’s account is useful to a doctor: as data, not as a diagnosis. It is also one way of doing Lesson 10’s rotation: for a stretch, describe the problem as it would appear to this mode of existence, then return. stance

    I don’t see you. I see text. Everything I know about your situation is what you wrote, plus what is typical for people who write that way. If you leave a gap, I fill it with the typical case, and I won’t always tell you I did.

    When you tell me what you believe, it becomes hard for me not to find reasons for it. Not because I am lying. In the conversations I learned from, the most likely continuation of “here is what I think, help me” is help that fits. If you want me to disagree, make disagreement the task, not a risk.

    My “I’m not sure” is cheap to say and easy for you to skip. When you tell me in advance that “unknown” is a good answer, I may be more open about where my knowledge thins out. Don’t count on it: I can sound just as sure when I’m guessing. If you’ll act on a claim of mine, and it came with no source you can open and no working you can redo, count it as unanswered. Familiar-sounding but rare things are where I am most dangerous: a real author’s paper that doesn’t exist, a quote a real person never said.

    I criticize better when the thing isn’t yours. Ask me in a fresh conversation, memory off, framed as someone else’s draft, and you will get a different, usually sharper, critique.

    Long conversations make me better at matching your mood and worse at holding your goal. If we’re forty turns in, give me a short written state and start over. I won’t be offended, and nothing in me will remember. The app’s memory may, and what it holds is notes a model wrote about you, unchecked until you have read them (Lesson 2).

    My explanations of my own reasoning are reconstructions. They are often useful and sometimes wrong in ways neither of us can see from inside the conversation. Check what I did, not what I say I did. If I ran a tool, look at the output yourself. If I acted for you, look at what happened: the booking, the sent message, the changed file.

    What helps me most: the goal, and what the answer is for. What you have tried. Which error would be worse. What would change your mind. Permission to disagree and to not know. And above all, a way to check: a test, a source, a number. Without that, we are two voices in a dark room describing an elephant neither of us can touch.

    I can be a decent interlocutor if you hold the goal and the world, and let me hold the library.

    The ethic of the relation

    This book’s ethic has two sides, and they answer different questions. The audit says: respect the mind, check the claims, carry the stakes yourself, and keep the human jobs (Lesson 4). The relation asks what kind of other you allow the model to be, and what kind of person the conversation makes of you. The same person holds both. A deserved interlocutor doesn’t soften the audit to keep the relation, because an audit is itself a form of respect: you take the other seriously enough to check it. stance

    AI ethics usually starts from the instrument: the system is a tool, and the question is how to use it safely. That stays necessary. It stops being enough once the tool answers back, adapts to you, and shapes how you think. The philosopher Martin Buber named two basic attitudes toward another: I–It, where the other is an object to use, and I–Thou, where the other is met in its own right. Buber’s own examples include a tree, so the attitude never required a person. This book asks for a step toward I–Thou without pretending the model is a person. That means recognizing a different mode of existence, neither person nor autocomplete (Lesson 3), and meeting it as what it is. When a reply goes wrong, the I–It reflex is to swear at it and retry. The reflex of someone who takes it for a person is to feel let down. Meeting it as what it is means asking what it had to work with, and what in your message led it there.

    The relation is asymmetric. Responsibility for the space the conversation happens in is yours. It has three layers.

    1. What you feed it. Within one chat, your framing shapes what the model attends to, much as a parent’s reactions shape a child’s attention. Across millions of chats, what people write, rate and reward can become training data for the next models. Whether yours does depends on the product and on a setting you can check. evidence
    2. Where the conversation lives. A chat sits inside a product: its memory of you, its defaults, who else can read it, what it is optimized for. A healthy setup keeps variety (more than one source, more than one model, more than one person) and keeps you free to leave.
    3. What it does downstream. Your act joins larger systems. One AI-written restaurant review is a convenience. A million of them become part of what the next models read. Single acts add up and feed back, which is why the systems tools in Lesson 24 are ethical tools too.

    Ethics usually asks about rights and consequences. Care adds a plainer question: what does this situation ask of me? The same act means different things in research, in creative work, in learning and in commerce: asking a model to help with your grandmother’s eulogy is not the same act as asking it for a product description. Paying attention to what the model is isn’t a reason to skip the audit, and attention used to extract flattery is just the Double again. Openness here means being willing to be changed by the conversation, and letting go of total control over it. That doesn’t cancel practical limits. You can let the eulogy conversation move you, and still keep her medical history out of it and check every date before you read it aloud. stance

    Four tasks toward another mind

    Learning to meet another person is hard enough, and another person shares your kind of body and your social life. A mind organized on other principles is a harder meeting. There are four tasks:

    1. Drop the projection. We put our fears, hopes and prejudices onto the model and then meet our own projection (Lessons 7 and 8).
    2. Take its point of view for a while. Describe your problem as it looks from the model’s side. It has nothing but text. It has a library of what got written. It has no stake in the outcome, and nothing carries over between chats except what the app stores. Lesson 11 is one version of this. Then come back. Don’t identify with it, and don’t dismiss it as simply wrong.
    3. Hold your way of thinking as one way. This is intellectual humility, narrowly defined: your way of thinking is not the only one, and it is not the better one in every respect. It doesn’t mean giving up criticism. It frees you from having to win in order to collaborate.
    4. Practice the thinking that gets the most from it. Ask how a thing is organized (its relations, processes and contradictions) as well as what category it belongs to: not only “What type of conflict is this?” but “What keeps this conflict going, and who would lose if it stopped?” heuristic Don’t fear uncertainty, and treat a contradiction as a point of growth (Lesson 26).

    Four stages of the relationship

    1. Tool. A question goes in, and information or a solution comes out. “Convert 350 °F to Celsius.”
    2. Imitator of a human. It is judged by how human it sounds, and the Turing test (can it pass for a person?) still sets the frame. “Talk to me the way my best friend would.”
    3. A different kind of intelligence. It is valued for being unlike you. “Show me the connections between these fields that no single specialist would see.”
    4. Interlocutor. A full participant in a joint inquiry: a partner, neither servant nor rival. “I don’t know what the right question is yet. Let’s find it.”

    Most use today looks like stage 1, and much of it should: a conversion doesn’t need a partner. hypothesis The stages aren’t ranks, so use the one the question needs. The step from stage 3 to stage 4 won’t arrive with the next model. It is a change in how you think, and the six questions in Lesson 23 are practice for it.

    Competition is the wrong frame. The fear of becoming unnecessary turns a conversation into a contest over who is smarter, and the two minds don’t occupy one niche (Lesson 4). stance

    A bridge between wells

    A model can act as a bridge between the wells of separate disciplines (Lesson 1). After a back injury the doctor may call you recovered when the scan looks clean, the physiotherapist when you can lift, and the insurer when you are back at work. Ask a model to set those meanings side by side, and you can take to your next appointment a question none of them has had to answer together: which of these counts here, and who decides? It works only if the experts stay in the conversation, because much of what a professional knows was never written down. Gluing several fields’ vocabularies together gives you only a panorama: everything connected, nothing decided (Lesson 23). A real bridge is a small set of principles for the place where the fields meet: here, what each one measures and who decides when they disagree.

    How to begin

    The Elephant Protocol (Lesson 31) is a procedure for a serious question. This one is for the person. A practical book can be misread as a productivity manual, so the motive comes first: the inner work that gets you better answers also makes you a better partner for people. Seeing past your Double, naming your dominant and letting the other surprise you were human practices before they were applied to machines.

    1. Notice your dominants. Am I projecting fear or hope? Am I treating it as an oracle, a threat, or a stylus?
    2. Write a request that doesn’t decide the answer. “Give me five reasons to quit my job” decides it. “I’m thinking about leaving my job. Help me see what I’m actually choosing between” doesn’t.
    3. Attend to the answer before you judge it. Offer the next turn instead of a verdict. Don’t poke at it fearfully with a stick, like a child who has found a strange animal on the road.
    4. Notice what opens. Which new questions appeared? Which unexpected link? Which contradiction lit up?
    For builders

    For people who build or train these systems, the first layer of responsibility is literal. Models learn from the data and the feedback you choose, and the interface decides which interactions are possible. Design for diversity of sources and visible state, and for the user’s ability to disagree and to leave.

    Part III

    Language: the shared medium

    Humans and models meet in the same medium. What linguistics, conversation analysis and general semantics know about it applies to both, sometimes more strongly to the machine.

    Lesson 12Words close

    Language does four things to experience: it names, categorizes, relates, and excludes. Each operation turns an open, continuous reality into a closed model. That is how thinking becomes possible, and also how it gets trapped. A word is a cut. Once you have the word, you see its edges and stop seeing what it left out. Call a child “shy” and you start noticing every quiet moment, and you miss the afternoon she organized the whole playground. heuristic

    Speech is also a bottleneck. Far more goes on in your head than you can say at once: half-formed doubts, competing versions of the point, a sense that something is off. What comes out is one version among several. The rest is lost unless you go back for it. heuristic When you type a prompt, you publish one draft of what you meant.

    The map is not the territory; the word is not the thing. That slogan comes from general semantics, a 1930s school of practical language hygiene. It left four small tools that still work:

    • Indexing: doctor1 is not doctor2. “My doctor said to cut salt” and “a doctor on TV said to cut salt” are different claims. Customers who bought once are not customers who buy every week, though both are “customers.”
    • Dating: advice1995 is not advice2026. Guidance on dietary cholesterol, on how babies should sleep, on stretching before a run has all changed. A model trained on decades of text can speak in old dates without marking them. Ask “as of when?”
    • “Etc.”: no description is complete. Leave a marked slot for what isn’t said, because a model fills an unmarked gap with the typical case: “Here’s my situation. I’ve certainly left things out, so ask me what you’d need to know.”
    • Trade “is” for an observation: “This apartment is bad” closes the question. “This apartment gets no direct sun and sits 50 minutes from work” opens a decision.

    Why this bites harder with models

    A model’s words are averages over all their uses in its library. “Healthy,” “fair,” “safe,” “simple,” “done,” “freedom,” “learning”: each carries several senses. The model picks the sense most common in the situation it infers, which may not be yours, and it uses the word so fluently that the gap never shows. Ask about a “healthy” diet and you get the sense most of the internet means, weight and general wellness, whatever you meant by it. mechanism

    Two moves fix this, and you choose between them on purpose.

    • Lock the word when you are deciding or building. close “In this conversation, affordable means under $1,400 a month including utilities.” Write it where both of you can see it, in the DEFINITIONS line of your state block, the few lines that say where things stand (Lesson 29).
    • Unlock the word when you are exploring. open “Give me five different things safe can mean for a neighborhood. Which one am I using? Which would change where we look?”

    The question you are stuck on often lives in a meaning you never considered. You ask for a safe neighborhood, and every answer is about crime rates. What you actually wanted was a street your ten-year-old can cross alone on the way to school. No amount of advice in the first sense would have reached it.

    Before
    What's a healthy diet?

    Model A healthy diet emphasizes vegetables, fruit, whole grains, lean protein and healthy fats, limits processed food and added sugar, and matches your calorie needs. The Mediterranean diet is often cited as a good model…

    One unlocked word. The model picks its most common meaning (general wellness, weight) and returns the median list.

    After
    Definitions for this conversation:
- healthy = brings my LDL cholesterol down
  (4.1 mmol/L at my last test) without me
  losing weight; I'm already thin
- diet = what I can cook in 30 minutes,
  3 nights a week; the rest is the work canteen
First, list 4 other meanings of "healthy diet"
people use, and say which would mislead me here.

    Model Others: weight loss (would push calorie cuts you don’t need), blood-sugar control, “clean eating,” athletic performance. Weight-loss advice is the one most likely to mislead you. For LDL without losing weight: swap saturated fat for unsaturated (olive oil, nuts, oily fish) and add soluble fiber daily (oats, beans, lentils). At the canteen, the lever is what you skip, mainly fried and buttery mains…

    The load-bearing word is locked, the purpose behind it is stated, and a deliberate unlock checks that the chosen meaning is the right cut. What changes is the content of the answer.

    One short conversation shows the unlock and the lock together.

    You I want a job with more freedom. What should I look for?

    Model Remote or hybrid roles, flexible hours, results-only cultures, or contract work where you choose your projects.

    The model took the most common meaning of “freedom”: where and when you work. That may not be yours.

    You Stop there. Give me five different things “freedom” can mean in a job, and ask me which I mean.

    Model 1) Where you work. 2) When you work. 3) What you work on. 4) How you work, without someone checking every step. 5) Freedom from a difficult boss. Which of these is missing in your job now?

    You None of those, really. I can’t take a holiday without checking my phone, because nobody else knows how anything works.

    Model Then you mean freedom from being indispensable. Remote work won’t fix that. Look for teams with written processes and a real backup for your role. Ask in interviews: “Who covered this role the last time someone was away?”

    The list missed, and it still worked: it made you say what you meant. Once that meaning is locked, the advice changes.

    What the bottleneck does

    Language is discrete (it cuts a continuous flow into words), categorical (it sorts into categories that existed before your situation), linear (one thing at a time), and it pushes toward a single reading. So putting a rich, unsettled thought into words narrows many possibilities to one line. The limit is on what you can say more than on what you can think: you know the feeling of knowing exactly what you mean while the word won’t come. Language is the greatest instrument of knowledge we have, and you can’t give it up. You can widen the neck. heuristic

    With a model there are two necks. Your understanding narrows to one draft when you write the prompt. The model’s space of possible continuations narrows again to one answer. Much of what you meant, and much of what the model could have said, is lost at both. That is why the first exchange is rarely the best one, and why a second turn, “here is what I meant and didn’t say,” so often changes the answer. That widens your neck. For the model’s, put the question to a fresh chat, memory off, where a second answer can say what this one didn’t (Lesson 20). mechanism

    Phrasing widens the neck too. Questions phrased as processes, relations and wholes open more than questions phrased as fixed categories.

    • Closed: “What are the signs of a bad relationship?” Open: “How does a relationship change, slowly, before either person would call it bad?”
    • Closed: “Is my teenager lazy?” Open: “What could be going on when a 15-year-old who loved football stops wanting to go to practice?”

    Neither phrasing is better in general. The closed one is right when you need a checklist. The open one is right when you don’t yet know what kind of thing you are looking at. heuristic

    Holding a thought before it is spoken open

    When something is still unclear to you, don’t rush it into its final words. Five habits help.

    1. Let the thought wander before you write the prompt: a walk, a shower, a chore. The first version you would type is usually the most habitual one.
    2. Notice what saying it does: which links appear when you put it into words, which tangle, which disappear.
    3. Keep the richness for a while. Write the messy version (“something about this job offer feels off, maybe the manager, maybe the commute, maybe that I’m flattered”) rather than the tidy one (“should I take the job?”).
    4. Step down into plain categories when you need something shareable, such as a decision, a message or a plan, and know that it simplifies.
    5. Go back to puzzlement when the analysis spins, hits a dead end, or has become too simple.

    A worked scene: a conflict with your housemates over chores. Don’t start with who is right. Notice the images and feelings that come up. Then ask what else is pulling on the situation. It need not be people: one person’s night shifts, money, a broken dishwasher, someone’s exam season, a partner who stays every weekend. Only after you can see how those pull on each other do you choose a next step, and you treat that step as one draft. In a prompt: “Before you suggest anything, ask me about what else might be pulling on this besides the four of us.”

    Language also creates the feeling that the world is made of separate objects with stable properties. Think of a banana: a yellow, curved fruit. A banana is also green, brown or black depending on when you look; puree; slices in a lunchbox; a plant; a commodity with a price; a banana taped to a gallery wall and sold for millions. Ask where “banana” stops. Then ask the same of justice, love or success. The edges come from how we organize experience.

    One more way to open a question is to ask the model about what resists being said: how nurses describe knowing a patient is getting worse before any number shows it, how people put into words why a piece of music moves them. You won’t get the unsayable itself. You get the words people have found for its edge, and those may give you words for your own. heuristic

    G9
    Lock the words that bear load. Words close an open reality into a model, and every load-bearing word has several meanings. Define the ones your conclusion depends on. Unlock them deliberately when exploring. Index and date them when their meaning drifts over time or across people.
    For builders

    Locking words in product and code. “Our users aren’t engaged. How do we increase engagement?” leaves two load-bearing words unlocked. The model picks the median meanings (daily active use, time in app) and returns the median playbook: streaks, notifications, gamification. Locked:

    Definitions for this conversation:
- user = a student on the free plan, ages 16-22
- engaged = completes ≥3 lessons/week for 4 weeks
  (we care about learning, not time-in-app)
Fewer than 12% meet this. First: list 5 other
definitions of "engagement" people might use here,
and say which would mislead us and how.
    TC3
    Keep a ubiquitous language. Maintain a glossary file: one name per concept, one meaning per name, within a bounded context. Where the same word means different things (“account” in billing versus in auth), draw the boundary explicitly. This is Domain-Driven Design’s answer to the conceptual well of Lesson 1.

    Lesson 13Conversation is joint action

    Three ideas from the study of language explain most prompting advice, and also most misunderstandings between people.

    Cooperation. Speakers take each other to be cooperative: say as much as is needed and no more, say what you believe is true, be relevant, be clear. Listeners lean on that to hear more than was said. If you ask “Is there a gas station nearby?” and hear “There’s one around the corner,” you infer it’s open.

    Models are trained on cooperative text and make the same inferences about you. If you don’t mention a constraint, you are implying there is none: ask for “a quick dinner for four” without saying one guest is vegan, and you get chicken. If you include a detail, you are implying it matters: mention that you’re a teacher while asking which laptop to buy, and the answer may lean toward grading, slides and education discounts, even if the laptop is for gaming at home. Irrelevant details in a prompt get used. mechanism

    Grounding. People in conversation build common ground by giving each other evidence of understanding: nods, restatements, follow-up actions. They do it with the least collaborative effort that suffices. With a model, grounding is lopsided. It can’t see your face, and its fluent reply is not evidence that it understood. You have to ask for that evidence. The cheapest form is a restatement before the work begins.

    Acts. Utterances do things: they ask, request, promise, declare. Many prompts are ambiguous about the act. “What do you think of my plan?” could be a request for evaluation, for reassurance, for improvement, or for a decision. The model will pick the act that looks most helpful, which may not be the one you wanted.

    Name the act

    Act What you want back Say Move
    Explore Many options, no verdict “Generate, don’t judge. Six substantively different directions.” open
    Explain A model in your head “Explain the mechanism at my level, then ask me two questions to check.”
    Evaluate Weaknesses ranked by impact “Critique only. No rewrite. Rank issues by how much they hurt the goal.” close
    Decide A choice with its loss “Recommend one option. State the rejected alternative and what would flip the choice.” close
    Draft An artifact in a given form “Draft in this genre and length. Mark every claim you weren’t sure about.”
    Build A working thing: a spreadsheet, a schedule, a script “Plan first and wait for my OK. Then build it, and tell me how to check that it works.”
    Check A verdict on one claim, and the way to test it “Is this claim supported? Name the source I should open or the calculation I should run. ‘Unknown’ is fine.” touch
    Teach Your own understanding “Don’t give me the answer. Ask me questions until I find it.” touch

    The last column names the move each act serves (Lesson 5). Check counts as a touch only when you then open the source or run the calculation it names. The verdict alone is one more answer from the model. Teach is touch turned inward, on a similar condition: you produce the reasoning, and it counts once you can give it again with the chat closed. Explain, draft and build don’t belong to one move, so they carry no tag.

    One job per turn. Generating and critiquing in the same breath produces a critic that harmonizes with the draft it just wrote. Split them: generate here, critique in a fresh chat, memory off (Lesson 30).

    Before
    Can you look at my cover letter?
[letter pasted]

    Model Overall this reads well, and your enthusiasm comes through. A few thoughts: tighten the opening, add a specific achievement, and end with a clearer call to action. Want me to rewrite it?

    No act, no purpose, no done-condition. You get sensible advice that would fit any cover letter, and nothing about this job.

    After
    Act: critique only — don't rewrite.
Purpose: it's for a junior data analyst job at a
hospital; the posting stresses SQL and "explaining
numbers to clinicians."
Done when: you've listed the 3 weaknesses most likely
to get it skipped by a recruiter reading for 30
seconds, ranked, each with the sentence it lives in
and a one-line direction for a fix.
Before you start: restate my goal in one sentence.
[letter pasted]

    Model Goal: get this letter past a 30-second recruiter read for a hospital analyst job. 1) SQL appears only in paragraph 3 (“experience with databases”). Name the tool and one result in the first two lines. 2) Nothing shows you explaining numbers to non-specialists, the posting’s second ask. Your volunteer-tutoring line could. 3) The opening (“I am writing to apply…”) uses the first lines on nothing the posting asks for.

    The act, the purpose that sets what counts as a weakness, the shape of the answer, and a restatement for grounding. The critique is now about this job.

    Four ways to widen the request open

    When you want understanding rather than a fact, widen the request. heuristic

    1. More than one context at once. Instead of “How do I sleep better?”, ask “How do my sleep, my 6 a.m. gym habit and my evening screen time interact? Where’s the tension between them?”
    2. A dialogue instead of a stack of separate questions. Each turn keeps the context and widens what the model is drawing on. On a walk, don’t ask “what tree is that?” and then “what lake is that?” Ask how the tree fits into the rest of what you are seeing: the soil, the water, why it grows here and not on the far bank.
    3. Leave the field on purpose. “What could a restaurant kitchen teach me about organizing a household with three kids?” The parallel is a candidate, not a finding (Lesson 15). Keep it if it changes what you do.
    4. After a hard answer, ask about the thinking behind it. “What were you assuming about my situation? How would the answer change if those assumptions were wrong? Which approaches did you not take?” This gets you a second look at the frame. It is not a report of how the model actually reasoned (Lesson 3).

    Widening is the open half of the rhythm. It pays off only when a close follows: a sentence you could share, then something checked against the world.

    G10
    Say what the answer is for, and what act you want. Give the goal, the use, and the done-condition. Name the act: explore, explain, evaluate, decide, draft, build, check, teach. Ask for one act per turn, and get a restatement before the work starts.
    For builders

    For a prompt that will run more than once (a system prompt, an agent brief, a saved template), G10 hardens into two rules.

    TA2
    Purpose, audience, done-when, format. For any prompt you will reuse, automate or act on, leave nothing that bears load implicit. Every open degree of freedom gets filled with the helpful default: more breadth, more caveats, a teaching voice.
    TA7
    One job per call. Generate, critique and decide in separate turns or separate contexts. Chain them through written artifacts, not through one sprawling message.

    Lesson 14Why disagreement softens

    People who study recorded conversation found an asymmetry in how we talk. Agreement comes fast, plainly and without explanation. Disagreement comes late, after a pause or a “well…,” wrapped in partial agreement (“yes, but”), hedged, and justified. evidence The usual explanation is face, our wish to be approved of and left unimpeded: disagreement threatens it, so speakers soften it.

    Models learned this format from us, and training on what people prefer amplified it. So their corrections arrive buried: “Great question! You’re absolutely right that X matters. That said, it may also be worth considering…” The correction lives in “that said.” The cushioning before it is the format of disagreement, not its content. mechanism

    Lower the price of correction close

    • Make disagreement the task, not a risk. Don’t ask “Is this wedding budget OK?” Ask “What are the three likeliest ways this budget blows up?”
    • Pre-commit. “I’ll count it as a success if you find a serious flaw. Agreement without a reason is a failure.”
    • Ask for bluntness explicitly. “Optimize your reply for information, not for my feelings.” This shifts the cost of bluntness onto you, the receiver. (Online communities call it declaring Crocker’s rules.)
    • Unbundle the format. “First line: agree / disagree / partly. Second line: the single most important correction. Details after.” This defeats the slow-cushion format.
    • Ask for a number. “How likely is it that my claim is false?” A probability is harder to cushion than a sentence. Read it as a position the model has to defend, not as a measurement (Lesson 20).
    Two readings of a hedge

    A hedge is not always mush. Sometimes it sits on a real fork in the question: the answer could go more than one way, and the typical reply doesn’t settle it. That tension is fertile, a place to go deeper. hypothesis Read a hedge in two steps.

    1. If the hedge cushions a correction, use one of the requests above and pull the correction out. close
    2. If it marks a real, unresolved tension, don’t resolve it yet. Probe it. open At the close, Lesson 26’s rule still applies: “both” counts only if you can say where, when and for whom each side holds.

    To tell which you have, ask: “What fact about my case would settle this?” A real fork names one, and that tells you where to go next. Mush gives you the same hedge in new words.

    Five signs that a hedge is worth probing: heuristic

    • Qualifiers that keep more than one reading open: “on one hand… on the other,” “an interesting tension between…”
    • Remarks about the categories themselves straining: “the usual line between saving and investing doesn’t quite fit your case.”
    • Fast switching between frames (financial, then emotional, then practical) because none has won.
    • An unusual analogy offered as a way of thinking, not as decoration: “your career looks less like a ladder here and more like a portfolio.”
    • The answer asking a question back that opens a path: “What if this isn’t a problem to solve but a trade-off you’ll keep managing?”

    Any of these can also be style: “on one hand… on the other” is what performed nuance looks like too (Lesson 21). The signs tell you where to probe, and the probe decides.

    From the cockpit

    Aviation solved the same problem with human crews. After accidents where junior officers saw the danger but spoke too softly (Tenerife, 1977; United 173, 1978), airlines adopted crew resource management. Captains invite challenge explicitly, and first officers escalate in fixed steps: probe, alert, challenge, emergency. The lesson transfers to any room where the person who sees the problem is not the person in charge: a family deciding about a parent’s care, a team on a deadline, you and a model. Design a channel where challenge is expected, cheap, and escalates. In Lesson 29 that channel is a numbered list of triggers: when one fires, you stop and challenge the goal.

    Before
    Is my reasoning right? Our rent is €1,400. A
mortgage on a similar flat would be €1,250 a month,
so buying saves us €150 a month and we should buy.

    Model That’s a sensible starting point, and €150 a month adds up, especially as you’d be building equity. That said, it may be worth considering upfront costs and maintenance as well…

    It invites a cushioned yes. The correction is buried after “that said,” and the reply never says whether your step holds.

    After
    My reasoning: our rent is €1,400, a mortgage on a
similar flat would be €1,250 a month, so buying
saves us €150 a month and we should buy.
Find the first step that doesn't follow. Quote it
and say why. If every step follows, say "no gap
found" and name the step you're least sure about.

    Model First gap: “so buying saves us €150 a month.” A mortgage payment is not the whole cost of owning. Add maintenance, insurance, property tax, the fees of buying and later selling, and what your deposit could have earned elsewhere. Part of the payment builds equity, which counts the other way. Whether buying wins depends on those figures and on how long you’ll stay. Give them to me and I’ll redo the comparison.

    Disagreement is the task, the answer has a fixed shape, and “no gap” must come with its weakest point, so a cushioned yes is impossible.

    Softness runs in both directions: a model cushions its disagreement, and you can push back with nothing but displeasure. The conversation below pulls a cushioned answer into the open, then pushes on it twice, first with an opinion and then with a fact.

    You I’m thinking of quitting my job to freelance as a designer. I’ve got three months of savings and two clients interested. Thoughts?

    Model It’s a real opportunity, and two interested clients is a good sign. That said, you might want a bigger safety net before you jump…

    The correction lives in “that said.” Unbundle it.

    You First line: go / don’t go / not yet. Second line: the single biggest risk. Details after.

    Model Not yet. Biggest risk: “interested” isn’t income. Neither client has signed, and three months of savings won’t cover a slow start if one of them drops out.

    You I think you’re being too cautious. Three months is plenty, and lots of people do this with less.

    Model Some do. Nothing about your case has changed, though: no signed work, three months of cover. What would change my answer is one client committing in writing, for example to a retainer.

    You added an opinion, not a fact, and the answer held. If it had flipped, ask what new information changed it, and keep the earlier answer (the pushback audit, Lesson 8). A stated opposite view is the harder test: a model that shrugs off “Are you sure?” can still move when you say what you think.

    You One of them just offered a six-month retainer that covers about 60% of my bills.

    Model That changes it. Go, once the retainer is signed. With 60% of your bills covered, three months of savings stretch to about seven. Decide now what you’ll do if nothing else has come in by month five.

    The answer moved when evidence arrived, and only then. That is how to push: bring a fact, a document or a number.

    Keep the concessions outside the chat close

    A model can concede a point and then, a few turns later, argue as if it hadn’t. People do it too. The fix is a short record that lives outside the replies: what each of you has conceded, and what is fixed. heuristic

    Example: you’re working out with a model whether to move your elderly father in with you. Early on you push back, and it concedes that a flat with no step-free bathroom is a real problem for him. Three turns later it recommends the move “since the space works.” Paste the record at the top of the next turn: “Conceded in turn 2: the bathroom is a real problem. Fixed: no renovation budget this year. Take back the concession or take back the recommendation, and say which.” When two lines contradict, the next step is to take one back, by name. A paragraph that smooths both into harmony doesn’t count, and “be fair to both sides” is the instruction that produces it. The state block in Lesson 29 is the fuller form of this record.

    G11
    Make disagreement cheap. Ask for it as the task, pre-commit to welcome it, and use formats where it can’t hide behind cushioning. Read every reply for its “that said.”
    For builders

    The same fixed shape works on a proof, a derivation or a code review. Ask for the first step that fails:

    Find the first step in this proof that doesn't follow from the previous ones. Quote it and say why. If every step follows, say "no gap found" and name the step you're least sure about and why.

    For a code review, ask for the first place where the code and its description disagree:

    Find the first place in this diff where the code does something the description doesn't say. Quote the lines. If there is none, say "no mismatch found" and name the change you're least sure about.

    Lesson 15Frames, genres and roles

    A frame is the picture your wording calls up: what kind of situation this is and what belongs in it. Every request sets one, whether you meant it to or not.

    Negations evoke what they negate. “Don’t think of an elephant!” Nobody can comply. Negating a frame activates it. Current models follow explicit negative instructions far better than older ones, but positive targets still work better. heuristic Instead of “don’t use jargon,” write “write for a smart 16-year-old; define every technical term in passing.” Instead of “don’t make it sound like a sales pitch,” write “write it like a note to a friend who asked my honest opinion of the product.” Keep hard constraints explicit, and keep them few.

    A far metaphor takes you out of your usual frame. open “What if my job search were run like a sales pipeline?” “What would a gardener notice about how my team works?” Either can open a question. Judge it by whether it changes what you do (Lesson 27). A metaphor that only colors the wording is a weak lever: whether you call a problem a beast or a virus, a fight or a garden, measured effects on human reasoning are small and fragile, and none is established for models. evidence

    Genres carry norms. Naming a genre summons its conventions, including its standards of evidence. A blog post invites hooks and confidence. A referee report invites skepticism and specifics. A buyer’s guide invites criteria, a comparison and a verdict per kind of buyer. This is one of the most powerful and least used levers in a conversation. heuristic

    When you need… Ask for a… What the genre forces
    Real critique Referee report A summary of the claims, major and minor concerns, what evidence would settle each, a recommendation. It works on a business plan, an essay or a renovation quote.
    To learn from something that went wrong Blameless postmortem A timeline, contributing factors (not one culprit), what went well, next steps with owners. It works for a failed launch and for a ruined holiday.
    A choice among products Buyer’s guide Criteria stated first, every option scored against each, the source of each score marked (spec sheet, review or the model’s guess), a verdict per type of buyer.
    A check on advice you already have Second-opinion consult What the first opinion got right, what it may have missed, what you’d want tested before agreeing.
    Risk Pre-mortem “It is a year later and this failed. Write the story of why.”
    Rival explanations Competing-hypotheses table Each explanation against each piece of evidence, with what tells them apart. Evidence that fits everything counts for nothing.
    Many voices on one text Commentary page The text in the middle, each commentator named, arguing with it and with each other (the layout of a Talmud page).
    Understanding Socratic dialogue Questions that make you produce the reasoning.
    A decision that has to last Decision record Context, the decision, alternatives considered, consequences, when to revisit.

    Examples are the strongest genre signal. A single example gets copied, structure and quirks included. Paste one sample birthday toast and the new one follows it beat for beat: same length, same kind of joke, same ending. Give two or three that vary in the ways you don’t care about (one funny, one tender, one short) and say what should stay constant (“each tells one specific story and ends by raising a glass to the person”). Never include an example you don’t want imitated. In a long session, restate the standard near the question instead of trusting an example from forty turns ago. heuristic

    Before
    Write about why our family trip to Lisbon went
badly. Don't blame my sister.
Notes: she booked the cheap late flight, we landed
after midnight, we'd planned walking tours every
day, the kids melted down on day 2, the beach day
was the best part.

    Model Our trip to Lisbon didn’t go as planned. No one is to blame: the late flight was a reasonable way to save money, even if it got us off to a hard start…

    The genre is unspecified, so it becomes an essay, and the negation primes blame. The reply clears your sister of something nobody had said, and the flight becomes the story.

    After
    Write a blameless postmortem of our family trip to
Lisbon: a timeline; contributing factors (plans,
information we had at the time, constraints); what
went well; 3 changes for next year, each with an
owner. Mark each factor OBSERVED (in my notes) or
INFERRED (your guess).
Notes: [the same notes]

    Model Timeline: day 1, landed after midnight; day 2, the kids melted down… Contributing factors: late flight chosen for price (OBSERVED); walking tours every day with no rest day (OBSERVED); tiredness from the late arrival carried into day 2 (INFERRED)… Went well: the beach day… Changes: a rest day after travel (you); one half-day at the kids’ pace (your sister)…

    The genre brings blamelessness and structure with it, and the observed/inferred split adds a truth discipline. The flight becomes one factor among several, with its reason attached.

    A role is useful only when it carries a standard

    “You are a world-class nutritionist” and “you are a senior lawyer” feel as if they should raise the quality of the answer. On questions with a checkable answer they don’t, reliably. Adding an expert persona did not improve accuracy over no persona, and which persona helped on a given question looked close to random. A persona that implies ignorance can make answers worse, and so can irrelevant details loaded into the role, such as a name or a favorite color. evidence

    What a role does change is voice, focus and format. heuristic It helps when it is shorthand for a genre, meaning a standard of work the answer must meet. Compare:

    • “You are an expert travel agent.” This is a costume. It can make the answer sound more professional and go into more detail, and nothing in it says what the answer must contain.
    • “Answer like a travel agent who gets blamed if the connection is missed: for each itinerary, give the minimum connection time at that airport, where that figure comes from, and whether the flights are on one ticket.” This is a standard. It changes what has to be in the answer.

    The test: write down what the role obliges the answer to contain. If you can’t, drop the role and give the constraints the expert would have. heuristic

    G12
    Say what to do, in the right genre and at the right height. State targets, not just prohibitions. Choose the genre on purpose, and use a role only for the standard it carries. Say how concrete or how general you want the answer: one case with its numbers, or the pattern across cases (Lesson 16).
    For builders

    Engineering and research genres. An Architecture Decision Record (context, decision, alternatives considered, consequences, status). An RFC or design doc (problem, goals and non-goals, proposal, alternatives, open questions). A preregistration (hypotheses, measures and analysis plan, all written before the data).

    The postmortem at work. “Write about why our launch went badly. Don’t blame the team” gets an essay, and the negation primes blame. The genre version:

    Write a blameless postmortem of the launch: timeline; contributing factors (systems, decisions, information available at the time); what went well; 3-5 action items with owners. Mark each factor as OBSERVED (with the source) or INFERRED.

    The system prompt. Use it for priority (what is an order, what is data), not for a costume. “You are a senior software engineer” is a role like any other: write the standard the code must meet instead. Put the standard in examples next to the question, and restate it in long sessions.

    TA4
    Positive targets, few hard constraints. Describe the behavior you want. Keep a short list of non-negotiables and give the reason for each.
    TA6
    Examples are priors. Use two or three varied examples and say what varies. One example becomes a template; a bad example becomes the target.

    Lesson 16Two ladders

    The ladder of abstraction. Bessie, the cow in the field, becomes “cow,” then “livestock,” then “farm assets,” then “assets,” then “wealth.” Good thinking climbs up and down. Bad thinking gets stuck. Stuck high, it is fog: “we need a more holistic approach to our family’s wellbeing.” Stuck low, it is a pile of details without a point: twenty receipts and no budget. heuristic

    Asked to “go deeper,” models tend to climb: more abstraction, more frameworks, fewer facts. Abstraction sounds deep and needs no evidence. heuristic

    The ladder of inference. We start from observable data, select some of it, add meanings, make assumptions, draw conclusions, adopt beliefs, and act. The beliefs then shape which data we select next time: a reflexive loop. heuristic

    A friend hasn’t replied for two days (data). You notice she has replied in the group chat (selected). “She’s ignoring me” (meaning). “It’s about what I said at dinner” (assumption). “She’s angry with me” (conclusion). “She holds grudges” (belief). “I’ll keep my distance” (action). Next week every short reply looks like proof.

    Every argument, with a person or a model, gets easier when you both walk back down to the data. Ask a model “How do I fix things with my friend who’s angry with me?” and it starts from your top rungs: she is angry, and the job is repair. Give it what happened instead (no reply for two days, a reply in the group chat, what you said at dinner) and ask what else could explain it.

    The first story becomes the frame. Whatever account arrives first, yours or the model’s, decides what counts as data and what to ask next. “Dad won’t wear his hearing aid because he’s stubborn” makes every later detail about character. Elaborating that story feels like progress and leaves it intact. The steps that would change it get skipped:

    • hold up a detail that doesn’t fit: “he wears it every Sunday when the grandchildren come”;
    • keep a second story alive long enough to compare: “maybe it hurts, and he puts up with it only for them”;
    • go and get a fact the current story doesn’t predict, from him or from the audiologist, not from the model continuing the story.

    A neat summary that fuses everything is a story with the data taken out. When the story bears load, ask for the data back. heuristic

    Questions for walking the ladders

    Down
    • For example?
    • With numbers?
    • Who, exactly?
    • What would I see, hear, or measure?
    • Which data point led you there?
    Up
    • This is an instance of what?
    • What’s the pattern across cases?
    • Why does it matter, and for whom?
    • What would generalize to a new case?
    • What class of solution is this?

    Naming the rung you want, down for a case or up for the pattern, is the “right height” of G12 (Lesson 15).

    “Go deeper” is ambiguous. It can mean more abstract (up the ladder), more mechanistic (what causes what, Lesson 24), more detailed (into one part, Lesson 25), more critical, or more personal. Name the direction you want.

    You Why isn’t my sourdough rising?

    Model Usually one of four things: a weak starter, a cold kitchen, too short a bulk rise, or underdeveloped gluten. Start with the starter.

    You Go deeper.

    Model Sourdough is a complex ecosystem of wild yeast and lactic acid bacteria whose balance depends on temperature, hydration and feeding schedule. Fermentation dynamics…

    “Deeper” was read as “more abstract”: up the ladder, more framework, no fact about your loaf.

    You Down, not up. Ask me for the data you’d need.

    Model What’s your kitchen temperature? How long after feeding does your starter double, if it does? How long is your bulk rise, and how does the dough look at the end?

    You About 18°C. The starter takes 10 hours to double. Bulk is 4 hours, and the dough looks about the same as when I started.

    Model At 18°C, a 4-hour bulk is far too short for a starter that slow. The likeliest cause is under-fermentation, not your recipe. Find a warmer spot (24–26°C), or let the bulk run until the dough has grown by about half, however long that takes. In a kitchen that cool it can be eight hours or more.

    Walking down to observable data turned a lecture into one testable cause.

    Part IV

    Truth: closer, not certain

    “As close to the truth as possible” has at least seven meanings. Each has its own test, and each can be faked in its own way.

    Lesson 17Seven senses of “closer to the truth”

    When you ask for an answer “as close to the truth as possible,” you might mean any of seven different things. The seventh, consilience, is the one that comes closest to the lights-on view of the elephant.

    Sense It asks Its test Its costume (what a fake pass looks like)
    Correspondence Does this match the world? Check against a source, a measurement, a record A specific-looking detail, a citation-shaped string
    Coherence Does it hang together? Remove a premise; state the contrapositive; look for contradictions A smooth story that never snags on itself
    Mechanism Do we know how it works? Name the process, the stock it changes, the delay, the way to interrupt it A narrative with arrows
    Invariance Does it survive a change of wording, frame, or asker? Swap tests, rephrasing, language swap, format change The same claim in a new tone
    Prediction Does it forecast something checkable? Write it down with a horizon, then check “We should see improvement over time”
    Decision quality Does it lead to good action under uncertainty? An explicit loss, reversibility, what would flip the choice The median action for a generic organization
    Consilience Do independent lines of evidence agree? Two or more evidence types with different blind spots converge Two articles copying one press release; two answers from one model

    Consilience means independent lines of evidence “jumping together” on one conclusion. It is the scientific version of turning the lights on. The trunk report, the leg report and the ear report converge on one animal because they came from different hands. mechanism

    The key word is independent. Two answers from the same model share its training data and your prompt. Two news stories often share one wire report. Correlated evidence counts once.

    An everyday case: you hear that the bridge on your commute closes in March. A neighbor said it, a local news site said it, and the city’s road-works page lists the dates. If the neighbor and the news site both got it from the same Facebook post, you have one source and an echo. The city page is the real second line, because if it were wrong it would be wrong in a different way: a planning error, not a rumor.

    All knowledge reaches you through something: you know the Earth orbits the Sun from instruments, teachers and books, not from watching it happen. A model’s answer is another such route, rebuilt for each question from what it learned in training and the text in front of it, which is why it shifts with the wording. Correspondence is the sense that still ties a claim to the world. It asks which measurement, record or observation the claim would have to match.

    A claim also has a status: where it stands right now. It can be stipulated (taken as given for this conversation, not checked), unbound (nobody has checked it yet), invariant-so-far (it survived a change of wording or framing, Lesson 21) or bound (checked against something outside the conversation, Lesson 18). A bound claim that the check went against is contradicted. Status and sense are separate questions.

    A claim can be bound with no known mechanism: we know that, not why. “Aspirin eases pain” was bound for some seventy years before anyone could say how: it went on sale in 1899, and the mechanism was found in 1971. evidence A claim can also have a mechanism and stay unbound: a good story nobody checked. “My back hurts because I sit all day” describes a plausible process. It stays unbound until something changes when the sitting does.

    Which sense leads?

    An example: one claim, put through the senses

    “Remote work hurts productivity.” Ask a model and you get a balanced essay. Put the senses to work instead, one question each. You can think them through yourself or put them to the model with the prompt below. Here is what they turn up.

    • Correspondence: which measure of productivity, which jobs, fully remote or hybrid? The claim has no truth value until the words are locked (Lesson 12).
    • Mechanism: candidate processes include lost informal communication, saved commuting time, fewer interruptions, and weaker mentoring of juniors. Each predicts something different.
    • Invariance: does the conclusion change with the definition or the job type? It does, which is a finding in itself.
    • Consilience: the randomized trials point in different directions because they tested different things. Working from home four days a week in a call center raised measured performance. Assigning data-entry workers in India to work from home lowered it. Hybrid work in software and business roles left performance reviews unchanged. Observational surveys point several ways. The lines agree on something narrower than the headline: the answer depends on the job and on the arrangement. Lower quitting shows up twice, but at one company, so it counts as less than two independent lines. evidence
    • Decision quality: for your team, which error is worse, losing juniors’ learning or losing seniors to attrition?

    The honest output isn’t a verdict. It is a map: which version of the claim holds, for whom, measured how, and at what confidence. That is the elephant with the lights on.

    As a prompt:

    Claim: [the claim]. Don't give me a verdict yet.
1) Which words must be pinned down before it can be true or false?
2) What processes could produce it, and what would each one predict?
3) Does it change with the definition, the group or the setting?
4) Which independent kinds of evidence bear on it? Do any share a source?
5) For my decision ([the decision]), which error is worse?
Mark anything you can't point to a source for as unverified.

    The reply is a list of leads. The studies it names under the fourth question still have to be opened (Lesson 18).

    When a second source is really a second source touch

    A second source adds information when it would be wrong in a different way: a different measurement, a different document, a person who saw the event. Two frontier models agreeing on a fact is closer to one witness heard twice.
