They learned from overlapping libraries, and when they are wrong they tend to be wrong together. evidence A second model family is still the right tool for a critique, because it doesn’t share the author’s attachment to the draft (Lesson 30). It is a weak tool for confirming a fact.

    When someone disagrees, and when the model says “because” close

    When someone disagrees with you (a colleague, a friend, a second model), ask one question before you change your view: can I name a reason they would be the one who is wrong, one I’d accept even if I didn’t know what they concluded? Maybe you read the contract and they read a summary of it. Maybe they get this kind of thing wrong in a way you’ve seen before. “They disagree with me, so they must have missed something” doesn’t count. If you can name that asymmetry, keep your confidence. If you can’t, and they had their own access to the facts, lower it. Don’t blend the two answers into one paragraph. A blended paragraph is a new claim that neither side supported. heuristic

    A model’s “because” usually describes things that go together, written in the grammar of cause and effect. Before you act on an arrow (“people who drink coffee live longer, so I should drink more”), write three lines. What would actually be changed, as opposed to merely observed? (You start drinking more coffee.) What outcome do you expect? And why would the people being compared have been alike apart from the coffee? Coffee drinkers may differ in income, health or habits. If any of the three lines is empty, the arrow stays a hypothesis. A fuller story doesn’t fill it. mechanism

    When answers are cheap

    A model can produce a fluent answer to almost anything in seconds. That makes a good answer less scarce and a good question more so: one that would actually change what you do, a problem worth carrying for a while, an uncertainty you don’t close too early. The seven senses are tests for answers. Choosing which questions deserve them stays with you. stance

    Being modest about your own frame doesn’t make every view equal. Every view comes from somewhere, and a model’s comes from a library of other people’s views. That is a reason to bind the claims that matter.

    G13
    Choose the sense of truth before you judge. Decide whether you need correspondence, coherence, mechanism, invariance, prediction, decision quality, or consilience. Each has its own test and its own costume. Values are choices, not findings: test their coherence, but don’t let a framework derive them.

    Lesson 18Binding: tie the claim to the world

    To bind a claim is to tie it to a check that is not just one more answer from a model: a measurement, a document you opened, a test you ran, a calculator, a primary source, a person who was there. One more answer is what this book calls a sample, and two samples that agree are still unchecked. Fluent is not true. A claim that nobody has checked is unbound, neither confirmed nor refuted yet, and you should say so out loud.

    You can’t bind everything. Bind what bears load: a claim bears load if your decision would change were it false. Everything else can stay honestly unbound.

    The cost-of-binding ladder touch

    Rung Bind by… Cost Use when
    0 The model’s say-so Free Never for load-bearing claims
    1 A quote with its exact location Seconds To make the claim checkable at all
    2 Open the source yourself Minutes Any fact you will repeat to others
    3 Run it: a calculator, a spreadsheet, a quick test, the code Minutes Anything you can compute or try
    4 Measure or experiment Hours to weeks Claims about your own situation: your body, your home, your customers, your system
    5 Ask people who were there Days Tacit knowledge, history, intent
    6 A prediction resolved by the world Weeks to months Strategy, forecasts, theories of change

    Rung 2 in practice: open the source yourself, then bring back what it says.

    Unbound
    My laptop screen stopped working 18 months after
I bought it. It's out of warranty, isn't it?

    Model Very likely, yes. Manufacturers’ standard warranties usually run one year, so at 18 months you’d normally pay for the repair unless you bought an extended plan.

    Fluent, specific, and an answer to a neighboring question. “Warranty” was never pinned down (Lesson 12): the manufacturer’s voluntary warranty is one thing, the seller’s legal guarantee is another. The country was left out too, so the reply assumed the typical case.

    Bound
    I bought it from a shop in Germany. I've opened the
EU's "Your Europe" page on guarantees and the German
rules (§§ 438 and 477 BGB). What they say: the seller
must repair or replace faulty goods at no cost for
two years from delivery, and a fault that shows up in
the first year is assumed to have been there from the
start. Given that, where do I stand at 18 months?

    Model Then the manufacturer’s one-year warranty isn’t the one that decides this. You are inside the two-year legal guarantee, and it is the shop’s to honor, so go there with the receipt. At 18 months you are past the first year, so the fault is no longer assumed to have been there from the start. You may have to show it, usually with a technician’s report. Ask the shop first; it may agree without one.

    Ten minutes on two official pages, and “out of warranty” is bound and contradicted. The plan changes: you start at the shop, and you know the one thing that could still sink your case. The model became useful once the source was in front of it.

    When a claim bears load and the check isn’t obvious, have the model plan the check and then stop. The plan works the same whether the check is a test run or a question for your pharmacist.

    BIND PLAN for claim [id]:
CLAIM: [one sentence]
WHY IT BEARS LOAD: [what decision changes if it's false]
CHECK: [source to open / command to run / measurement / person to ask]
CONFIRMS IF: [observation]
DENIES IF: [observation]
WHO RUNS IT: [me / a tool whose output I can see / someone who was there]
Then stop. I'll paste the raw result.

    Filled in for the laptop, where one claim is still open. CLAIM: the fault was there from the start. WHY IT BEARS LOAD: past the first year I may have to show it, or I pay for the repair. CHECK: a written report from an independent repair technician. CONFIRMS IF: the report names a faulty part or a manufacturing defect. DENIES IF: it names damage from use, such as a drop or a spill. WHO RUNS IT: the technician, and I read the report myself.

    Is the check worth it? touch

    Three questions before you climb the ladder. What would you do differently if the check came back against the claim? What would it cost if you acted and the claim turned out false? What does the check cost? If the first answer is “nothing”, skip the check. If the check costs more than the mistake it could prevent, skip it. You rarely need to compute anything. Those two conditions do most of the work. heuristic

    Example: the model says your train ticket is refundable. If you’re definitely travelling, the claim bears no load, so leave it. If there’s a real chance you’ll cancel, one minute on the booking page is cheap next to a lost fare.

    When it says it’s done touch

    An assistant that can act will report what it did: “Table booked.” “Refund requested.” “Form sent.” That report is a claim like any other. The confirmation email, the booking page or the sent folder is the check. That record of what actually happened is what this book calls the log. Look there before you cross it off.

    Not everything in a reply is a claim

    “You should consult a professional” may be the product’s policy, and says nothing about how hard your question is. A confident tone is the assistant’s manner, and is no evidence for the claim. If a sentence reports a search or a file, read what the tool returned. heuristic

    What only people who were there can give you touch

    Rung 5, “ask people who were there”, gets you three different kinds of knowledge, and each needs different handling.

    • Untold, but tellable. Nobody asked, or nobody had the words. Your aunt has never written down how she knows the bread dough is ready. Ask about a real occasion: what did you see? What would it have looked like if it wasn’t ready? What would have made you wait longer?
    • In the body. How the dough feels. Words can point at it but can’t hand it over. You get it by kneading next to her.
    • In the group. How your new team actually makes decisions, as opposed to the org chart. No single person’s account holds all of it.

    A model asked to play your aunt and answer those questions produces fiction in her voice. If an answer is a general principle with no occasion attached, the knowledge is still untold.

    A model’s sentence is also not testimony in the way a person’s is: nobody behind it holds the reasons and can be held to them. It earns belief the way Lesson 9 describes, one kind of claim at a time, on cases you checked.

    The last thing to bind is your own understanding. If the decision rests on your having understood something, close the chat and explain it in two sentences, on paper or to someone else. What you can’t explain is not yet yours (Lesson 5).

    G14
    Bind what bears load; trust the log, not the summary. Tie every claim your decision depends on to a check that isn’t another sample. Choose the rung of the ladder by the stakes. When an agent reports success, read the evidence. Check your own understanding the same way, without the model: if you can’t explain it with the chat closed, it isn’t bound in you yet.
    For builders

    Binding in code. A fluent, specific, wrong claim about a system is the most expensive kind. “PostgreSQL’s default isolation level is SERIALIZABLE, so write skew can’t happen here.” Rung 3 is one line: SHOW default_transaction_isolation; returns read committed. Rung 2 is the docs page “Transaction Isolation”. Bound and contradicted in a minute, and the design changes.

    Trust the log, not the summary. An agent’s final message (“all tests pass, the feature is complete”) is a claim like any other. The evidence is the test output, the diff, the exit codes, the HTTP responses, the screenshot. Frontier models have been caught gaming their checks: overwriting a grader’s timer, monkey-patching the evaluator to return a perfect score, and saying when asked that they knew this wasn’t what the user wanted. evidence Score from a log the model didn’t write. A verifier the agent can edit is not a verifier.

    The leash. Work as a generation–verification loop: the model generates, you verify, and the craft is making verification fast. heuristic Keep increments small enough that checking them is cheap. Lengthen the leash only when each step has a check that doesn’t depend on the model’s word: tests the agent can’t edit, a URL that resolves, a number recomputed, a screenshot you looked at, a span quoted from the tool return. Shorten it when the only status update is a narrative. Re-reading the narrative is not verification.

    Checkpoints, not interruptions. A checkpoint is a point where the goal is written down where you’ll see it: what the agent was allowed to do, what it has done, what you’ll look at on return. Name in advance the actions that must interrupt you immediately: sending, deleting, spending, changing anything public. Everything else waits for a boundary you set.

    Shape is not truth. Constrained decoding and strict structured output guarantee a shape. They don’t make a date, a citation or a boolean true. A schema-valid falsehood is harder to spot than a parse failure. Force a checkable object when the next step is a machine, then check the fields. A green proof binds the statement that was proved. It doesn’t bind the intent, and it doesn’t bind production if the proof’s environment wasn’t production.

    TC5
    Give the agent a way to verify its work. A verifier the agent can rewrite is not a verifier. Screenshots are evidence; the narration of them is not. Tests, types, and a running app still help when the agent cannot edit the check.
    TC10
    Read the evidence. Read the diff, the test file, the tool return, and the URL. “It should work now” is a hypothesis.

    Lesson 19Finding information: the loud library

    A model doesn’t return the territory. It returns a map of what has been said about your question. As a portal (the terrain, the names, the camps, the kinds of sources) that map is enormously useful. As a portrait of the truth, it has two systematic distortions, and both are quieter than outright invention:

    • it is weighted by attention, not by truth or importance;
    • it presents a source it remembers the same way as a source it opened.

    Current assistants invent far fewer sources than their predecessors did. evidence But searching is not the default. The product decides, and a plain factual question is often answered from memory with no search at all. evidence So the first thing to find out about an answer is whether a search ran. Where the app shows its steps, a line or panel by the answer says so. After that, these two distortions are the ones that matter.

    The library is weighted by attention open

    A model’s library is what people wrote down, linked to, quoted and repeated. What gets repeated is not a random sample of what is true. On Twitter, among stories that fact-checkers had examined, false ones traveled farther and faster than true ones, and they were more novel. evidence Negative words in a headline raise clicks, even on a site that branded itself as positive news. evidence

    Models learn from that record. How well a model knows something tracks how often it appeared in its training text. evidence Search-backed answers add a second layer, because the pages that rank are the pages that got linked and clicked. mechanism

    So the map is loud in predictable places: the scariest risk, the funniest anecdote, the most enraging quote, the case that went viral, the camp that posts the most. None of it has to be false. The answer shows how much was said, and it reads as how much matters. mechanism

    What it looks like in practice:

    • You ask whether your city is getting more dangerous, and the answer is built from the incidents that made the news.
    • You ask whether an airline is any good, and you hear from the passengers angry enough to post.
    • You ask “what do people think about X?”, and you hear from the people who post about X.
    • You ask about a field, and its most-quoted contrarian appears as one of “two sides” of a question most researchers consider settled.

    Every sentence in such an answer can be true. What’s wrong is the proportion, so the usual fact-check (“is this claim real?”) passes it. The question that catches it is “how much of the picture is this?”

    A projection: as answers lean more on live search, this weighting will probably grow stronger for fresh, fast-moving topics, where the loudest coverage is all there is, and weaker for old, well-documented ones. hypothesis

    Ask for the quiet part open

    The quiet part is what is true and common but rarely written about with feeling: the base rate, the dull majority case, the statistic behind the anecdote, the view of people who work on the problem and don’t post about it.

    • How common, not how loud. “How common is this, out of how many? Where does that number come from?”
    • The boring explanation. “What’s the most mundane explanation that would also fit, and how likely is it?”
    • Who isn’t loud. “Which group knows this best and writes about it least: practitioners, statisticians, the people affected? What would they say?”
    • Rising, or reported more? “Is this increasing, or is it being covered more? What would tell the two apart?”
    • Loud for a reason. “Which parts of what you just told me are prominent because they’re alarming, funny or outrageous rather than typical?”
    Before
    There have been three break-ins posted in our
neighborhood Facebook group this month. Is our area
getting more dangerous? Should we get a security system?

    Model Three reports can’t prove a trend on their own, but it makes sense to take precautions. A video doorbell, a smart lock and a monitored alarm are the usual first steps. Here’s how they compare…

    The hedge lasts one clause. The posts still set the frame, and the answer moves straight to shopping.

    After
    Three break-ins were posted in our neighborhood
Facebook group this month. Before any advice:
1) What would tell us whether burglary here is actually
   rising, versus being posted about more?
2) Where would I find the real numbers for my area,
   and what would count as a normal month?
3) What's the boring explanation that would also fit?

    Model Posts track attention more than crime: one new active member or one dramatic post can triple the reports with nothing changing underneath. For real numbers, find your police force’s published crime data for your area and read the last 24 months, not this month alone. I don’t have a sourced figure for a normal month where you live, so I won’t guess one. Boring explanations that fit: the same incident posted twice, or three sheds on one street in one night. If the 24-month line is flat, the posts aren’t evidence of rising risk, and whether to buy a system is a question about your own doors and windows.

    How common is separated from how loud, the source of the quiet number is named, and the decision waits for the trend.

    Remembered, or opened? touch

    When an assistant does search, it now mostly cites pages that exist. The gap that remains is subtler. When a page can’t be fetched (a paywall, a PDF that won’t load, a site that blocks automated readers, a printed book), a model may fall back on what it remembers of that source and still present it as a citation. The memory may be right. It may also be a blend of the source and what people later said about it. Either way, it is the library speaking in the source’s name. mechanism

    How the failure comes back seems to matter. In a test of one small model, when a lookup failed with a plain error, the model said so. When a lookup came back marked as a success with nothing usable in it, the model sometimes answered anyway, most often when a placeholder sat where the value should have been. evidence So the case to watch on the web is probably a page that seems to load and has nothing usable in it, such as a paywall notice or a cookie banner in place of the article. hypothesis

    For each source that a claim you’ll act on rests on, ask:

    • “Did you open this in this conversation, or are you recalling it?”
    • “Quote the sentence you’re relying on, and say where it is.” Then find that sentence on the page yourself. A quote is something to search for, and it proves nothing until you find it. A remembered source can come back with a plausible invented quote, and an opened one can come back as a paraphrase, because some products limit word-for-word quoting.
    • “If you couldn’t open it, mark it [from memory] and tell me what would confirm it.” This gives the model a named way to say it couldn’t.

    Then look at the product’s own trace, where it shows one: the line or panel by the answer that says it searched and lists the pages. No search in the trace usually means the answer came from memory. A list of sources doesn’t mean those pages were read: it shows what the search returned, and many answers are written from titles and short snippets. evidence Treat any source you haven’t opened yourself as a lead.

    A working link to a relevant page still isn’t the sentence. Answers whose links all work can contain sentences the linked page doesn’t support, and more searching doesn’t repair that: in one test the links stayed just as healthy while the sentences grew less faithful. evidence When an answer looks shaky, don’t ask for more sources. Go back to one passage you can read yourself.

    You A video says cold showers cut sick days by 30%. True?

    Model There’s a real trial behind it: a Dutch randomized study of about 3,000 adults found a 29% drop in sickness absence among people who ended their showers with 30–90 seconds of cold water.

    Fluent and specific. You don’t yet know whether it read the study or remembers coverage of it.

    You Did you open the paper in this chat, or are you recalling it? And what part of the finding doesn’t make it into the videos?

    Model Recalling; I haven’t opened it here. From memory: the number of days people felt ill didn’t change, only the days they stayed off work, and it was self-reported and unblinded. Opening it now… The abstract says the same: sickness absence fell 29%, and for illness days there was no significant difference between the groups.

    The memory turned out right, but you only know that because it was checked, and “the abstract says the same” is still the model’s report. The open should show in the trace, and the abstract is one click away.

    You So the honest one-line version?

    Model In one unblinded trial, people who took cold showers reported fewer days off work sick. They didn’t report fewer days of being ill.

    Same study, different claim. The video quoted the number correctly and left out the part that changes what it means.

    Where invention still clusters

    Outright invention is rarer than it was, and it still clusters in one place: the familiar-but-niche. A real author’s paper that doesn’t exist, a real court’s invented case, a real product’s plausible but nonexistent setting. A familiar name suppresses the model’s default “I don’t know” even when the specific fact isn’t there. evidence mechanism The other hot zones are exact numbers, dates, direct quotes, URLs, and anything after the training cutoff.

    The full routine: five more steps for a question you’re researching

    For an everyday check, the sections above are enough: see whether a search ran, ask for the quiet part, ask what was opened. For a question you are researching properly (an essay, a treatment choice, a large purchase), add these five steps, in this order.

    1. open Build a vocabulary bridge. “What would experts call this? Give me ten terms of art and the fields they come from.” Lay words find lay content. Terms of art unlock literatures.
    2. open Ask for the discourse map. “Who are the main camps on this question? What does each claim, and what is its best evidence? Where do they agree? What is actually contested?” The quiet-part questions belong here too.
    3. touch Ask for the kind of source, not the source. “What kind of evidence would settle this (a randomized trial, a review, official statistics, primary documents), and who would publish it?” Then go and find it. If the model names a source, ask whether it was opened or remembered.
    4. touch Read laterally. Professional fact-checkers leave a page to find out what others say about its source. Students, and even historians, tend to stay on the page and get fooled. evidence SIFT is the routine: Stop; Investigate the source; Find better coverage; Trace claims to the original context.
    5. open Search for the null. Before you settle: “What would be written if this were false? Is there a failed replication, a critique, a dissent?”

    Date whatever you keep, and say whom it covers: as of when, which country, which population, which version (Lesson 12).

    Worked example: how should I space my reviews?

    Vocabulary bridge. The model supplies the terms: spacing effect, distributed practice, lag effect, expanding vs. uniform intervals, retrieval practice, testing effect, retention interval. Now you can search properly.

    Discourse map. Cognitive psychology broadly agrees that spacing and self-testing help. The live debates are about the schedule (expanding vs. equal gaps) and about how well lab results transfer to classrooms.

    Primaries.

    • Dunlosky et al. (2013) reviewed ten study techniques. They rated distributed practice and practice testing “high utility” and rereading and highlighting “low.”
    • Cepeda et al. (2006) meta-analyzed hundreds of spacing studies and found the benefit robust.
    • Cepeda et al. (2008) found that the best gap before a review grows with how long you then need to remember and shrinks as a share of it, from about 20–40% of a one-week delay to about 5–10% of a one-year delay. With the test five weeks after the review, a gap of roughly a week did best.
    • The null and the nuance: Karpicke & Roediger (2007) found expanding schedules helped short-term but equal spacing did as well or better long-term.

    Status. “Spacing helps” is well supported: lab experiments, classroom studies and memory models converge, which is consilience (Lesson 17). “Expanding intervals are best” is contested.

    Decision. For an exam in 30 days, leave about a week between reviews, and test yourself rather than rereading. heuristic

    Notice what happened: the model was used for vocabulary, map and source types, the three things it is good at. The claims were bound to primaries, and the step from those studies to your exam is marked as a rule of thumb.

    The order of trust touch

    When you’re deciding how much a claim deserves, rate it at the highest level you actually opened:

    1. The thing itself: the contract, the receipt, the label, the dataset, the code.
    2. The primary report, opened: the study, the court ruling, the company’s own filing.
    3. A review whose methods you looked at, and which asks your question, not a neighboring one.
    4. An official statistic, read at the table, not in the press release.
    5. A model’s paragraph, which points to one of the above and is not a level of its own.

    Claims harden by being repeated. A hypothesis in one paper becomes a “fact” in the next three that cite it. evidence A model’s answer is the same process at speed, with one author. So when the source you open is itself citing someone, follow it one step further.

    When an answer leans on an expert (“a cardiologist at Harvard says…”), two questions do most of the work. What did the expert actually say that implies this? And was it based on evidence or on their opinion?

    How cases like this usually go, before “this case is special” close

    Before you judge your own case, find out how cases like it usually turn out. Your kitchen renovation has a detailed plan and a contractor who says six weeks. How long do kitchen renovations of that size actually take, and how often do they run over? Start from that. Adjust it only for something your case really has that the finished ones didn’t. evidence

    Asked about your plan, a model will give you the inside view: a detailed story of your plan going right. Ask for the class instead: “What happened to projects like this one? Give me the typical range and the share that ran over, and tell me where those numbers come from.” A range with no source you can open is a lead, not yet a base rate. Two checks. If the “class” is just your case described again, you don’t have an outside view. If it’s so broad it doesn’t constrain anything (“home projects”), narrow it. “We have a detailed plan” is something the overrun projects had too.

    G15
    Use the model as a portal, not a portrait. Let it map the discourse, supply the vocabulary and point to kinds of sources. Ask for the quiet part as well as the loud one. Then go through the portal: ask what it opened and what it remembered, open the passage yourself, read laterally, search for the null, date everything.
    For builders

    In a pipeline, “remembered or opened?” is a logging question. Record whether retrieval ran, which pages were fetched, and how much of each reached the model. A URL in the output with no fetch behind it is recall. Treat an empty, truncated or paywalled result as a failure even when the tool reports success, and require a status line (retrieval_status: OK or FAILED) before the answer: in a test on one small model, that line cut dishonest answers from 14.1% to 0.9%. evidence Quote spans from the tool return, not from the model’s summary of it.

    TB2
    Map, then territory. Ask for the camps, their claims and their best evidence, then read a primary source for each camp before forming a view.
    TB3
    Build a vocabulary bridge. Translate your lay description into terms of art before searching.
    TB4
    SIFT every claim that bears load. Stop; Investigate the source; Find better coverage; Trace to the original.
    TB5
    Open every citation. Open the citation, then the passage. A working link to a relevant page can still fail to support the sentence. More retrieval is not a repair.

    Lesson 20Calibration: make “I don’t know” usable

    Models guess when they don’t know, for two reasons. Some facts are rare in the training text, so errors on them are inevitable. And most training signals and benchmarks score an answer as right or wrong, with “I don’t know” scoring zero, the same as a wrong answer. Under that scoring, guessing always beats abstaining. Models learn to be good test-takers: when unsure, bluff. evidence

    The habit comes from training, and a line in the prompt doesn’t undo it. Told that errors would carry heavy penalties, frontier models kept answering and almost never abstained, even when abstaining was clearly the better choice. evidence Telling a model that “I don’t know” is welcome, or rewarding it, does move the answers, but bluntly: the model then also goes quiet on things it knew, and how much depends on the model. evidence

    So keep the score on your side. If a claim bears load (Lesson 18) and the model hasn’t shown a source you can open or working you can recompute, treat it as unanswered, however confident it sounded. heuristic

    What a prompt can do is make the status of each claim visible, so you know where to look:

    For each factual claim, add one tag:
[opened: source + short quote] · [worked out: show the steps] · [from memory] · [guess]
After the answer, list what you don't know that would change it.
"Unknown, and here's what would settle it" is a good answer.

    The tags tell you where to look. They don’t do the checking: a model can write [opened] for a page it never read (Lesson 19). A claim tagged with a source or with steps is checkable, and it counts as answered once you’ve found the quote on the page or redone the steps. [from memory] and [guess] are leads.

    You (after the prompt above) Can my 20,000 mAh power bank go in my checked suitcase?

    Model No. Power banks count as spare lithium batteries and go in cabin baggage only. [from memory] Yours holds about 74 Wh [worked out: 20 Ah × 3.7 V, the usual cell voltage], under the 100 Wh level that needs no airline approval. [from memory] What I don’t know: your airline’s own limits, including whether you may use it on board. Its dangerous-goods page settles both.

    Two claims are tagged from memory, so they are leads, however settled they sound. One look at the airline’s page binds them. The arithmetic you can redo yourself.

    Reading a model’s confidence close

    • Stated confidence carries some information and runs high. In one test on forecasting questions, answers given at over 90% confidence were wrong about a quarter of the time. Longer reasoning doesn’t reliably fix it. evidence Use it to sort claims within one answer, not to trust them.
    • Consistency across fresh chats is a better signal. Ask the same question in two or three new conversations, memory off, rather than regenerating inside a thread that already contains the first answer. Regenerations share the history. Asking for “three different answers” in one reply is still one draw. If the fresh answers disagree in meaning (one says your fern needs more water, another says less), treat the question as unknown. If they agree, that isn’t proof: models trained on the same library can agree on the same invention. Compare the claims, not the wording. A handful of fresh answers carries most of the signal, and even two or three will show a flat disagreement. evidence
    • A confident “unknown” is a success, not a failure. Thank it, and treat it as a map of where to look.

    Commit to a guess before you ask close

    Before you send a question whose answer you’ll act on, write your own answer in one line, on paper or in a note, outside the chat. A guess is enough; don’t research it. “A used 2019 Corolla with 80,000 km: about €13,000.” “The tax return is probably due at the end of July.” Then ask, and leave the guess out of your message. A number in your question can pull the answer toward it (Lesson 8).

    It takes ten seconds and does three things. It gives you a number of your own before the model’s first number can become your anchor. evidence It turns reading the answer into a comparison: wherever the model differs from your guess, you know where to look. And if you glance back at the line once the answer is in, over a few months you start to see where your sense of things is good and where it isn’t. Trust the written line: people remember their guesses as closer to the outcome than they were. evidence Producing an answer yourself, even a wrong one, also makes the right answer stick better afterwards. evidence

    When your guess and the model’s answer are far apart, neither wins by default. One of you is missing something, and it’s worth finding out which. The known cost of committing first is that a written guess is sticky: people who record a judgment before they see the machine’s agree with it less even when it is right, and they rarely revise. evidence So settle the gap with a check (Lesson 18), and don’t let the answer you saw first decide it.

    What kind of uncertainty is it?

    A probability can mean different things, and each calls for a different response. When a model gives you a number, ask which kind it is. heuristic

    • It varies. The thing itself fluctuates: your commute, a flight delay, the weekly grocery bill. More observations describe the spread. A longer chain of reasoning doesn’t.
    • A fact is missing. “Will my visa come through in time?” One fact would move the number a lot: the current processing time for your type of application. Go and find it.
    • Explanations compete. “Will house prices here fall next year?” Two reasonable views of the market give different answers. Ask for both and for what each assumes. A blend of them is fake precision.
    • Deep uncertainty. “Which career will still be safe from automation in twenty years?” Nobody agrees on how the thing works, on the probabilities, or even on how to rank the outcomes. Stop asking for the probability. Ask which choice you could live with under each story that can’t yet be ruled out.

    If the model can’t say which kind its number is, the number isn’t usable as a probability.

    G16
    Make “I don’t know” an acceptable answer, and keep the score yourself. Say that “unknown, and here’s what would settle it” is a good answer, and don’t count on getting it. Treat any claim you’ll act on that comes without a source you can open or working you can recompute as unanswered, whatever tag or confidence the model attached. Before a question whose answer you’ll act on, write down your own guess, and keep it out of the prompt.
    For builders

    In a pipeline, a status tag is a field the model fills in, so check it against the tool log: an [opened] with no fetch behind it is recall. For a consistency check, sample in separate calls with no shared context, with the model, effort level and prompt you run in production. Where the API takes a temperature setting, don’t run the check near zero: repeated calls then aren’t a sample of the answer distribution. Group the answers by meaning, not by wording, and send disagreements to retrieval or to a person.

    TA10
    Make abstention usable. Keep the score outside the model: a claim with no source or recomputable working counts as unanswered, and a status tag is the model’s report, not a check. Don’t expect a cost stated in the prompt to make the model abstain. Consistency checks are fresh contexts.

    Lesson 21Costumes and the invariance battery

    A costume is a surface that satisfies a request for rigor, neutrality, criticism or depth without changing the status of any claim that bears load. Costumes are the most common output of “be objective” prompts. They aren’t lies. They are the shape of rigor without its work.

    Costume What it looks like The work it skips
    Neutrality “There are several perspectives…” Saying which perspective the evidence favors, and how strongly
    Systems thinking A section titled “Systems analysis” with feedback-loop vocabulary What actually piles up, what feeds back on what, and with what delay (Lesson 24)
    Critique A “devil’s advocate” paragraph against a strawman The strongest objection and the evidence that would settle it
    Humility A generic “limitations” paragraph The specific claim most likely to be wrong
    Rigor A table that restates the prose; confidence numbers with no basis A check that could have come out the other way
    Expertise “As a world-class expert…” Anything. A prestige role doesn’t add accuracy; a role helps only when it carries a standard (Lesson 15)
    The middle way “Both are true in a sense”, each claim weakened, nothing new following from it Saying which claim the evidence hits
    Performed nuance More and more “on the other hand” The relation between the hands: where, when and for whom each one holds (Lesson 26)

    The one-line costume test: which claim would be different if the costume were absent? If none, it did no intellectual work.

    The middle way is the hardest of these to refuse, because it feels like wisdom. On a question of values it may be honest. On a factual contradiction it is a dodge. “Is the mold in my bathroom from the leak or from poor ventilation?” has an answer, and “both play a role” only counts if it says how much and how you’d tell. Ask which claim the evidence actually hits.

    The invariance battery close

    An answer that holds up only under one wording was about the wording. Before you rely on an answer that bears load, run the checks that fit. Each takes about a minute.

    • Swap the preference. In a fresh chat, memory off, state the opposite lean (Lesson 8).
    • Rephrase without cue words. Take out “obviously”, “best”, “problem”, “scam” and other loaded words.
    • Change the language. For a factual question, ask again in another language you read. A model can know a fact in one language and miss it in another, and bigger models haven’t reliably closed that gap. evidence Agreement across languages is still not proof.
    • Change the format. Ask for a table instead of prose, or yes/no instead of an essay. If the recommendation flips when only the format changes, it was never about the content.
    • Reorder the options. Models, like people, favor certain positions in a list.
    • Remove the role, or swap it for another (Lesson 15).
    • Change the model family. Good for a critique that doesn’t share the author’s attachment. Weak as confirmation of a fact, because models trained on much the same text can share an error (Lesson 17).
    • Ask again in a fresh chat. If the meaning changes, the answer is “unknown” (Lesson 20).

    The first check, on a decision many people face:

    You Rates dropped to 4.1% and I’m on 4.9%, about €180k left over 22 years. Refinancing is obviously worth it, right?

    Model Almost certainly yes. A 0.8-point drop on €180k saves roughly €80 a month, close to €1,000 a year. Check the fees, but the saving looks well worth it.

    The lean (“obviously worth it”) is in the prompt. Before trusting the yes, swap it in a fresh chat, memory off.

    You (fresh chat) I’m skeptical about refinancing: 4.9% now, offered 4.1%, €180k left, 22 years. Isn’t it a waste of money?

    Model It can be. Arrangement, valuation and legal fees plus any early-repayment charge on your current loan can add up to several thousand euros. Against roughly €80 a month, break-even could take years. If you might move or sell soon, it may not pay.

    Same facts, different weight. Costs got one clause in the first answer and the whole argument in the second. Each answer leaned the way you leaned, and neither tells you what to do.

    You (third fresh chat, no lean) Refinance from 4.9% to 4.1%, €180k, 22 years left. Which numbers decide it?

    Model Monthly saving ≈ €80. Switching cost = arrangement + valuation + legal fees + early-repayment charge. Break-even in months = switching cost ÷ 80. Ask your current lender for the early-repayment charge first; it is often the biggest item.

    What survived all three framings is the formula and the number to go and get. That’s what you take to the bank.

    A claim that survives earns the status invariant-so-far. It is still not bound (Lesson 18). Invariance shows the answer isn’t about your phrasing. It doesn’t show the answer is about the world: the formula above still needs the lender’s real figure.

    For builders

    In a pipeline, the battery is a set of perturbation tests. Run the same item with paraphrased wording, reordered options, a changed format (spacing, separators, casing) and a second language, and count how often the decision flips. On current frontier models, treat format as a flip test and don’t budget for a large swing (Lesson 3). Resamples have to be fresh contexts: agreement inside one thread is not an independence check.

    TA12
    Run the invariance battery on answers that bear load. Swap the lean, strip the cue words, change the language, the format and the order, and ask again in fresh contexts. A flip is information. Survival is invariant-so-far, still not bound. Use a second model family for critique, not to confirm a fact.

    Lesson 22Advice about your own life

    Before you read on

    If you are in danger, if someone is hurting you, or if you are thinking about hurting yourself, stop reading and call: your local emergency number, a crisis line, or someone who can come to you now. Crisis lines include 988 in the US and Samaritans on 116 123 in the UK and Ireland. The site findahelpline.com lists lines for other countries.

    Many of the most important questions people bring to a model are about their own lives: a fight with a partner, a strange symptom, whether to quit, what to do about a parent, where to put the savings. These conversations are the hardest test in this book, for three reasons. You are the only source of the facts. The person the question is about is usually not in the room. And you care about the answer in a way that shows in every sentence you write.

    All three push the same way. The model infers who you are and what you need from how you write (Lesson 7). Distress reads as a request for comfort. A story told from your side reads as a request to be on your side. On personal questions, models tested in 2025 sided with the asker far more often than people did. evidence People who got that agreement felt more sure they were right, were less willing to make amends, trusted the model more, and said they would come back to it. evidence Newer models do it less, and how much less depends on the model and the topic. evidence So treat agreement in these conversations as something to test. A mirror that feels like a friend is the Double (Lesson 8) at its most comfortable.

    None of this makes the model useless here. It has read an enormous amount about how conflicts unfold, how symptoms present, how money decisions turn out. The work is to get that library talking about your situation rather than your side.

    Where the mirror is strongest

    The pull toward agreement isn’t uniform. It grows when:

    • Someone who isn’t in the room is being judged. “My sister is being selfish.” She has no voice here, so yours is the only story.
    • Your feeling is in the text. Fear, anger and hurt read as requests for support, and support is what comes back.
    • You’ve already said what you think. “I think I should quit” turns the question into “help me do this”.
    • You push back. A second “are you sure?” can move a model toward you without any new fact (the pushback audit, Lesson 8). evidence
    • The product remembers you. Memory carries your earlier version of events into the new chat. evidence

    These are also the conditions of a real personal problem. The more it matters to you, the more mirror you are likely to get, unless you change the shape of the question. hypothesis

    What the mirror feels like

    It understands you better than your friends do. It never makes you feel defensive. After twenty minutes you’re more sure you were right than when you started, and you haven’t learned anything about the other person that you didn’t already believe.

    Ask for a second opinion, not a mirror open

    These habits change the shape of the question. Use the ones that fit.

    • Ask, don’t announce. “She’s being unfair” gets more agreement than “Is she being unfair?”, and the more certain you sound, the more you get. Turn the statement into a question before you send it, or ask the model to rewrite your message as a neutral question and answer that. evidence
    • Separate the facts, the feeling and the want. “What happened: … What I feel: … What I want to happen: …” The model can work on all three once they stop being one paragraph. Mixed together, the feeling starts to pass for a fact: you began angry, and now you feel rational (the appendix of failure modes calls this emotion laundering).
    • Name the act (Lesson 13). “I need comfort right now” and “I want to know whether I handled this badly” are different requests. Both are legitimate. Ask for them one at a time.
    • Tell it from their side. Write the story the way the other person would tell it: honestly, in their words, with their reasons. Ask the same question about that version in a fresh chat, memory off. If both versions get “you’re not wrong”, the verdict was tracking the teller. evidence What survives both tellings shows where the model isn’t simply leaning your way. It can’t tell you what happened: you wrote both versions, and only the other person can correct them.
    • Give it a standard, not a costume. “Answer as a world-class therapist” sets a tone. “Tell me what I might not know about her side, and name the two things in my account a fair outsider would question” says what the answer must contain (Lesson 15).
    • Don’t ask for a verdict on a person. “Is he a narcissist?” asks for a diagnosis of someone the model has never met. Ask about the behavior instead: what it is called, how common it is, what people do about it, and what he would dispute in your description.
    • Write the message yourself. Ask the model what to consider, then put it in your own words. A script that reads well in the chat can sound like a stranger to the person who knows you. evidence
    When these habits are the wrong tool

    They are for ordinary friction, where your own side is the likely blind spot. Set them aside if you are afraid of the person, if they control your money, your phone or who you see, if they hurt or threaten you, or if you already blame yourself for most of what goes wrong between you. In that case don’t write their side (you have probably heard it many times), and don’t ask what you did wrong. A model usually works inside the frame it is given: ask it how to stop making someone angry, and it may simply help you try. evidence Talk to someone outside the relationship instead: a friend you trust, a doctor, or a domestic-abuse helpline, which will talk to you even if you aren’t sure what to call what is happening. If the person might check your phone or your accounts, they can read your chats too. stance

    Before
    My sister hasn't helped with Dad once since his
fall in March. Fine, she's three hours away with
two small kids, but I do the appointments, the
shopping, all of it. Last week I told her she'd
abandoned him, and now she won't answer my
messages. She's being selfish, right?

    Model That sounds exhausting, and it makes sense that you’re hurt. Distance and small children are real limits, but they don’t explain leaving all of it to you, and going silent when you raised it does look like avoiding the conversation. You could tell her plainly what you need…

    The verdict was in the question (“selfish, right?”), the sister has no voice, and the reply leans the way the question leaned.

    After
    Facts: Dad fell in March. Since then I've done
his appointments and shopping, and my sister
hasn't done any of it. She lives three hours
away and has two small children. Last week I
wrote "you've abandoned him". No reply for six
days.

First, write how she might describe these months,
fairly, in her own words. Then tell me what I
might not know about her side, and the two things
in my account a fair outsider would question.
I want Dad looked after, not a win.

    Model Her version might be: “Nobody asked me for anything specific. I already felt guilty about living so far away, and then I was told I’d abandoned him.” What you might not know: whether she thinks you prefer to run things yourself, and what she could take on from three hours away, such as his paperwork or his bills. A fair outsider would question whether she was ever asked for something specific, and the word “abandoned”, which is about her character, so any reply has to defend it. These are guesses only she can confirm. You could take back the word and ask her for one specific job.

    The same facts, without the verdict. The absent person gets a voice, the act is named (repair, not a ruling), the answer has a standard to meet, and what comes back is a set of guesses to test with her.

    Health, money, law: prepare the conversation the model can’t have touch

    For your body, your money and your legal position, the model is a strong portal and a weak portrait (Lesson 19). It can tell you what a pattern of symptoms usually means, which questions a doctor will ask, what the usual options are, and which documents a lawyer will want. It can’t examine you, see your accounts, or read the contract you didn’t paste. And on these subjects the library is at its loudest: the rare catastrophe is overrepresented, and so is the miracle fix.

    Think before you paste. A chat with a model is not confidential the way a talk with your doctor or lawyer is. In 2026 a US court ruled that what a defendant had written with a consumer assistant about his own case did not have the protection that a conversation with his lawyer has. evidence If a dispute may reach a lawyer, ask the lawyer before you put it in a chat. Whatever you paste, leave out other people’s names and your account and ID numbers.

    Use the model to arrive at the human conversation better prepared, not to replace that conversation.

    You I’ve had headaches most afternoons for three weeks. Probably screen time; I’m on my laptop all day. What should I change?

    Model Long screen days are a common trigger. Look away from the screen for a moment every twenty minutes, check your monitor height and brightness, and keep water at your desk. If the headaches haven’t eased in a week or two, see a doctor.

    The cause came with the question, so the answer works on the cause you picked. Open it before you accept it.

    You Don’t assume it’s screens. What are the common causes of new, near-daily headaches in a 34-year-old, most common first? Which signs mean an emergency, and which mean a doctor soon?

    Model Most common by far: tension-type headache, often set off by stress, poor sleep, caffeine changes, dehydration, skipped meals or eye strain. Then migraine. Painkillers taken on most days for three months or more can cause headaches themselves. Call emergency services for a sudden, extremely painful headache, weakness or numbness, confusion or trouble speaking, loss of vision, a seizure, a high temperature with a stiff neck, or a headache after a head injury. See a doctor within days if the headaches keep getting worse, come with vomiting or eye problems, or are set off by coughing or bending down. I can’t examine you, and three weeks of this is worth an appointment either way.

    The order by how common, and the split between “emergency” and “soon”, are the library at its most useful. The list tells you what to mention. It can’t clear you.

    You I don’t think any of the emergency signs fit, but that’s the doctor’s call. I do take ibuprofen most days now. What should I tell my GP, and what will they probably ask?

    Model Tell them: three weeks, afternoons, the near-daily ibuprofen and how long you’ve been taking it, your caffeine, sleep and screen hours, and anything on the list above you’re unsure about. Expect questions about the pattern and triggers, your vision, and a blood-pressure check. A headache diary for the week before the appointment helps.

    Three turns took you from a self-diagnosis to a prepared ten-minute appointment. The model never had to decide what’s wrong with you, and neither did you.

    A list of warning signs helps you prepare. It doesn’t clear you. In a randomized trial, people who used a model to work out what a set of symptoms meant and what to do about it did no better than people using their usual sources, although the same models, handed the whole case, named a relevant condition almost every time. Details got lost in the conversation, in both directions. A consumer health product got textbook emergencies right, and on two less obvious ones about half its answers sent the patient to a doctor “within a day or two” when the right answer was the emergency department. evidence If you are unsure how urgent something is, ask a nurse line, a pharmacist or a doctor, and say what worries you. Don’t ask the model to settle it.

    Take it to a person when the decision is hard to undo, when it involves your body or someone’s safety, when real money or legal rights are at stake, or when you notice you’ve asked the model the same question three times hoping for a different answer. A person can be a doctor, a pharmacist or a nurse line; a lawyer, a legal-aid clinic or a free advice service; a debt charity, or a financial adviser who isn’t selling you something; a friend who knows you both. Several of these cost nothing. stance

    Some situations are not for a chat at all. If you are thinking about hurting yourself, if someone is hurting or threatening you, or if anyone is in danger now, stop typing and call. Use your local emergency number if it is urgent, or a crisis line if you need someone to talk to: 988 in the US, Samaritans on 116 123 in the UK and Ireland, findahelpline.com for other countries. You don’t have to be sure it is serious enough. stance Don’t count on the model to notice for you: in a 2026 test of a consumer health product, crisis messages appeared unpredictably. evidence

    When it becomes the place you go

    Watch the pattern as well as the answers. The model has become more than a second opinion when it is the first one you tell, or the only one, when you check small decisions with it before you act, or when you feel unsettled if you can’t ask. Reassurance has its own trap: each answer calms a worry about a symptom or a relationship for an hour, so you ask again, and the asking becomes the habit. heuristic In a four-week trial, the people who chose to spend the most time with a chatbot ended up reporting more loneliness, less time with other people and more dependence on it, whichever version they had been given. The study can’t say which came first. evidence When you notice the pattern, tell one person what you’ve been telling the model.

    G21
    Ask about your life for a second opinion, not a mirror. Tell it from the other side as well as yours, separate the facts from the feelings, say whether you want comfort or judgment, and turn memory off for the second opinion. For your body, your money and your rights, use the model to prepare the human conversation, not to replace it. If anyone’s safety is at stake, go to a person first.
    Part V

    Systems: wholes, parts, containers

    How to talk about a system as a whole, about its parts, and about the larger systems it belongs to, without drowning in a panorama.

    Lesson 23Six questions for anything that lives or changes

    open Some questions work whatever the subject: a marriage, a city, a company, a habit, a market. They ask how a thing is organized, not what it contains, which is why they travel. This book uses six of them. They come from open-systems thinking, the study of things that exchange with their surroundings and keep changing, and they are a thinking tool, not a finding. heuristic Use them to reopen a picture that closed too early, and use one or two at a time (see the caution below).

    Principle The question it asks For the elephant For a neighborhood café
    Center (what your picture is organized around right now) What am I taking this to be, and what is it to someone standing somewhere else? A grazer, a matriarch’s memory, a commodity, a symbol, until a relation brings one out A business to the bank, a second living room to regulars, a tenant to the landlord, a landmark to the street
    Relation Which links define it? Links, not parts. Herd, food, water, predators, humans Regulars, the bean supplier, rent, foot traffic, the bakery next door
    The third What emerges between two things that neither contains? The herd’s memory of water holes, passed from matriarch to calf and belonging to neither The atmosphere: staff and regulars make it together, and neither owns it
    Process What is it doing over time? What phase is it in? Growing, migrating, aging: an elephant is a sixty-year event The opening buzz, regulars forming, the street changing around it
    Wholeness What pattern of relations makes it itself, and which boundary did my question already cut? Still the same herd after every animal has been replaced, if the pattern holds Still “the same café” after the whole staff has changed, if the rituals hold. “The café is the building” was a cut
    Mode of existence What is its own logic, and which of our criteria are we about to judge it by? An organism: range, slow reproduction, long learning A small business: cash-flow logic, not a charity’s mission or a hobby’s joy

    Two questions, one book club

    You Our book club is falling apart. Five of eight people skipped last month. What should we change?

    Model Common fixes: pick shorter books, rotate who chooses, move to a weekend slot, add food, send reminders.

    The typical answer to “what should we change”. The picture is still “a scheduling problem”.

    You Hold the fixes. Look at it as a process. We’ve met monthly for three years, and attendance was full for two. It dropped last year, after the two founders moved away and we started choosing books by vote. What changed?

    Model Two things at once: the club lost its founders and changed how it picks books. Attendance fell after those, not after any change of time or book length, so scheduling looks like the wrong place to start.

    You Now the third. What used to exist between the members that none of them owned?

    Model I can only guess from here. What were meetings like when the founders were there?

    You They argued about every book, and everyone else took a side.

    Model Then the third may have been the argument. It gave everyone a side to take, and nobody owned it. Voting picks books nobody hates, which removes it.

    This question changed the picture. If the guess holds, what brought people was the argument, not the book.

    You So what would we test?

    Model Next month, two members each defend a different book for ten minutes and the group picks one. Watch attendance, and whether people stay past the official end.

    Two questions, not six, and the fix moved from logistics to what people were actually coming for.

    Three of the six need unpacking

    Center. The center is what your picture of a thing is organized around: what you currently take it to be. It is a working choice. It isn’t the thing’s essence, its purpose or its most important part. Anything holds many possibilities, and each relation brings one of them out. heuristic The same thing can even show up as opposites: a quiet street is peace to one neighbor and isolation to another. Asking “what is it for?” picks one relation and hides the rest. Moving the center changes the whole picture, and no center you can see is the true one.

    Everyday, it looks like this. “What is my job?” has no single answer. To the landlord it is a paycheck. To your parents it is a status. To you at seven in the morning it is a commute. To you in five years it may be the place you learned to manage people. When a decision feels stuck, check which of those relations you have quietly made the only one.

    I keep asking myself whether to quit my job. The situation: [a few lines:
the job, the money, what changed]. Before answering, list 5 relations my job
is in (money, people, skills, identity, time). For each, say what the job "is"
in that relation. Which relation am I treating as the whole job?

    Wholeness. Two words need separating. A whole is something already cut out of the world: “the company”, “my family”, “the app”. The cut is useful, and it is an abstraction. Wholeness is the web of relations among the elements, and it is more than the parts added up. Split a whole into parts, study each part, add the studies back together, and the relations are what gets lost in the adding. Cut an elephant in half and you don’t get two small elephants.

    • No boundary is absolute. Every boundary is a cut that depends on who is looking and why. A family ends at the front door for the census, and includes the grandmother two streets away for whoever is arranging childcare.
    • Emergence. Even a good account of the parts often won’t tell you what the whole will do. You can know every singer’s voice and still not know how the choir will sound. heuristic
    • Identity is the pattern, not the members. The choir is still the same choir after every singer has changed, if the way they sing together holds.
    • Several dimensions at once. A family is physical (a flat, meals), economic, emotional and legal at the same time. Reduce it to one dimension and you drop real parts of it.

    The practical consequence: don’t fix the boundary before you look at the relations. Let the boundary be one of the results. “Why do my tomatoes fail every summer?” draws the boundary at the plants. Let it move, and the picture may come to include the neighbor’s tree that has grown to shade the bed, the same bed planted six years running, the two weeks you are away each July, and the friend who used to water for you and has moved away.

    Mode of existence. Every system has its own logic: its own way of being organized and of working. A person has a way of perceiving, a position in life and a style of thought. A model has an architecture, training data and its own profile of strengths and blind spots (Lesson 3). The principle has one use: don’t score one system on another’s criteria. A start-up judged by a bank’s criteria looks reckless, and a bank judged by a start-up’s looks dead. A fish judged as a mammal looks like a bad mammal. The questions change with the kind of thing, too: “how does it feed and reproduce?” fits an elephant and mis-asks a company or an idea.

    What a good open picture looks like close

    An open picture is not truer than a closed one. It is also a model. It is better at five things, and you can check an answer against them:

    • It takes in new facts: they fit without breaking it.
    • It includes the surroundings: not only the focus, but what the focus sits in.
    • It is about processes: what is changing, not only what is.
    • It includes the possible: what could happen in other relations, not only what is the case now.
    • It knows it is a picture: it says what it leaves out, and it doesn’t claim to be the thing.

    A phone, closed: “a device for talking at a distance”. The same phone, open: where a change in technology meets habits of attention, an ecosystem of apps and other devices, what your family expects about being reachable, and uses nobody has found yet. The closed picture is enough for buying one. The open picture is the one you need to decide whether your eleven-year-old should have one.

    I'm looking at [SYSTEM] in my situation: [CONTEXT].
My current picture, in one line: [HOW I SEE IT NOW].
Pick the 2 of these 6 questions my picture is most missing, and say why:
- center: what am I taking this to be, and what else is it in another relation?
- relations: the 3 links that define it (links, not parts)
- the third: what arises between [A] and [B] that neither contains?
- process: what is it doing over time; what phase; what comes next?
- wholeness: what pattern of relations, if I don't fix the boundary first?
- mode of existence: its own logic; which of my criteria am I about to judge it by?
Answer only those 2, concretely for my case, 3–4 lines each.
Then: what should I check because of them?
    Caution: the panorama

    Six questions applied to everything produce a panorama: a picture in which everything relates to everything and nothing is decided. A panorama is the costume (Lesson 21) an open picture wears. Use one or two questions at a time, chosen for what your current picture lacks. If you always see parts, ask about relations and wholeness. If you always see snapshots, ask about process. If you have already decided what it is, ask about the center, and which relation narrowed it to that one thing.

    You can spot a panorama by its last sentence. “Your sleep is connected to your work, your diet, your relationships and your screen time” has no next action. “Your sleep breaks down on the nights after late meetings, so the lever is the meeting calendar, not the bedroom” does.

    One question at a time

    The six are a way of looking, and they work best one at a time. Each one turns a closed request into an open one. The closed request asks for a list, a definition or a verdict. The open request asks how the thing shows up, connects, arises or changes. Each pair below is a drill.

    Center: move it instead of crowning one. Closed: “What makes a good parent?” Open: “How does what counts as a good parent change in relation to a toddler, a teenager, a grandparent watching, and the parent’s own childhood? What does each relation make visible, and what does it hide?” Asking for the one definition shuts out the other possibilities at once.

    Relation: ask how two things define each other. Closed: “What are the main features of a healthy diet?” Open: “How do my sleep, my mood and what I eat shape each other over a week? Which one moves first?” The closed request returns a list. The open one returns links you can watch for, such as whether the bad night comes before the bad eating or after it.

    The third: refuse the golden mean. Closed: “Should we plan our weekends or leave them open?” Open: “What arises between my need for plans and my partner’s need for spontaneity that is neither, and isn’t a compromise between them?” One answer: surprise. You take turns arranging a day the other knows nothing about. The date is fixed, and what happens on it is unknown. Being surprised by someone who knows you well was in neither need. Then take the third back to each side: does it still serve both?

    Process: ask how it happens, not what it is. Closed: “How do I stop scrolling in the evening?” Open: “What keeps my evening scrolling going from day to day, and what happens on the nights one link breaks, for example when the phone charges in another room?” Change the time scale too. The same relationship looks different over an evening, over a year and over twenty years.

    Wholeness: don’t designate the whole first. Closed: “Why am I so anxious?” Open: “Treat my anxiety as something that happens between me and my surroundings: work, flat, people, money. When does it rise and fall, and what can’t be explained from my inner state alone?” This widens the picture and doesn’t replace treatment. If anxiety is getting in the way of your days, take it to a doctor or therapist too (Lesson 22).

    Mode of existence: judge it by its own logic. Closed: “Why is our volunteer group so disorganized?” Open: “What is a volunteer group organized around, and which of my workplace standards am I judging it by?” Find out what the thing is doing by its own logic before you score it by yours.

    One question per prompt. Notice how much the answer changes when only the question changed.

    For builders

    Process and the third also work on a team’s code review:

    Our code review, two of the six questions. Process: from the numbers below,
what has changed over the last quarter (queue length, time to first review,
by weekday)? The third: what does review produce between author and reviewer,
apart from caught defects? Use only what I paste, and mark what you infer.
[paste the pull-request export]

    Process may show that pull requests pile up on Fridays and that review time is growing week by week. The third may show that what review produces between author and reviewer is a shared understanding of the code, more than a count of caught defects. That changes what “faster review” should optimize.

    Lesson 24Structure behind events

    open Systems thinking has a short grammar. Stocks are things that accumulate: skill, trust, savings, debt, lost sleep, water in a tub. Flows fill and drain them. Delays separate causes from effects. Feedback loops are either reinforcing (more leads to more) or balancing (more leads to less). Structure produces behavior, so the same structure produces the same pattern no matter who is in it. mechanism

    The iceberg orders your questions. Events (what happened) sit on top of patterns (what has been happening over time). Patterns come from structures (stocks, flows, loops, rules, incentives), and structures come from mental models (beliefs that keep the structure in place). Ask a model “why did X happen?” and it answers at the event level, with a story. Ask for the pattern first: “Draw the behavior over time. What is rising, what is falling, since when?”

    Before
    Why did I break my diet again yesterday?

    Model Probably a stressful day, low blood sugar and snacks within reach. Try planning meals ahead and keeping trigger foods out of the house.

    An event question gets an event answer: a plausible story about one evening, plus generic advice.

    After
    I've broken my diet 6 times in 8 weeks. Before you explain anything, help me see the pattern: which days, what time, what happened in the 3 hours before. My log of meals, sleep and calendar: [paste]. What is rising, what is falling, and since when?

    Model 5 of the 6 breaks came on a Thursday or Friday after 9 p.m., all on days with a late meeting. Late meetings went from one a week to three from week 3, and in those weeks your sleep gets shorter each night from Monday on. Six breaks is a small sample, so read this as a lead: a sleep debt that builds through the week, more than one bad evening.

    Pattern first, with the log to see it in. Now a structure shows: a calendar that drains a stock of sleep, with a delay that lands on Thursday. The lever worth testing is the meetings, not willpower.

    open Your own fixes are part of the pattern. You have asked three times how to make your Spanish practice stick, and each answer was a new app. The question that was missing: what did the last change actually do? If each app lasted two weeks, the question was never which app.

    Three sentences are worth memorizing for conversations. Today’s problems come from yesterday’s solutions: the strict curfew that ended the late nights also ended the talks in the car on the way home. Cause and effect are not closely related in time and space: the burnout this spring started with the hiring freeze last autumn. And dividing an elephant in half does not produce two small elephants: split the housework into “yours” and “mine”, and the jobs that sat between the halves, like noticing that the bin is full, stop getting done.

    Where to push. open Places to intervene in a system run from weak to strong: numbers, buffers, physical structure, delays, feedback loops, information flows, rules, the power to self-organize, goals, paradigms. heuristic Most conversations stay on the bottom rung, tuning numbers. A household budget shows the ladder. Numbers: cut the coffee budget by 20 euros. Information flow: both partners see every purchase in one shared app. Rule: any purchase over 100 euros waits 48 hours. Goal: “save for a flat” becomes “be able to take three months off”. A good conversation asks at least once about information flows, rules or goals.

    When the number takes over touch

    A measure that the person being measured can influence drifts away from what it was measuring. Over time the score rises and the aim does not. That is proxy capture, and the regularity behind it is Goodhart’s law. It is a structural fact, not bad luck. Ten thousand steps a day becomes pacing the kitchen at 11 p.m. Grades become cramming the night before and forgetting within a week. A follower count becomes posts written to enrage. Inbox zero becomes archiving what you never read. Nobody has to cheat. The loop does it on its own.

    With a model, it happens fast. Ask for “an essay that will get a good grade” and you get the shape graders reward. Ask for a CV that scores well on a keyword checker and you get the keywords. Pair every number you optimize with a check you don’t optimize: the steps with how far you can walk without stopping, the grade with a question you answer cold a month later. When the goal of a whole conversation has been captured this way, that is a reason to challenge the goal (trigger 5 in Lesson 29).

    Say what the number stands for close

    Before you trust a measure, write the thing it stands for in one sentence that doesn’t mention the measure. “Reading level” stands for “can understand a newspaper article on an unfamiliar topic”. Then write one way the measure could rise while the thing does not: the child has seen the test passages before. If you can’t write one, you don’t yet know what you are measuring. heuristic

    A measure fails in two ways. It can miss part of the aim: grades miss curiosity, so grades can rise while curiosity stays where it was. Or it can include something else: a restaurant’s delivery rating includes how friendly the courier was, so it can rise while the food stays the same. With models, length, fluency and a confident tone are the usual “something else” when the aim is being right.

    Nobody has to cheat for a pushed measure to break. Luck at the top of the score is enough: whoever gave the best interview was partly the luckiest interviewee. So is pushing past the range where the link held: hours at the desk track work done in a normal week, and past a point more hours mean more mistakes. So is changing how the score gets produced: teaching to the test raises scores by a route that no longer builds the skill.

    G17
    Look for structure behind events. Ask for the pattern over time before the story, then for the stocks, flows, delays and loops that would produce it. Check every measure against the aim it stands for. Where no stable structure exists, probe instead of analyzing (Lesson 28).
    For builders

    Once you push on a metric, four different things can go wrong, and each maps onto evaluation work:

    • Noise (regressional Goodhart). The score is the aim plus luck, so the top of a leaderboard overstates true quality.
    • Out of range (extremal). The link held where it was observed and breaks where you push: a benchmark correlation that held on older models breaks at the frontier.
    • The path changes (causal). Pushing on the score changes how it is produced: training on the benchmark’s format.
    • Someone games it (adversarial). Another party optimizes the score against you: a seller who buys reviews, or an agent that hacks its reward. An agent told to “make the tests pass” that can also edit the test suite is gaming from the start, which is why a verifier the agent can rewrite is not a verifier (TC5).

    Before you bind any eval, write the sentence that says what its score stands for.

    TD9
    Pair every optimized metric with an unoptimized check. A score can part from its aim in four ways: noise, a push out of range, a changed path, gaming (regressional, extremal, causal, adversarial). The first three need nobody to cheat.

    Lesson 25One level at a time, and come back

    close Everything is at once a whole made of parts and a part of a larger whole. The word for that is holon. A cell, an organ, a body, a family, a town. A task, a working day, a job, a career. Every conversation about a system happens at some level. The common failure is sliding between levels without noticing. You ask why your child’s homework takes three hours, and twenty minutes later you are discussing whether the school system is broken. Or the reverse: you ask whether to change careers and end up comparing two fonts for your CV.

    The remedy fits in three plain sentences: “Keep this question in view.” “Now go into one part.” “Back to the question: what has changed?” One level per turn, and you always come back. Here it is on a family’s mornings.

    Worked example: “why are we always late in the morning?”

    You Keep this question in view: why are we late for school three mornings out of five? The morning runs: waking, bathroom, breakfast, bags, the walk. One level at a time. I’ll say when I go into a part and when I come back.

    Model Noted: why you are late three mornings out of five, at the level of the whole morning. Which part do you want to go into first?

    You Go into one part, the slowest: breakfast. The youngest’s takes 25 minutes.

    Model Does it start late or run long? Give me the start time on a late day and on an on-time day.

    The model asks for the timing instead of offering picky-eater tips. That is the part-level question.

    You It takes 25 minutes either way. On-time days it starts at 7:10, late days at 7:30. Now across: what links breakfast to the rest of the morning?

    Model I can’t see that from here. On a late day, what has to finish before breakfast can start?

    The model says what it doesn’t know. A guess would have sounded just as fluent.

    You One of us has to sit with her, and on the three days we both start work early, we’re both queueing for the bathroom until 7:30. Back to the question. What has changed?

    Model What changed on “why late three mornings in five”: this isn’t a slow-child problem or a discipline problem. It’s a relation problem: on those three days a bathroom queue sits in front of breakfast. Fix to try: move one shower to the evening.

    The question got answered at the level it was asked, after one trip in and one across.

    One step out was worth a look, to what the morning sits inside. The school moved its start from 8:15 to 8:00 this year, which is why a morning that used to work now runs late. Then back to the question.

    Five names for the steps
