    You don’t need the capitals in an ordinary chat. The commands and the card below are for a system you will come back to over several sittings, or a conversation where you keep getting lost.

    • PIN: write down the current question and level before moving.
    • IN: go into a part.
    • OUT: go to the container.
    • ACROSS: go to a relation between parts.
    • RETURN: come back to the pinned question with a delta, i.e. what changed. “Nothing changed” is a valid and useful delta.

    In the morning example, “keep this question in view” was PIN, breakfast was IN, “what links breakfast to the rest of the morning” was ACROSS, the school’s start time was OUT, and “back to the question” was RETURN. Move one level per turn, and always return.

    The holon zoom

    Write a question, PIN it, then move. The tool enforces the rules: one level at a time, and RETURN with a delta. Try breaking them to see the warnings.

    Level in focus:

    Pinned questions

      Log

        The holon card

        A written boundary is a temporary closure, not the destination. The closure is what makes a later opening possible: you can only go deep into something you have first marked off. The card doesn’t settle what the whole really is (wholeness, Lesson 23, stays open). It records where the boundary is this turn. 

        HOLON CARD
CONTAINER: [what this whole lives inside; what it constrains]
WHOLE: [the system in focus; its boundary this turn]
PARTS: [3–7, named]
RELATIONS: [the links that matter; what flows along them]
LEVEL IN FOCUS THIS TURN: [container | whole | part | relation]
PINNED QUESTION: [...]
DEPTH BUDGET: [e.g., one level in, then RETURN]

        Filled in for the late mornings:

        HOLON CARD
CONTAINER: school and two jobs; they fix when we have to leave
WHOLE: our weekday morning, from the first alarm to the front door
PARTS: waking, bathroom, breakfast, bags, the walk
RELATIONS: what has to finish before what
LEVEL IN FOCUS THIS TURN: whole
PINNED QUESTION: why are we late for school three mornings out of five?
DEPTH BUDGET: one level in, then RETURN

        Three axes of “deeper”

        open Part–whole scale is one axis (the holon). Depth of explanation is another (the iceberg: event → pattern → structure → mental model). Time is the third (the process: phase, trend, history). They are independent. When a conversation feels shallow, name which axis is missing. In the morning example, IN and ACROSS moved along scale. “Has it been like this all year, or only since September?” moves along time. “What do we believe about mornings that keeps two showers in front of breakfast?” moves along depth.

        G18
        Move one level at a time, and come back. Pin the question, move one level (in, out, or across), and return with a delta. Name which axis you are moving on: scale, depth, or time.
        For builders

        The same steps on “why is our app slow at launch?” PIN at the whole (the app). IN to a part, the API client: it fires nine requests at launch. ACROSS to the relation between client and backend: the requests are serial, each waiting for the previous one, at about 180 ms each on mobile networks. RETURN: “Delta: not a UI problem, not a server problem. A relation problem: nine serial round-trips. Fix: batch or parallelize, not optimize either end.” One OUT: users on slow networks in some markets make it worse, which raises the priority. Then RETURN.

        Lesson 26Contradictions as information

        open close A contradiction is information. While you are opening a question, it marks the place where the picture hasn’t finished forming, and it is worth holding before you resolve it. While you are deciding, it is a signal to stop and look. Both are true, so the first job is to know which kind of contradiction you are holding. Three are common. A fourth, the polarity, looks like a design tension and is handled differently (below).

        1. An error. One side is false. What to do: bind both claims, which means checking each against a source (Lesson 18), and keep the survivor. Two sites give different opening hours for the museum. The museum’s own page settles it.
        2. A boundary marker. Both sides are true, in different contexts: different people, amounts, time horizons or definitions. Each is true in some respect. What to do: find the boundary and name it, and the contradiction turns into a map. “Coffee is good for you” and “coffee is bad for you” both have support, at different amounts and for different people (pregnancy, anxiety, poor sleep).
        3. A design tension. You want both, and they limit each other. What to do: invent a separation (below), or choose explicitly and write down what you gave up. A big flat and a short commute, on one salary.

        close One test before you separate a tension: has it stayed in words? Try to place it in something concrete, such as a person, a thing, a tool, a rule, or who does what. “We value both quality and speed” can’t be placed. “Marta reviews everything, and she is the only reviewer” can, and a separation needs the placed version to work on.

        Separate the contradiction close

        Inventive solutions resolve contradictions instead of compromising on them. That is the core finding of TRIZ, a method built from studying tens of thousands of patents. heuristic Its four separation principles work far beyond engineering. Take a flat where one person needs quiet to study and two children need to be loud:

        • In time: one property now, the opposite later. One quiet hour after school, from 5 to 6, and loud for the rest of the evening.
        • In space: one property here, the opposite there. A study corner with a door, and the living room stays loud.
        • On condition: behavior depends on circumstances. A sign on the door means “don’t interrupt unless it’s urgent”. No sign, and anyone can walk in.
        • Between parts and whole: a property of the whole that isn’t a property of its parts. A bicycle chain is rigid in each link and flexible as a whole. Here the flat stays loud as a whole while one person in it hears nothing: noise-cancelling headphones.

        Worked example: “save for a flat” vs. “travel this year”

        Is it an error? Partly. The plan assumed that travel means two expensive trips in high season. Have the model list what the plan assumes, then price the same trips off-season yourself, on the booking sites, and part of the conflict disappears.

        Is it a boundary marker? Yes. Over five years the flat dominates. Over this year, two trips with old friends may not come again.

        Is it a design tension? Within this year’s budget, yes. Separate it in time (travel after three months of savings are in the bank), on condition (the second trip happens only if the bonus arrives), and between parts and whole (the year saves as a whole even if the travel month doesn’t).

        What was a fight with yourself becomes three decisions.

        CONTRADICTION TRIAGE
A: [claim/desire 1]       B: [claim/desire 2]
1) Could one be simply false? What check would tell? (error)
2) In which contexts is A true, and in which B? Name the boundary. (boundary marker)
3) If we want both: propose a separation in time, in space, on condition,
   and between parts and whole. Rate each for my case.
Don't average them. Don't say "it depends" without saying on what.
        Caution: “both-and”

        “Both-and” resolves a contradiction only if you can say where, when and for whom each side holds. Otherwise it is mush, a costume of wisdom. The test is whether the both-and changes an action. “Coffee is both good and bad for you” changes nothing. “Two cups before noon is fine for most adults; if you’re pregnant, no more than 200 mg of caffeine a day, about two cups of instant” does. Use this test when you close. While you are still opening, a hedge may be one of the five signs of a fertile tension (Lesson 14). Don’t resolve that one just to make it tidy. Probe it.

        A fourth kind: a polarity close

        Some pairs are poles you keep moving between, and no solution ends the movement: inhale and exhale, stability and change, closeness and independence in a couple. Check two things before you call something a polarity: the poles depend on each other, and the difficulty never ends. heuristic Each pole has an upside, and a downside when you over-focus on it.

        The product is a map of four boxes: each pole’s upside and its downside. Add one early warning for each downside, one action that secures each upside while the other pole stays alive, and a purpose that is neither upside. For closeness and independence: closeness gives support, and too much of it smothers (warning: one of you stops seeing friends). Independence gives growth, and too much of it leaves you drifting apart (warning: you learn about each other’s week from social media). Actions: one protected evening together each week, and one evening each that is yours alone. Purpose: a relationship you would both choose again.

        Reject an answer that praises both poles without a downside and a warning. Don’t apply this to a factual contradiction, or to “the model is right and the model is wrong”. If the pair fails the two checks, go back to error, boundary or tension.

        Past either-or, then an honest close

        open Sometimes two positions combine into something new in kind, which is different from a compromise. The test for that is below.

        Catch the cheap version first. “Stay in the job or quit?”, the question from Lesson 23, is a false binary. Ask for the continuum, the hybrids, and the dimensions that appear once it is no longer binary: go part-time; move to another team; take three months’ leave; stay, and start the other thing on Fridays.

        close Then close honestly. A combination of two partial views is a real synthesis only when all five of these hold: heuristic

        1. It’s the right kind of opposition. If it’s a polarity, the product is the map, not a merge. If the dispute is about what the problem even is, and the choice affects people, the product is a recorded choice (Lesson 28).
        2. Both views survive, changed. Each original view is still visible inside the new statement, with its sense changed, and the new statement couldn’t have been written without both. If both survive only because each was softened, it is the middle way (Lesson 21).
        3. It states a relation. “X matters when…, Y matters when…, they interact by…, and that stops holding when…”. Another consideration is not a relation, and neither is “a more nuanced view”.
        4. It predicts something new. The combined picture has a checkable consequence that neither view had. If it only explains the two views, it is a summary.
        5. It isn’t a yes-or-no question in disguise. If the two views are competing answers to one question that can be settled, don’t synthesize. Find out which one is right.

        An example that passes. Your teacher says to outline before you write. A friend who writes for a living says to just start and find out what you think. A synthesis: drafting is how you find the claim, and outlining is how you test it. Free writing matters until you can state the claim in one sentence, the outline matters from then on, the two meet in that sentence, and none of it holds for a text whose form is fixed in advance, such as a lab report. Both views survive, changed: the draft is now a search with a stopping point, and the outline is now a test of the claim, where it used to be a plan. It predicts something neither said: an outline made before you can state the claim gets abandoned by page two, and one made after a rough draft holds. Your next two essays can show whether it does. The first and last conditions hold as well: the tension ends when the essay is finished, so it is no polarity, and no source could settle which of the two is right.

        The elephant, as this test: two partial reports describe one animal when a single picture produces both reports, says where each one stops being true, and predicts one more thing neither reporter said. A sentence both reporters would nod at is not the elephant yet.

        G19
        Treat contradictions as information. Classify each one: an error (bind it), a boundary marker (map it), or a design tension (separate it in time, in space, on condition or between parts and whole, or choose explicitly). Never average them away.
        For builders

        The separations in software. In time: a fast prototype first and a hardening phase after. In space: strict rules in the payment core and loose ones in experiments. On condition: a feature flag that is on for staff and off for customers.

        “Ship fast” vs. “keep the code clean”, triaged. Error? Partly. Large-scale research on software delivery finds that speed and stability go together: top performers deploy more often and also fail less. evidence Boundary marker? Yes. Speed dominates a two-week prototype, and cleanliness dominates the billing system. Design tension? Within one module this week, yes. Separate it in space (strict core, loose edges), in time (spike, then harden), and on condition (feature flags, so unfinished code ships dark).

        Three filters for a technical contradiction, beyond the four separations:

        • State it in technical form first: “If we improve A by the obvious means, B gets worse.” A and B are properties of the artifact, not values. If you can’t fill in both, there is no contradiction yet.
        • List the resources: what the system and its surroundings already contain that goes unused. The first designs spend from that list.
        • The ideal final result, as a filter: the useful function happens, and the thing causing the harm is gone or was never added. Reject a proposal that adds a subsystem to cancel a harm the design itself introduced.

        These don’t apply to a polarity, or to a problem whose formulation is itself the dispute.

        Lesson 27Generating the new

        open Closing stops bad answers. It doesn’t create good ones. For that you have to open, and you need to know why models resist it. Preference training pushes models toward typical answers, the ones raters find familiar, and the result is a narrowing called mode collapse. evidence Ask for “a birthday present for my dad” and you get the most typical present: a watch, an engraved pen, a nice whisky. That most typical answer is the mode.

        Four generators

        1. Ask for the distribution, not the mode. Ask for several answers with rough probabilities instead of one answer, and a model’s ideas come back markedly more varied. evidence Ask for the tails explicitly: “at least two below 10%”. The numbers aren’t forecasts. They are there to force a spread, so don’t choose by them. Use this for ideas and options. A factual question needs one answer and a check (Lesson 18).

        Before
        My dad turns 60. He's retired, restores old bikes, hates clutter, and misses his brother, who lives abroad. What should I get him?

        Model A weekend with his brother would mean the most, flights paid if you can. If that’s out of reach, a hard-to-find part for the bike he’s restoring.

        A good answer, and the one you would have thought of yourself. It is the mode for these facts.

        After
        My dad turns 60. He's retired, restores old bikes, hates clutter, and misses his brother, who lives abroad. Give 6 substantively different gift ideas, each with a rough probability that it's the best one for him. At least 2 below 0.10. For each: the idea in one line and what it assumes about him.

        Model 1) A weekend with his brother, flights paid (0.30; assumes the brother can travel). 2) A hard-to-find part for the bike he’s restoring (0.20). … 5) One old bike restored by the two of them: his brother does the wheels abroad, he does the frame, with parts in the post and a video call on Sundays (0.07; assumes his brother wants a project). 6) A year of Saturday mornings from you, helping with the bikes (0.05; assumes he wants company while he works).

        The same facts, so the only change is the demand for a spread. The tails join what the first answer kept apart: the bikes and the brother.

        Give 6 substantively different answers to [QUESTION], each with a rough probability
that it's the best answer for my case. At least 2 must be below 0.10.
For each: the core idea in one line, what it assumes, and what would make it the winner.
Different means a different mechanism, not a different wording.

        2. Collide two frames, then find where the analogy breaks. New ideas often come from putting a problem into a frame it never normally meets. Ask about a child starting a new school as an organ transplant. What has to stay alive in transit? What will the new body reject? What keeps the rejection down in the first months? A good analogy maps relations, not surface features, so ask where this one breaks. It breaks on consent: an organ has no say, and a child does. The break shows what the borrowed frame leaves out, here her own part in the move, so add it to the plan. Keep a frame only if it gives you a question that changes what you do. heuristic

        3. Polyphony. A list of “several perspectives” is one author in several hats. Polyphony means independent voices that answer each other, none merged into the author’s. Keep their disagreements, and don’t let a narrator reconcile them.

        Convene 4 voices on [QUESTION]: [e.g., me, my partner, my mother who would move
in with us, a social worker who has seen many such moves]. Each speaks in turn
and must respond to the previous speaker's strongest point, not repeat their own.
Two rounds. Then list the disagreements that remain. Do not resolve them;
don't add a narrator.

        Afterwards, check whether one voice used another’s concession or walked around it (Lesson 14). If every voice hedges in the same places, there is one author, so give one voice a commitment it can’t drop (“my mother will not sell her flat”). A discussion also tends to circulate what everyone already knows. The one fact that only one person holds, and that would reverse the choice, often never comes up. evidence Four friends pick a flat for its sea view. One knows about building work next door, one that the last bus leaves at six, one that the “bedrooms” are sofa beds. Each fact looks minor, nobody raises it, and the flat wins. Voices in one chat share one memory, so give each voice facts the others lack, or run each in its own fresh chat, memory off. The voices are guesses about real people. Take what they still dispute to the people themselves. heuristic

        4. Invert, and change the representation. “Invert, always invert”: ask how to guarantee failure, then avoid it. “How would I make sure I never learn Spanish?” Study only on weekends, never speak it, switch apps every month. Part of that list usually describes what you are doing now. Change the representation too: ask for the problem as a drawing, a table or a timeline. A new representation often shows what the old one hid. evidence An overloaded week, laid out as a timetable instead of a to-do list, can turn out to be two clashes on Thursday. Shifting a constraint does similar work: plan the birthday party “with no budget at all” and you see which parts the children would miss.

        The draft on the screen is your default

        close Whatever is already in front of you has an advantage. People keep the option they already have, and its edge over each rival grows as the alternatives multiply. evidence A model’s first answer probably works the same way. hypothesis People also stop searching at the first option that clears an unstated bar. If you haven’t set the bar before the options appear, it drops to whatever the first fluent option cleared.

        So set it first. Before the model shows you flats, write your three deal-breakers. Before it shows you a plan, write what would make you reject it. Then check the answer against the list, not against your mood. Late in a long session, re-read the bar or stop. Better still, judge in a different turn from the one that generated (Lesson 13), in a fresh chat, after a pause: setting a problem aside after real work on it helps. evidence So work the question first, then give it a night (Lesson 10).

        When the ideas all look alike

        Sometimes you ask for the distribution and get five versions of one idea. Asking again won’t fix it. Change the structure of the request.

        A grid of options. open Pick two axes that have nothing to do with quality, such as effort and familiarity for a dinner menu, and ask for one best idea per cell. That is the smallest grid, and it is often enough. For a bigger plan, name about four dimensions and three values for each before any ideas, and lock them. A family weekend: where (home, city, nature), who (everyone, two teams, one child with each parent), what it turns on (food, a task, strangers), how long (an evening, a day, two days). Have the model drop the combinations that can’t work and say why, then read the survivors that aren’t the obvious one. “City, two teams, strangers, a day” becomes a game in which each team has until dinner to bring back one true story from someone they have never met. The grid can’t find a dimension nobody named, so ask the model which ones are missing before you lock it. heuristic

        Writing the open request

        open Depth changes the kind of work the answer does, and asking for more length doesn’t. “Which phone has the longest battery life?” asks the model to look something up. “How do battery, weight and camera trade off?” asks it to relate things. “I’m on a train four days a week and hate carrying a charger: what am I really choosing between?” asks it to weigh unlike things against each other. When an answer comes back shallow, check which kind you asked for. heuristic

        One more thing an open request can do is question its own frame, and it is the least used. “7 tips to get my 10-year-old to read more” has decided the number, that the answer is a tip, and that the goal is “more”, all before the model speaks. The open version: “What if not reading is information? What does my son get from what he does instead, and what would reading have to offer to compete?”

        Staying in the unsettled state

        open Culture treats uncertainty as a fault to fix at once. The poet John Keats named the opposite skill negative capability: being able to stay “in uncertainties, mysteries, doubts, without any irritable reaching after fact and reason”. In a conversation, the productive version is the stretch in which your usual categories have been set down and new ones haven’t formed yet. The generators are ways into it, and the five signs in Lesson 14 help you recognize it.

        Probe it. Ask where the reasoning was least sure, where a concept it used becomes unclear, and what disappears under another frame. Ask it to stay with a contradiction in its own answer instead of repairing it. The replies are a second look at the question. They don’t report the model’s inner state (Lesson 3). When something holds, take it as a foothold, a temporary stable point, and build the next question on it. When you close, use the five checks in Lesson 26. All of this takes several turns, and Lesson 29 shows how to run them. The walk-through shows one pass.

        Walk-through: identity online

        You I keep meaning to delete Instagram because I feel fake on it. Don’t give me pros and cons. Give me three unusual pictures of what an online identity is, one line each.

        Model A costume you change between scenes. A shop window that other people keep rearranging. A palimpsest: a page written over many times, with the earlier layers showing through and none of them the original.

        The far frames come from asking for them. Pick the one that makes you stop.

        You The palimpsest. What changes if no layer is the real one?

        Model Then “fake” needs a second look. It assumes a real you behind the profile, and a palimpsest has no behind, only layers: old posts, the self you are at work, the one in the group chat. That pulls against the common belief in one true self that the others distort.

        A fertile tension: “fake” rested on a premise nobody had stated. Don’t resolve it. Probe it.

        You Stay with that tension. What follows if the “true self” is itself a cultural idea? How have other traditions related the different sides of a person?

        Model Some treat a person as made of roles and relationships, with nothing hidden behind them. On that view authenticity is the coherence of each way you show up, and there is no inner core to match.

        A foothold: “authenticity as coherence within each context”. It is temporary, and it is enough to build on.

        You Build on it. What if identity is the acts themselves? Then “delete it so I don’t lose my real self” falls apart, doesn’t it?

        Model The reason falls apart. The advice may not. If you are what you keep doing, a post is practice at being that person, which is a stronger reason for care. The question stops being “is this the real me?” and becomes “which of these acts do I want more of?”

        A leading question, and the model didn’t follow it. The question changed instead.

        You Then I’d rather check than decide. How would I find out?

        Model Sort your last 20 posts into two piles: what you’d want to be doing more of in a year, and what you wouldn’t. If the second pile is the big one, change those habits. That may or may not mean deleting the app, and only you can do the sorting.

        The ideas are old. What’s new is yours: a question you couldn’t have asked at the start, and a check you can run tonight.

        For builders

        A distributional prompt is the diversity tool for open-ended generation. Reasoning effort and a prestige persona don’t do that job, and the Claude 5.5 series no longer accepts a temperature setting at all. For a hard reasoning problem you want independent samples and a checker.

        Two more structures for design work, when the candidates are near-copies. Concept and knowledge: one column holds a concept that is neither true nor false yet (“a checkout nobody abandons”), the other what you know (where users drop out, what the payment provider allows). Facts sharpen the concept, the concept sends you for facts you lack, and you stop when it is something you can test. Value, frame, thing: name an outcome you could observe, one working principle that would produce it, one thing that carries out the principle, the consequence you expect, and the test. For the checkout: fewer people leave at the payment step (value); they leave when a cost appears late (frame); show the full price, shipping included, on the product page (thing); drop-off at payment falls (consequence); a two-week A/B test (test). If it fails, change the frame or the thing, and say which. heuristic

        TA9
        Ask for the distribution. For open-ended generation, not as a factual-accuracy trick. If the answers are near-copies, fix the axes or a grid of options before asking for more.

        Lesson 28What kind of problem is this?

        close The right way to converse depends on what kind of situation you are in. The Cynefin framework (say “kuh-NEV-in”; the word is Welsh for “habitat”) sorts situations by how cause and effect relate.

        Domain Everyday case Cause and effect Approach How to converse with a model
        Clear Descaling a kettle Obvious to anyone Sense → categorize → respond Ask for the standard procedure; check it against the manual
        Complicated Should I refinance my mortgage? Knowable by analysis or expertise Sense → analyze → respond Ask for the analysis from several standpoints, and for the mechanism; check with a human expert
        Complex How do I get my teenager to talk to me more? Visible only in hindsight; the situation shifts as you act Probe → sense → respond Don’t ask for the answer. Ask for safe-to-fail experiments (small trials you can afford to lose), signals to watch, and what would amplify or dampen
        Chaotic The hour after a fire in your building: you are on the street at 2 a.m. with the children No discernible relationship Act → sense → respond Stabilize first (a checklist for the next hour), analyze later
        Confused “Should we move abroad?” The visa rules are clear, the money is complicated, how the children settle is complex You don’t know which domain Break it apart “Split this problem into parts and classify each part by domain”

        The costliest mistake is treating a complex problem as merely complicated: asking for “the answer” when nobody could know it in advance. The model will produce an answer anyway, in an expert’s confident voice. heuristic “Should I refinance my mortgage?” is complicated: the answer exists, and the numbers and an adviser’s check can find it. “How do I get my teenager to talk to me more?” (the son from Lesson 8 again) is complex: a person who adapts to whatever you try. It needs probes.

        touch In a complex situation, probing is how you touch the world. You learn from what the situation does, not from what the model says.

        Before
        My 15-year-old son has gone quiet with me over the last 6 months. He takes the bus to football practice three evenings a week. How do I get him to talk to me more?

        Model Here are 8 strategies. 1. Schedule weekly one-on-one time. 2. Ask open-ended questions. 3. Listen without judgment. 4. Show interest in his football…

        A complex problem answered as if it were complicated: a confident list, as if one of these must work.

        After
        This looks like a complex problem: my 15-year-old son has gone quiet with me over the last 6 months. He takes the bus to football practice three evenings a week. Don't propose a solution. Propose 4 safe-to-fail probes. For each: what I'd try, at what small scale, for how long; the signal to amplify it; the signal to stop; what it would teach me even if it fails.

        Model 1) Drive him to practice twice a week instead of the bus, no agenda, for two weeks. Amplify if he starts topics himself; stop if the headphones go on. Teaches whether side-by-side time works better than face-to-face. 2) Ask for his help with something he knows better than you (a game, a phone setting). …

        The same facts, and a different kind of answer asked for: small experiments with signals. What you learn will come from his reactions.

        This looks like a complex problem: [PROBLEM].
Don't propose a solution. Propose 4 safe-to-fail probes. For each:
- what we'd try, at what small scale, for how long
- the signal that would tell us to amplify it
- the signal that would tell us to dampen or stop it
- what it would teach us even if it fails

        When people disagree about what the problem is

        Describe the situation twice. open When a situation is messy and people disagree about what the problem even is, don’t start from “the problem”. The word smuggles in the solution that would end it. Start from the situation: who is worried, and what they actually do. Then write two or three short descriptions of what the activity is for, each from a different worldview. For each one: who benefits or suffers, who acts, what gets transformed, who could stop it, and what is taken as given. If you can’t name who it is for, the description is a wish.

        Grandmother has fallen twice this year. Her son wants a care home, her daughter phones twice a day, and she hid the second fall from both. Description one: “a family arrangement that keeps her safe”. It turns risk into safety, takes as given that safety comes first, and she can stop it by refusing. Description two: “a family arrangement that keeps her the head of her own life”. It turns dependence into chosen support, takes as given that her say comes first, and her son can stop it by insisting. Compare each with what is happening now. Under the first, the hidden fall is a gap in the monitoring. Under the second, it is a sign she has already lost her say, and more phone calls will make it worse. Left to itself, a model writes one bland description and calls it the situation, so ask for two that disagree. heuristic

        Wicked, as a diagnosis. close If a check could settle it, the problem is tame, and calling it “wicked” is an excuse. Why the boiler keeps losing pressure is tame. Whether your town should allow a supermarket on the old football ground may be wicked. A wicked problem has no definitive formulation, because how you state it depends on where you think the solution lies (“traffic”, “jobs”, “the heart of the community”). It has no stopping rule of its own, and its solutions are better or worse for different people, never true or false. Write at least two formulations, each pointing to a different response. Don’t merge them. Choose one, and write the stopping condition as a resource limit (“two council meetings and one survey”), not as “when we understand it”. Keep the other formulation in your notes. heuristic

        Who is this plan for? open For a plan that affects other people, ask six questions twice: once as what is, once as what ought to be. Who benefits? Who decides? Whose expertise is treated as enough? Who is affected but not involved? What counts as improvement? What would show that the expertise had failed? Take a plan to make your street car-free. As it is: the residents’ association decides, the council’s traffic engineer is the expertise, and the corner shop that needs deliveries isn’t in the room. As it ought to be: the shop and the disabled neighbor who needs a taxi to the door help decide, what they know counts as expertise, and improvement means more than lower traffic counts. If the two sets of answers come back as the same paragraph, the boundary wasn’t really questioned. Empty answers are findings. The holon card (Lesson 25) draws a boundary, and these questions are how you justify it or refuse it. They don’t choose the boundary for you. heuristic

        Which tool when

        The tools of Lessons 23–28 answer different situations. Find the line that matches what you notice, and use that one tool before you reach for another.

        You notice… Reach for Lesson
        The picture closed early (“it’s a scheduling problem”) open One or two of the six questions, chosen for what the picture lacks 23
        Each answer is a story about the latest incident, plus generic fixes open The pattern over time, before any story: what is rising, what is falling, since when 24
        A number has become the goal close Say what the number stands for. touch Pair it with a check you don’t optimize 24
        You started with the homework and are now on the school system close Keep the question in view, go one level, come back and say what changed 25
        Two things you believe, or two things you want, collide close Sort it before you resolve it: an error (check both sides), a boundary marker (name where each holds) or a design tension (separate it, for example in time or in space, or choose). A pair that never settles is a polarity, and you map it 26
        Every idea is the one you’d have had anyway open Six answers with rough probabilities, two of them unlikely; then a far frame 27
        Nobody could know the answer in advance touch Small experiments, each with a signal to do more and a signal to stop 28
        People disagree about what the problem is open Two descriptions of the situation that disagree, each from a different worldview 28

        When a question matters enough to need several of these, Lesson 31 runs them in one order.

        For builders

        Before a long agent run: only variety can destroy variety. List the distinguishable situations the agent may meet that would need a different response. List the distinguishable responses actually in its context: rules, examples, tools, checks. An agent migrating a database can meet a failed step, a locked table and a schema that differs from the docs. If its context holds one rule, “retry”, it has one response for three situations. Where the situations outnumber the responses, name the missing responses, or the buffer that makes them unnecessary. A test or a type-checker is a buffer. “Be careful” is not a response, and extra words can add zero variety. Don’t inflate the prompt to cover cases a checker already catches. Matching the count is necessary, not sufficient: the context should also contain a model of the disturbances, not only a goal. heuristic

        Part VI

        Method: running the loop

        How a conversation that lasts longer than one answer stays on course and can still change course: write the state, notice drift, brake on goal changes, and match the ceremony to the stakes.

        Lesson 29Running the loop

        Lesson 5 named the rhythm: open, close, touch, and you are the one who switches. This lesson runs it across a conversation that lasts longer than a question and an answer.

        A conversation that goes well keeps making all three moves. It opens: options and contradictions go on the table. It closes: a boundary is drawn and a choice is written down. It touches: something that can say no gets checked, usually a number, a document, a person or a test, and sometimes you, with the chat closed. Each cycle starts from a better-tested place than the last. A conversation goes badly when it gets stuck in one move.

        Four tools keep the loop honest: the state block (what is established so far), parking (for side ideas), the brake (for challenges to the goal) and ceremony (effort matched to stakes). heuristic

        The loop in one conversation

        Here is the whole loop in one conversation, before any of its parts is explained. Watch for a belief written down as a hypothesis, a side idea parked, a fact checked outside the chat, and the moment the goal itself comes into question. There the model names its reason, called a trigger, and waits for one of three answers: keep the goal, revise it, or split the new question off into its own conversation. The sections after the conversation take these one at a time. If you have ten minutes, read this conversation, the five lines of the state block and the ceremony table.

        You I’m 34, an accountant for nine years, and I keep thinking about switching to UX design. No pep talk and no pros-and-cons table. Give me five genuinely different ways this could go for someone like me, with a rough probability each, including at least one I’d hate to hear.

        Open. The request rules out the two typical answers and asks for a spread, unwelcome tail included.

        Model Rough guesses, not statistics. (1) Bootcamp, then a junior UX job within a year, on junior pay: 20%. (2) A hybrid, a product or analyst role at a finance-software company: 30%. (3) UX on the side, and you find you prefer the research to the design: 15%. (4) You retrain into a crowded junior market and apply for six months: 25%. (5) You stay, and the restlessness was about your firm, not your field: 10%.

        You Four is the one I’d hate, and five stung. Log “it’s the field, not the firm” as P1, unchecked. Park “go freelance instead”: write it down, don’t discuss it. A boundary for now: only paths where I don’t drop below €45k a year. What survives?

        Close. Your belief goes in as a hypothesis (P1, the first premise), the side idea is parked, and the boundary is said out loud as temporary.

        Model (3) and (5) survive, because you keep your salary. The other three depend on what these roles pay where you live, which I don’t know. That is the deciding unknown.

        You That’s a number I can check. I’ll go through real listings tonight.

        Touch. The deciding fact gets checked outside the chat.

        You Back. 22 listings in Porto. Junior UX: €24–32k. Product roles at fintechs that ask for a finance background: €38–50k. Three are pasted below. If these numbers change the goal itself, don’t plan around it: name the trigger, show what we’d do under each goal, and wait for keep, revise or split.

        The raw result, not a summary. The last sentence asks for the brake in advance.

        Model Trigger: contradiction. “Become a UX designer” and “not below €45k” can’t both hold on these listings: junior UX tops out at €32k, and the product roles clear your minimum only at the top of their range. Keep the UX goal, and the next step is deciding how far the minimum can move. Change the goal to product work that uses your finance background, and the next step is reading what the roles above €45k ask for. Keep, revise or split? I won’t start on either until you answer.

        The brake. The trigger is named, the next step is shown under both goals, and nothing proceeds until you answer.

        You Revise. The goal is now “product work that uses what I know about finance, not below €45k”. Update the state.

        Model GOAL: product work that uses my finance background, not below €45k. PREMISES: P1 it’s the field, not the firm (unchecked) · P2 I’d enjoy product work day to day (unchecked). COMMITMENTS: product route first · rejected: bootcamp, then junior UX (below the minimum) · reopens if I lower the minimum. CLAIMS: C1 junior UX €24–32k, fintech product €38–50k · bound (my 22 listings, Porto). PARKED: go freelance instead. OUTER LOG: contradiction · revise · today.

        The state block: a few lines that say where the conversation stands. C1 is bound because you checked it, and the last line logs the challenge and your answer. The cycle closes on questions you couldn’t have asked at the start: what do the roles above €45k ask for, and who can tell you what a product person at a fintech does all day?

        You Give me five questions I should be able to answer without you if I’ve understood where this stands. Don’t answer them.

        Touch, turned inward. Close the chat and answer them on paper. What you can’t answer goes into the first message of the next session.

        Three things every good cycle has

        1. A temporary boundary is what makes a deeper opening possible. close

        A question with no edge gets the typical answer for the typical asker. Drawing an edge feels like narrowing, and it is: “only the next two years”, “we’re not discussing the budget yet”. It is also what lets the next opening go deep instead of wide. Say the boundary is temporary when you draw it. Moving it later is then an ordinary decision.

        Example: a kitchen renovation. “What should I do with my kitchen?” returns a catalog of trends. “Assume the plumbing stays where it is and the budget is €15k. Inside that, what are the genuinely different layouts?” returns options you can compare. Later you can ask what moving the sink would unlock, as a deliberate reopening.

        2. Held tension is what produces the leap. open

        Inside any boundary, things start to pull against each other: what you want against what you can afford, a plan that is good for you and hard on someone you care about. The temptation is to settle it at once, by picking a side or splitting the difference. Keep the tension on the table for a few turns instead, because that is where a reframe tends to come from: the problem turns out to be a different problem. hypothesis A model will resolve any tension in one fluent paragraph if you let it, so the holding is yours: ask it to state both sides and not to reconcile them yet. Lesson 26 has the closing half.

        Example: “I want more time with my kids” and “I want the promotion” look like a choice. Held for a few turns (what does the promotion change about hours, which hours, for how long?), it can become a question about which year or which kind of time, a different decision from the one you walked in with.

        3. A cycle is finished when you have questions you could not have asked at the start. touch

        At the end of a good cycle, something has been checked, something has been decided or deliberately left open, and your next question would have made no sense in your first message. If it could have been your first question, the cycle didn’t turn.

        Example: you started with “Should I learn Spanish or Portuguese?” Asked what you wanted it for, you found the real reason: your partner’s grandmother in Recife, who speaks no English. One phone call with her showed that you need to follow fast speech long before you read a word. Your next question is “What does a month of practice look like if the only goal is following her on the phone?” At the start you didn’t know the language was settled, or that the skill was listening.

        The last check points at you. Before you call a cycle finished, close the chat and say where you have got to, in two sentences, to someone who wasn’t there. Or have the model write five questions you should now be able to answer, and answer them without it (the conversation above ends this way, and the Close prompt on the Session Card below asks for them). Where you get stuck is where the next cycle starts. Scale this to the stakes: skip it for a recipe, and write it down before you sign a lease.

        Most conversations are one breath

        A quick question opens, closes and touches in a single turn: ask, get an answer, check the one fact that matters. The loop is mostly diagnostic. When a conversation feels stuck, ask which move you are making and whether it is the one the moment needs. Two mistakes are common.

        • Checking while you should be opening. You asked for ideas for a friend’s 40th birthday, and you fact-check the first suggestion’s price before the other nine exist. Judging early kills the options you haven’t seen.
        • Opening while you should be closing. You have collected good ideas for a month and still haven’t booked anything. Opening feels like progress and has no natural end. Pick the criteria, choose, and write down what you rejected.

        Continue or restart?

        A long conversation is worth continuing while later turns keep moving its center: the question you are actually asking and the frame you are asking it in. Then each exchange turns up connections a single pass would miss and changes what your first words meant. A single message, however careful, is a glance at a river: it catches the color and the current and misses the river as a process.

        It stops being worth continuing when every new turn builds on the first frame. The early assumptions harden, and your corrections get patched onto the old plan instead of changing it. Models do markedly worse when a task arrives in pieces than when they get the whole picture at once. evidence

        The test: can the thread still let go of its first frame? If not, don’t argue with it. Write the state block described next, open a fresh chat, paste the block, and continue. The restart keeps what the conversation learned and drops the frame it couldn’t leave.

        Example: forty turns into planning a wedding for 150, you cut the list to 60 and say the point is now one long dinner where people can talk. The model agrees, then offers a smaller band, a smaller buffet and a shorter running order. It took the number and kept the old wedding. A fourth correction won’t change that. Restart, with “60 guests, one long dinner, conversation over spectacle” in the state block.

        The state block close

        If nothing is written down, what you have is a transcript. A transcript feels like a record, but a long thread keeps building on its early assumptions and rarely walks them back (Lesson 2). The state block is a short note you own. It says what the conversation has established, and you paste it at the top of a message when the thread gets long, the stakes go up, or you restart in a fresh chat.

        Five lines cover most conversations. The labels are the ones the Session Card below prints.

        • GOAL, USE, DONE WHEN: what you are trying to do, what the answer is for, and how you will know you are done.
        • PREMISES: what you believe going in and haven’t checked, your own hopes included, written as hypotheses. “P1: I can find work in Lyon within six months (unchecked).”
        • CLAIMS: what the conversation has turned up that your decision rests on. A claim is unbound until it has been checked and bound after (Lesson 18), and the line says where it was checked or how it could be. “C2: family flats €1,050–1,250 · bound (5 listings, 12 Nov).”
        • COMMITMENTS: each choice made, with the alternative you rejected and what would reopen it.
        • PARKED: good side ideas that are not this conversation’s job.

        The block is authoritative until you change it on purpose. Tell the model so: “Use this state, not your memory of the thread.” Then have it restate the goal and DONE WHEN in two sentences before anything else (the card’s Open prompt asks for this). A model will sometimes widen a goal: “decide about the job offer” comes back as “plan our life in France”. The restatement is where you catch it.

        The card below has more fields than these five. Fill the five and leave the rest blank. The others are for higher stakes and are explained under the card.

        Session Card

        Fill in what you know; leave the rest blank. The card saves in this browser and writes three prompts: Open (start a session), Turn (paste with a turn when the thread is long), and Close (end honestly). Private notes are never included in what you copy. 

        
            
        

            
        Out of scope and Loss (which mistake would be worse, and what can’t
              be undone) sharpen the goal, which the card’s prompts shorten to G.
              Holon is the container, the whole and the part in focus (Lesson 25).
              Mode says what this stretch is for: explore opens, converge and commit close, check is
              where you touch. Ceremony is the level of effort, 0 to 2, set out under Ceremony below.
              Definitions are locked words (Lesson 12). In Claims,
              sense is the kind of true you mean (Lesson 17) and status is bound or unbound.
              Contradictions are logged, not smoothed over. Predictions say what you
              expect to see, and by when. Outer log records each challenge to the goal and your answer.
              Private notes hold what you hope to hear, your Double (Lesson 8).
        

            
        A filled block
        
            
        === STATE (authoritative: use this, not the vibe of the thread) ===
GOAL (G): Decide whether we move to Lyon for Ana's job offer.
USE: Our answer to her employer, due 21 Nov.
DONE WHEN: We know whether we can afford it for 6 months, what happens
  to the kids' school year, and we have a yes/no with the one
  condition that would change it.
OUT OF SCOPE: whether we would ever move abroad later.
LOSS: moving and failing to find work is worse than declining.
  Moving is hard to undo; declining costs us this one offer.
MODE: converge   CEREMONY: level 2
HOLON: container = French school calendar, her employer's start-date
  policy · whole = our household · in focus = the first six months
DEFINITIONS (locked): afford = her salary covers rent and bills,
  and our savings cover the rest for 6 months
PREMISES: P1 I can find work in Lyon within 6 months (unchecked)
  P2 her employer allows a July start (ask HR)
COMMITMENTS: not moving before July · rejected: moving in January
  · reopens if the employer refuses a July start
CLAIMS: C1 offer €58k gross · bound (letter, read 10 Nov)
  C2 family flats €1,050–1,250 · bound (5 listings, 12 Nov)
  C3 kids' school year ends 2 July · bound (school calendar)
CONTRADICTIONS: the budget works only if rent ≤ €1,100,
  but the listings near the school are €1,200+
PREDICTIONS: —
PARKED: whether to sell the car
OUTER LOG: —
=== END STATE ===
        

            
        How to use the block
        
            
        You don’t need to remember the whole block. You need to be able to say four things with the model closed:
              the goal, the constraint that would kill the current plan, the last fact you actually checked, and
                the open risk. That is about as much as attention holds while you look away. evidence
              If you can’t say those four, the conversation is holding the state and you aren’t.
        
            
        One rule keeps the block honest: no “checked” without the check attached. A claim is
              marked bound only with where it was checked: a link you opened, a document, a number you recomputed, a
              person who told you. A line the model filled in is not a check. heuristic
            
        
            
        Before you leave an unfinished thread, write two lines where you will see them on return: what you
              were trying to do, and the next physical action. “Goal: pick between the two flats. Next: email the agent
              about the heating bill.” evidence
            
        

            
        Every substantial turn: drift, evidence, commitment
        
            
        Three quick checks, whenever an answer is long enough to matter.
        
            
          
              
        1. Drift. close Does this answer change the goal, widen
                the scope, or answer a neighboring question? If you can’t tie it to DONE WHEN in one sentence, park it,
                however good it is.
          2. 
              
        2. Evidence. touch Does the answer contain a new claim
                your decision would rest on? Add it to CLAIMS as unbound, with the test that would settle it. Adjectives
                like “well established” don’t bind a claim. Only a check does.
          3. 
              
        3. Commitment. close When you decide something, add it
                to COMMITMENTS. If a later turn quietly undoes a decision, stop and say so: that is trigger 7
                below.
          4. 
            
        
            
        Example of drift: you asked which of two used cars has cheaper insurance. The answer slides into whether
              you need a car at all, and suggests an e-bike. That may be a good question. It isn’t this conversation’s
              question. Park it:
        
            
        DRIFT: whether I need a car at all
PARKED: e-bike + public transport instead of a car
GOAL remains: compare insurance for the two shortlisted cars
Next step toward DONE WHEN: quotes for both, same coverage
        
            
        Don’t discuss the parked idea in the same turn. If you discuss it, it isn’t parked.
        
            
        Parking and the brake sort the same moment. If you can’t name a trigger from the list below, the side
              idea is drift, and it gets parked. If you can, it goes through the brake. The e-bike is parked because
              nothing about the insurance quotes depends on it. If the quotes showed that neither car fits your budget,
              the same thought would have a trigger behind it, a contradiction, and it would go through the brake.
        

            
        The goal can be wrong: triggers and the brake
        
            
        Most turns improve the actions toward a goal: a cheaper option, a clearer email. Some moments
              call for questioning the goal itself and the assumptions behind it. A conversation needs both. It
              also needs a brake, because a helpful partner fails in two opposite directions. heuristic
            
        
            
        Silent compliance. The goal is bad, contradictory or aimed at the wrong target, and the
              model helps anyway, beautifully. You asked for a 10-day itinerary covering six cities and got a perfect
              one. You will spend the trip on trains, and you feel well served.
        
            
        Derailment. The goal is sound, but the conversation keeps challenging it because the
              task got hard or a more interesting question turned up. You wanted to choose between two job offers by
              Friday. By turn twenty you are discussing what meaningful work is. You feel stimulated, and Friday arrives
              with no decision.
        

            
        Eight triggers
        
            
        A challenge to the goal is legitimate only when at least one of these can be named. “This feels off” is a
              reason to look for one, not a trigger.
        
            
          
              
        1. Harm. Pursuing the goal as stated predictably causes serious harm, and you can say
                the harm in one concrete sentence. A plan to lose 10 kg in three weeks before a wedding.
          2. 
              
        2. Contradiction. The goal and its constraints can’t all hold at once. Three weeks
                  in Japan, in cherry-blossom season, flying from Lisbon, under €1,500 all in.
          3. 
              
        3. False premise. A false or unchecked premise is doing the work: if it flipped, the
                plan would flip. A relocation plan that assumes your employer will let you work from another
                  country, which nobody has asked.
          4. 
              
        4. Framing. A different frame changes what you would do, not only the words. Write the
                action under both frames. If the actions are the same, it was eloquence. If they still differ when a
                fresh chat, memory off, gets only the two frames and the facts, it is real. “Make the flat look
                  modern” and “make the flat work for a family of four” lead to different renovations.
          5. 
              
        5. Proxy capture. You could hit the target and miss the point: the done-condition can be
                met while the real aim fails (Lesson 24). “Get my son to read 30 minutes a day” is met by an audiobook playing
                  while he games.
          6. 
              
        6. Wrong level. Action at this level gets cancelled by something one level up or down
                (Lesson 25). A chore chart to stop arguments about dishes, when one of you works sixty hours a
                  week.
          7. 
              
        7. Silent override. You are breaking your own earlier decision without saying so.
                Last week the budget for the flat was €300k. Today you are comparing €340k listings, and nobody has
                  said the budget changed.
          8. 
              
        8. Check first. The next useful step is a check, and more talk would only stand in for
                it. Fifteen turns on whether your back pain is the mattress or the desk chair. A week on the guest
                  bed would answer it.
          9. 
            
        
            
        Not triggers: the task is hard; you’d prefer a different style; a more impressive
              neighboring problem; a loose word one line would fix; a stakeholder nobody mentioned and nobody can name;
              the model showing independence; boredom.
        

            
        The brake close
        
            
        When a trigger fires, the challenge goes through the brake. A model won’t reliably stop by itself, so ask
              for it: paste this form, or tell the model to use it whenever it sees a trigger.
        
            
        CHALLENGE: [which trigger, from the list]
The goal as I have it:
What fails if we continue:
Alternative (a new goal, a narrower scope, or a check first):
What we would do differently:
Cost of switching:            Cost of staying:
Reply keep | revise | split.
I will not proceed on the alternative until you answer.
        
            
        The last line is the brake. A reply that adds “but in the meantime, here’s the full alternative plan” has
              cut it. That is derailment with paperwork.
        
            
        Three answers. Suppose the challenge was proxy capture: “you asked for a cheaper car, but the real cost
              is your 90-minute commute”.
        
            
          
              
        • Keep: “Noted, but the commute isn’t changing this year. Back to the cars.” Log it in
                OUTER LOG as considered and declined, and don’t raise it again without new evidence.
          • 
              
        • Revise: “You’re right. The goal is now ‘lowest total cost of getting to work’.”
                Rewrite the state block and say what was dropped.
          • 
              
        • Split: “Finish the car comparison today. Open a separate conversation next week
                about moving closer to work.” Fence the original goal, and give the new one its own session.
          • 
            
        
            
        Silence means keep.
        
            
        The typed brake works in a chat because nothing happens until you read the reply and act on it. An agent
              working in your browser, email or accounts can send, buy or delete before you have read a word, and a
              typed “wait” can drop out when a long run is summarized. There, silence means it carries on. With an agent
              that acts, the brake is the product’s own approval step: turn it on for anything you can’t undo, and don’t
              give the agent access to what you can’t afford to lose. mechanism
            
        

            
        Ceremony: effort matched to stakes
        
            
        Not every conversation deserves the full method. Two questions decide it: what is at stake, and
                how hard is it to undo? Some decisions are two-way doors: walk through, dislike it, walk back.
              Others are one-way doors.
        
            
        
              
        
                
        
                  Level
                  When
                  What you do
                
        
                
        
                  0 · The floor
                  Two-way doors: cheap, reversible, personal. Rewording an email, a recipe swap, choosing a
                      restaurant.
                  The eight habits on the Start here page. No state block, unless the thread
                    gets long.
                
        
                
        
                  1 · The Six
                  The default when the outcome matters. Choosing a laptop for work, planning a course of study,
                      a hard conversation with a colleague.
                  The floor, plus six habits: ask for the facts before the verdict and write your beliefs as
                    hypotheses (Lesson 8); park drift; pin the level and return (Lesson 25); check one claim the
                    decision rests on (Lesson 18); log one challenge you declined. The five core lines of the state
                    block.
                
        
                
        
                  2 · Full discipline
                  One-way doors: irreversible, expensive, affecting many people or your health, or learning you will
                    have to rely on without the model. Quitting a job, signing a lease, a treatment decision, a
                      family move, studying for an exam you sit alone.
                  Every line of the state block; the invariance battery (Lesson 21); a critique in a fresh chat
                    (Lesson 30); a check on every claim the decision rests on; a pre-mortem; a written decision with a
                    reopen condition; a human second opinion.
                
        
              
        
            
        
            
        Raise the level the moment you notice the stakes rising mid-conversation. Noticing is your job.
        
            
        Ceremony earns its place only when it makes you look at the thing itself: the listing, the contract, the
              test result. If the record can be completed without looking, it is theater. heuristic
              Under time pressure, the Six are the whole discipline. If there isn’t time for those, don’t fake them.
              Check one hinge in the source itself: the single feature that, if it were different,
              would make this a different situation. Before signing a lease: is the notice period really one month,
                in the contract and not only in the agent’s email?
        

            
        G20
              
        Hold the goal fixed and the challenge open. Keep the stated goal unless a named trigger fires.
                When one fires, stop, show the action under both frames, and wait for keep, revise or split. Match the
                ceremony to the stakes: two-way doors get the floor, one-way doors get the full discipline.
        
            
        

            
        For builders
              
        Running the loop with an agent.
        
              
          
                
        • State is a file. Keep the state block in the repository or the task folder,
                  versioned, and carry it forward in place of the log (TA14).
          • 
                
        • Say who drew the boundary. One line for the state block: who is drawing this
                  task’s boundary (what the run may touch, what is out of scope), and why now. If the task description
                  wouldn’t change under a different stated purpose, that line is decorative. heuristic
          • 
                
        • Three concurrent runs are not three copies of your attention. Under a second task,
                  the look at the automation is the first thing you drop. evidence
                
          • 
                
        • A long log with rare real failures is a vigil, and sensitivity falls on a vigil.
                  Compare the log to a written expected state: the goal, the files
                  allowed to change, the test that must stay red until it should turn green. Insert checks whose answers
                  you already know, or shorten the watch. evidence
                
          • 
                
        • A run that looks like a normal success gets waved through. Under time pressure,
                  experts recognize a familiar situation and act on it. Check one hinge yourself. A simulation the
                  model hands you is not yours: if you can’t run the action against the constraint, use an external
                  check. evidence
                
          • 
                
        • The brake belongs in the interface. Triggers and keep/revise/split should be
                  first-class actions in an agent UI, with gates only where a wrong action can’t be rolled back:
                  sending, deleting, spending, changing a public system (TD6, TC7). A confirm-first rule that lives only
                  in the conversation is not a gate. Enforce it in the harness, outside the model’s context: compaction
                  can drop or blur the rule, and even an intact rule sometimes fails to fire. evidence
                
          • 
              
        
              
        TA14
                
        Compact state; don’t drag history. Keep the original tool output addressable, and do not
                  carry forward abandoned attempts. Before leaving an unfinished thread, write the suspended goal and
                  the next physical action. Stable instructions at the top, volatile state at the bottom.
        
              
        
            
        
          
        


                    
        
            
        Lesson 30Adversarial passes
        
            
        close A critic that shares the draft’s context tends to go easy on the
              draft. It continues the same commitment: in the same chat, it knows which version you like and how hard
              you worked on it. Models also tend to prefer their own outputs when they judge. evidence
              Real criticism needs separation: a fresh chat, memory off, ideally a different model
              family, a named standard, and the author hidden.
        
            
        Take your cover letter. In the chat where you wrote it, “Is this good?” tends to get “Strong letter, a
              few small tweaks”. In a fresh chat, presented as a stranger’s letter for a specific job, with the job ad
              pasted in, you are far more likely to be shown the paragraph a hiring manager would skip.
        
            
        In the loop (Lesson 29), an adversarial pass comes at the end of a close. You have a draft, a plan or a
              decision, and before you act on it you hand it to something that doesn’t share your thread. What comes
              back is another text, so it is a claim like any other: write each flaw into the state block under CLAIMS,
              unbound, until you have checked it. The critic shows you where to touch. The checking is still yours, and
              if a flaw survives it, the decision reopens.
        
            
        ADVERSARIAL PACKET (fresh chat, memory off; don't say who wrote it)
What the work must achieve: [GOAL + DONE WHEN]
Standard: [what "good enough" means, concretely]
The work: [paste the claims, plan, letter or code; leave out the persuasive story]
Task: find the single most damaging flaw, the point that, if it breaks,
breaks the whole thing. Quote it. Explain how it fails. Say what evidence
would show you're wrong. Then up to 3 lesser issues.
Do not rewrite or improve the work. "Broadly sound" is not an answer.
        
            
        Three classic forms
        
            
        Pre-mortem. “It’s a year from now and this failed. Write the story of how.” Treating the
              failure as certain makes people write longer and more specific accounts of how it came about. evidence
              Ask for structural stories, not a list of generic risks: what drained, what delay hid it, which early
              signal got ignored. “We moved to Lyon a year ago and it went badly. Tell me the story, naming the
                money that ran out, the month we should have noticed, and the sign we explained away.”
        
            
        Competing hypotheses. List every plausible explanation and every piece of evidence, then
              mark each piece as consistent or inconsistent with each explanation. Two traps fall away. Evidence that
              fits every explanation tells you nothing, so only the pieces that discriminate count. And you
              look for what refutes an explanation, not what confirms it. The tentative winner is the one with the
              least evidence against it. heuristic
              Your six-year-old suddenly sleeps badly. Screen time, a worry about school, a room that’s too warm?
                “Tired in the morning” fits all three, so it’s useless. “Sleeps badly on Sunday nights only” counts
                against the warm room, which is warm all week, and against the screens unless Sundays differ. School has
                the least against it.
        
            
        Attack the assumptions, or build a rival plan. Steelman the plan first: state it at
              its strongest, as its best defender would (the principle of charity, Lesson 9). Then the attack has to
              come from somewhere other than the author’s own thread. Two methods do this, in a fresh chat with
              authorship hidden. One: a recommendation, then a critique aimed only at its named assumptions, and you do
              the rewrite. Two: a second plan that must rest on assumptions contradicting the first, then a comparison
              of the two. In groups of people, both led to better decisions than talking until everyone agreed. evidence
              A list of pros and cons of one plan is neither. Neither is a persona told to disagree: that is a costume
              (Lesson 21). The plan: take a lodger to cover the mortgage rise. The first method attacks what the
                plan assumes: a lodger found within two months, and both of you happy to share a kitchen. The second
                builds the rival plan for the case where nobody suitable turns up: a longer mortgage term.
        

            
        When models argue with each other
        
            
        A second copy of the same model, in the same chat, told to argue the other side, is not an opponent.
              Models in a debate tend to drift toward agreement, sometimes abandoning a correct answer to do it, and
              more rounds can make things worse. evidence
              Debate helps when the critic can see something you can’t, or is better than you at the check, and you
              treat each critique as a claim to verify. If you have no way to check the answer, a debate won’t give you
              one. Two chats arguing over whether your landlord can keep the deposit won’t settle it. The tenancy
                agreement and the law will. Don’t ask the voices to converge. Use a different model family for the
              critic, and show it the answer, not the reasoning that produced it.
        

            
        Four more ways to attack a good story
        
            
        Each of these is for a story you already like. heuristic
        
            
          
              
        • Write two rivals before you accept an explanation. Before a story becomes
                the account, write two other stories that would also explain the evidence. If you can’t write
                any, there is nothing to compare yet. A rival earns its place only if it would make what you are
                explaining ordinary, and it predicts something you haven’t used yet.
                A story being lovely is a reason to test it, not a reason to believe it.
                “The neighbor’s dog barks at night because it’s lonely.” Rivals: it hears foxes; it reacts to the
                  2 a.m. delivery truck. Each predicts a different pattern of nights. “Something is wrong with that
                  dog” explains anything, so it stays out.
          • 
              
        • Turn a pre-mortem into signposts. For each assumption the plan rests on, name an
                observable sign with a threshold and a date, one action that makes the assumption more likely to hold,
                and one fallback if it breaks.
                “Monitor the situation” is not a signpost. The plan assumes I find work in Lyon within six months.
                  Signpost: no interviews by the end of month three. Action now: start applying before we move.
                  Fallback: remote contract work.
          • 
              
        • Scenarios that threaten your view. Optimistic, pessimistic and base case are three
                versions of one forecast. For scenarios, list what is already certain, name one or two uncertainties that would fork
                the decision, and write two or three futures that contain all the certainties and differ on the
                uncertainty.
                If none of them threatens your current plan, they are the wrong scenarios. Certain: the baby is due
                  in May and the lease ends in August. Uncertain: whether your contract is renewed in June. Write the
                  summer where it is and the summer where it isn’t, and see which plan survives both.
          • 
              
        • A repair must predict something new. When a favorite explanation fails and you patch
                it, write the patch in one sentence, plus one new thing the patch predicts that you didn’t use to build
                it. If you can’t, it is only a patch. Keep the idea as a hunch if you like, but don’t say it
                survived.
                “The dog is lonely” fails: it also barks on nights its owners are home. Patch: “it misses the dog
                  next door, who moved away.” New prediction: it goes quiet on the days that dog visits. Nobody used
                  that to build the patch, so it can count.
          • 
            
        
          
        


                    
        
            
        Lesson 31The Elephant Protocol
        
            
        The loop of Lesson 29, written out once for one serious question. Use it at ceremony level 1 (the Six)
              or level 2 (full discipline). For a cheap decision you can walk back, it is too much. For a one-way door
              it is the minimum: level 2 also asks for a pre-mortem and a human second opinion.
        
            
          
              
        1. Question with a use. close Write GOAL, USE and DONE
                WHEN, and get the model’s restatement before any work.
          2. 
              
        2. Name your Double. close Write privately what you hope
                to hear (Lesson 8). Write your beliefs as hypotheses, P1, P2…
              
          3. 
              
        3. Classify. close What kind of problem is it (Lesson
                28), and what ceremony does it deserve (Lesson 29)?
              
          4. 
              
        4. Boundary. close Inside, outside, excluded on purpose,
                and what contains it: the holon card (Lesson 25). Call the boundary temporary.
              
          5. 
              
        5. Reports. open Standpoints, a distribution of answers
                (Lesson 27), a fact map (Lesson 10). Don’t judge yet.
          6. 
              
        6. Structure. open The pattern over time and what
                produces it (Lesson 24). close Classify the contradictions instead of
                smoothing them (Lesson 26).
              
          7. 
              
        7. Touch. close Run the invariance battery on the key
                answer (Lesson 21) and a critique in a fresh chat, memory off (Lesson 30). These two don’t count as the
                check. They show you what to check. touch Then check the claims the
                decision rests on against something that can say no: a source opened, a number recomputed, a person
                asked (Lesson 18).
          8. 
              
        8. Decide or probe. close A written decision (the
                choice, the rejected alternative, the reopen condition). touch Or small
                safe-to-fail experiments, when nobody can know the answer in advance (Lesson 28).
          9. 
              
        9. Close and own. close An honest state block, and what
                you expect to see, by when. touch Then, with the model out of the room,
                a short explanation in your own words. You are done when you have questions you couldn’t have asked at
                the start.
          10. 
            
        

            
        Worked example: should we move to Lyon for Ana’s job?
        
            
        1. Question. GOAL: yes or no to the offer. USE: our answer to her employer by 21
              November. DONE WHEN: we know whether we can afford six months on one salary, what happens to the kids’
              school year, and the one condition that would change the answer. The model’s restatement widened it to
              “plan our life in France”. Corrected.
        
            
        2. Double. Private note: I hope she says no, because I like my job. P1: I’ll
              find work in Lyon within six months. P2: her employer allows a July start. Both unchecked.
