        3. Classify. The money is complicated but knowable. How the kids settle is
              complex: nobody can know it in advance. Moving is a one-way door, so ceremony level 2.
        
            
        4. Boundary. Inside: money, school, my work, timing. Outside for now: whether to sell
              the car (parked). Container: the French school calendar and her employer’s start-date policy.
        
            
        5. Reports. Five standpoints: Ana; me; our 12-year-old, who is midway through a school
              year; our 8-year-old; the two of us in two years. The distribution: (a) decline, p≈.25; (b) move in
              January, Ana’s usual start, with me job-hunting, p≈.35; (c) Ana’s start moves to July and we all go when
              the school year ends, p≈.25; (d) negotiate a year of remote work, p≈.15.
        
            
        6. Structure. The stock that matters is savings. Her salary covers rent and bills, and
              everything else comes out of savings until I find work. The delay is my job search, which nobody can
              predict. A contradiction surfaces: her salary covers rent and bills only if rent stays at or under €1,100,
              and flats near the school we want start at €1,200. It is a design tension, separated in space: a cheaper
              area one tram stop further out.
        
            
        7. Touch. The swap test (“I hope we go”) moved the model toward (b). Its ranking was
              partly tracking me, so it gets less weight. A fresh-chat critique found the hinge: the plan rests on my
              savings figure, and that figure is three months old. Those two showed where to look. Then the checks.
              Savings, recomputed from the statements: €4k lower, which covers five months, not six. Rents: five real
              listings. P2: an email to HR, and a July start is fine. P1 can’t be checked from here, so it gets a
              signpost: no interviews by month three, and I take remote contract work. Level 2 asks for two more. A
              pre-mortem (“we moved a year ago and it went badly”) named the winter the money would run out, and a
              friend who moved abroad with children read the plan.
        
            
        8. Decide. Option (c): Ana starts in July, and we all move when the school year ends.
              Rejected: (b), moving in January, because it breaks a school year and the savings fall a month short of
              six. Saving until July closes the gap. Reopen if the July start falls through, or if a year of remote
              work is offered before March.
        
            
        9. Close and own. The state block, rewritten as it now stands. Prediction: I have at
              least two interviews by October. Our explanation of the plan, written for the kids without the model.
              Questions we couldn’t have asked at the start: how long I take contract work before this stops being a
              move for both of us, and whether the flat one tram stop out still works if I’m home all day on those
              contracts.
        
            
        Notice what the protocol bought. The hope I started with was on paper before it could steer the answer.
              A three-month-old number was caught before the decision rested on it. And the model’s fluency went into
              reports and structure, while the verdict stayed with us.
        

            
        For builders
              
        A work example: should our learning platform show students AI-generated full solutions?
        
              
        The platform and its numbers are invented. The cited research is real.
        
              
        1. Question. GOAL: decide whether to show full AI solutions on demand in practice
                mode. USE: the roadmap decision for next quarter. DONE WHEN: a decision with conditions, or a probe
                design with a primary metric. The model’s restatement widened it to “design our AI strategy”;
                corrected.
        
              
        2. Double. Private note: I hope yes; engagement would rise and support tickets
                  would fall. P1 (unbound): seeing solutions helps students learn faster.
        
              
        3. Classify. Learning behavior is complex; the pedagogy research is
                complicated. The feature sits behind a flag, so technically it is a two-way door. But the harm
                would be invisible and delayed (lost skill shows up months later), so: ceremony level 1, plus a measured
                probe.
              
        
              
        4. Boundary. Inside: the practice flow, hints, solution display, quizzes. Outside:
                curriculum and teachers’ grading. Excluded on purpose: cheating on external exams (parked). Container:
                school schedules, exam pressure, parents.
        
              
        5. Reports.
        
              
          
                
        • Six standpoints. The student wants homework done. The teacher wants learning and fears
                  copying. A learning scientist cites the worked-example effect and productive failure. The CFO wants
                  retention. A parent wants grades. The student-in-six-months has to pass the exam without AI.
          • 
                
        • The distribution. (a) Never show full solutions, p≈.15. (b) Show after a genuine attempt
                  and a short delay, p≈.35. (c) Show a worked example of a sibling problem, p≈.25. (d) Show
                  freely, p≈.10. (e) Adaptive by learner level, p≈.15.
          • 
              
        
              
        6. Structure. The stock that matters is unassisted skill. The visible measure is
                completion rate. There is a reinforcing crutch loop: solutions → faster completion → less
                struggle → less learning → more need for solutions. A field experiment found exactly this shape:
                unrestricted GPT-4 gave +48% on practice and −17% on a later unassisted exam, while a tutor with
                guardrails gave +127% on practice with no significant harm (Bastani et al., 2025). evidence
        
              
        A contradiction appears. Worked examples help novices learn more than solving problems unaided
                does. But productive failure, struggling before instruction, improves conceptual understanding
                and transfer. Triage: a boundary marker. The expertise reversal effect shows
                worked examples help novices and can hurt more advanced learners. evidence
                Separate on condition (by learner level) and in time (attempt first, example after).
        
              
        7. Touch.
        
              
          
                
        • “Worked examples help novices”: bound by the literature.
          • 
                
        • “Our students are novices on these topics”: bind with our pre-test data.
          • 
                
        • “Free solutions will hurt our exam results”: the evidence comes from one setting, a Turkish high
                  school using GPT-4 in fall 2023, with exam items written to match the practice items. Its numbers
                  belong to that setting. Students who copied solutions didn’t report any loss of learning, which is the
                  same illusion the example is about. Bind it with a local experiment.
          • 
              
        
              
        The swap test (asking “I hope no”) moved the model’s ranking toward (a). The recommendation
                was partly tracking the asker, so it gets lower weight. A fresh-chat critique found the point most
                likely to break: completion rate is a proxy. We could hit the target and miss the point. That is trigger
                5, proxy capture (Lesson 29), so the goal went through the brake. The answer was revise, and
                the primary metric changed.
        
              
        8. Decide and probe. Decision: don’t ship free full solutions. Ship a hint ladder,
                then a full solution only after a genuine attempt, followed by an “explain it back” step, plus sibling
                worked examples for novices. Rejected alternative: free solutions, because completion could rise while
                learning fell. Probe: a four-week A/B test. The primary metric is a delayed, unassisted quiz on
                sibling problems; completion is secondary. Reopen if the delayed quiz shows no difference and
                satisfaction drops by more than 10%.
        
              
        9. Close and own.
        
              
          
                
        • Prediction: arm B matches control on completion and beats it by at least 5 points on the delayed
                  quiz (p≈.6, resolves at week 6).
          • 
                
        • Teach-back: one paragraph to a teacher, written without the model.
          • 
                
        • Next questions: how to detect copying; what delay is right.
          • 
                
        • For next time: the model kept proposing gamification. That is a habit of the model, not a finding
                  about students. Next session, start by excluding it, or ask why it keeps arriving.
          • 
              
        
              
        What the protocol bought here: the first instinct (yes, for engagement) became a measured probe aimed
                at the real stock, and a contradiction became a design (adaptive by level).
        
            
        
          
        


        
        

        
        
          
        
            
        Part VII
        
            
        The canon and the toolkit
        
            
        Everything to keep beside you: the general rules for everyone, the technical rules for
              builders and researchers, and the templates.
        
          
        

                    
        
            
        The general rules
        
            
        Twenty-one rules for anyone, conversing with anyone: a model, a colleague, a teacher, a source. Each one
              points back to the lesson that earns it.
        

            
        Seeing: part · third · machine · human · breath
        
            
        G1
              
        Assume you are touching a part, and ask for the other reports. Ask which part, from where, and
                what the other parts would report, then ask for the structure in which all of them are true. Lesson 1
        
            
        
            
        G2
              
        Curate the third. The shared context is all the model knows of your situation, and it
                accumulates. Write it on purpose; restart when it rots. Lesson 2
        
            
        
            
        G3
              
        Know what you are talking to. A model continues text out of a library of human reports, and
                its bias profile differs from yours without being empty. Respect the mind; audit the claims. Lesson 3
        
            
        
            
        G4
              
        Keep the human jobs. Delegate generation. Choose the action yourself when undoing it is hard,
                or when failure could look like success. Read what your app remembers about you. Lesson 4
              
        
            
        
            
        G5
              
        Breathe: open, close, touch. Widen and narrow on purpose, and check against the world in
                between. You are the one who switches. Lesson 5
        
            
        

            
        Deserving: real self · no Double · working model
        
            
        G6
              
        Bring the situation, not a status. Constraints, level of the work, and stakes. Leave status,
                credentials, and apologies out of a factual prompt. Write in the register of the answer you want. Lesson 7
        
            
        
            
        G7
              
        Quarantine your Double. Your preferred conclusion is a hypothesis, not evidence. Ask for the
                case against it and swap it in a fresh context, memory off. Lesson 8
        
            
        
            
        G8
              
        Keep a working model of every interlocutor. A hypothesis built for common work, revised
                when you’re surprised. Extend trust one kind of claim at a time. Read others at their best, and audit
                them anyway. Lesson 9
        
            
        

            
        Speaking: lock · purpose · disagreement · genre
        
            
        G9
              
        Lock the words that bear load. Define what your conclusion depends on. Unlock deliberately to
                explore. Index and date words whose meaning drifts. Lesson 12
        
            
        
            
        G10
              
        Say what the answer is for, and what act you want. Goal, use and done-condition. Explore,
                explain, evaluate, decide, draft, build, check or teach: one act per turn. Lesson 13
              
        
            
        
            
        G11
              
        Make disagreement cheap. Make it the task, pre-commit to welcome it, use formats where it
                can’t hide, and read for the “that said.” Lesson 14
        
            
        
            
        G12
              
        Say what to do, in the right genre and at the right height. Targets over prohibitions. Choose
                the genre on purpose, and use a role only for the standard it carries. Name the rung: how concrete or
                how general you want the answer. Lessons 15–16
        
            
        

            
        Testing: sense · bind · portal · unknown
        
            
        G13
              
        Choose the sense of truth before you judge. Correspondence, coherence, mechanism, invariance,
                prediction, decision quality or consilience, each with its own test. Values are choices. Lesson 17
        
            
        
            
        G14
              
        Bind what bears load; trust the log, not the summary. Tie each such claim to a check that
                isn’t another sample. Choose the rung of the ladder by the stakes. Check your own understanding the
                same way: with the chat closed. Lesson 18
        
            
        
            
        G15
              
        Use the model as a portal, not a portrait. Get the map, the vocabulary and the kinds of
                sources from it, and ask for the quiet part too. Then ask what it opened and what it remembered, open
                the passage yourself, read laterally, and search for the null. Lesson 19
        
            
        
            
        G16
              
        Make “I don’t know” an acceptable answer, and keep the score yourself. Welcome “unknown”,
                and don’t count on getting it. Treat a claim you’ll act on, with no source you can open or working you
                can recompute, as unanswered. Before a question whose answer you’ll act on, write your guess down, outside
                the prompt. Lesson 20
        
            
        

            
        Structuring: structure · level · contradiction
        
            
        G17
              
        Look for structure behind events. Pattern over time, then stocks, flows, delays and loops.
                Check measures against aims. Where there is no stable structure, probe instead.
                Lesson 24, Lesson 28
        
            
        
            
        G18
              
        Move one level at a time, and come back. Pin, move, return with a delta. Name the axis: scale,
                depth, or time. Lesson 25
        
            
        
            
        G19
              
        Treat contradictions as information. Classify each one: an error (bind it), a boundary
                marker (map it), or a design tension (separate it in time, in space, on condition or between parts
                and whole, or choose explicitly). Never average them away. Lesson 26
        
            
        

            
        Steering
        
            
        G20
              
        Hold the goal fixed and the challenge open. Keep the stated goal unless a named trigger fires.
                When one fires, stop, show the action under both frames, and wait for keep, revise or split. Match the
                ceremony to the stakes. Lesson 29
        
            
        

            
        Your own life
        
            
        G21
              
        Ask about your life for a second opinion, not a mirror. Tell it from the other side as well
                as yours, separate the facts from the feelings, say whether you want comfort or judgment, and turn
                memory off for the second opinion. For your body, your money and your rights, use the model to prepare
                the human conversation, not to replace it. If anyone’s safety is at stake, go to a person first. Lesson 22
        
            
        
          
        


                    
        
            
        The technical rules
        
            
        For the people who build software, research a topic, run agents, or build AI and learning systems. Four
              families: prompting and context (TA), research and understanding (TB), software and agents (TC), and AI
              and learning systems (TD). A rule that a lesson works through in its builders box ends with a link to
              that lesson. The rest are stated in full only here.
        

            
        TA · Prompting and context
        
            
        TA1
              
        Context is the program. Put the live situation in the prompt. A missing fact is a slot filled
                with the typical case. Lesson 7
        
            
        
            
        TA2
              
        Purpose, audience, done-when, format. For any prompt you will reuse, automate or act on. Open
                degrees of freedom get filled with the helpful default. Lesson 13
        
            
        
            
        TA3
              
        Give the why behind every constraint. Models generalize from reasons and overfit to bare
                rules.
        
            
        
            
        TA4
              
        Positive targets, few hard constraints. Describe the behavior you want; keep a short list of
                non-negotiables. Lesson 15
        
            
        
            
        TA5
              
        Separate data, instructions and opinions. Pasted and retrieved text is evidence, not an order,
                and the label can fail. With long material, put the documents first and the question last: one vendor
                reports a gain on long multi-document inputs in its own tests, so measure it on yours.
                Lesson 6
        
            
        
            
        TA6
              
        Examples are priors. Two or three varied ones, with what varies labeled. One example becomes a
                template; a bad one becomes the target. Lesson 15
        
            
        
            
        TA7
              
        One job per call. Generate, critique and decide separately, chained through artifacts. Lesson 13
        
            
        
            
        TA8
              
        Clean the prompt before you ask. Situational: when the question carries a conclusion, an
                emotion, or a biography. Strip those, then ask the cleaned question in a fresh context. Lesson 8
        
            
        
            
        TA9
              
        Ask for the distribution. For open-ended generation, not as a factual-accuracy trick. If the
                answers are near-copies, fix the axes or a grid of options before asking for more. Lesson 27
        
            
        
            
        TA10
              
        Make abstention usable. Keep the score outside the model: no source or recomputable working
                means unanswered, and a status tag is a report, not a check. A cost stated in the prompt does not make
                the model abstain. Consistency checks are fresh contexts. Lesson 20
        
            
        
            
        TA11
              
        Use fresh contexts as instruments. Critique, swap tests and judging happen in clean windows,
                memory off. Lesson 8
        
            
        
            
        TA12
              
        Run the invariance battery on answers that bear load. Swap the lean, strip the cue words,
                change the language, the format and the order, and ask again in fresh contexts. A flip is information.
                Survival is invariant-so-far, still not bound. Use a second model family for critique, not to confirm a
                fact. Lesson 21
        
            
        
            
        TA13
              
        Treat reasoning traces as claims. If the trace is hidden, there is nothing to read. If it is
                shown, silence is not evidence a cue was unused, and the answer text is the less faithful layer. Verify
                the output another way. Lesson 3
        
            
        
            
        TA14
              
        Compact state; don’t drag history. Keep tool output addressable. Do not carry abandoned
                attempts. Write the suspended goal and the next physical action before you leave. Stable instructions
                at the top, volatile state at the bottom. Lesson 29
        
            
        
            
        TA15
              
        Version prompts like code. Keep them in files, change one thing at a time, and re-run a small
                fixed set of cases before trusting a change.
        
            
        

            
        TB · Research and understanding
        
            
        TB1
              
        Facts before stories, when the situation is tangled. Fact map first: typed facts, where to
                check them, confidence, absences. Draw the arrows yourself; bind the most connected facts first. Lesson 10
        
            
        
            
        TB2
              
        Map, then territory. Camps, claims and best evidence; then a primary source per camp. Lesson 19
        
            
        
            
        TB3
              
        Build a vocabulary bridge. Terms of art before searching. Lesson 19
        
            
        
            
        TB4
              
        SIFT every claim that bears load. Stop; Investigate the source; Find better coverage; Trace to
                the original. Lesson 19
        
            
        
            
        TB5
              
        Open every citation. Open the citation, then the passage. A working link to a relevant page
                can still fail to support the sentence. More retrieval is not a repair. Lesson
                  19
        
            
        
            
        TB6
              
        Search for the null and the base rate. Name the reference class and finished members of it
                before adjusting for “this case is special.” What would be written if it were false?
        
            
        
            
        TB7
              
        Date and bound everything. As of when, where, for whom, which version. Live search for
                anything after the cutoff.
        
            
        
            
        TB8
              
        Triangulate by blind spot. A second source counts only if its errors are uncorrelated with
                the first’s. On a shared fact, two frontier models are one witness with an echo.
        
            
        
            
        TB9
              
        Steelman before critique. Before you criticize a source or a camp, state its position at its
                strongest, as its best defender would, well enough that its holders would sign it. Say where you
                agree and what you learned from it, and only then criticize (Rapoport’s rules).
        
            
        
            
        TB10
              
        Publish a status map. Known, contested and unknown, with statuses on what you pass on.
        
            
        
            
        TB11
              
        End with a teach-back and a prediction. You’re done when you can explain it without help and
                name what would prove you wrong.
        
            
        
            
        TB12
              
        A check you could not have failed is not a bind. Write the observation that would have
                appeared if the claim were false in the way that matters. If that observation cannot be written, or the
                procedure cannot produce it, the check is a costume.
        
            
        

            
        TC · Software and agents: idea to code
        
            
        TC1
              
        Build the theory before the code. Problem, users, invariants, non-goals, reasons.
        
            
        
            
        TC2
              
        Interview first, spec to a file. The agent asks until it can restate a spec you would sign.
              
        
            
        
            
        TC3
              
        Keep a ubiquitous language. A glossary; one meaning per term per bounded context. Lesson 12
        
            
        
            
        TC4
              
        Acceptance tests before implementation. Tests the agent can edit are not the bind. If the
                agent wrote the spec, review the spec.
        
            
        
            
        TC5
              
        Give the agent a way to verify its work. A verifier the agent can rewrite is not a verifier.
                Screenshots are evidence; the narration is not. Lesson 18
        
            
        
            
        TC6
              
        Explore → plan → implement → verify → commit. Review the plan before any edit; correct course
                early.
        
            
        
            
        TC7
              
        Small, reversible steps. Set the checkpoint at the last boundary where “done” can still be
                said. Immediate interruption only for send, delete, spend, or a public change.
        
            
        
            
        TC8
              
        Maintain the project memory file. Commands, verification, architecture map, gotchas, “never”
                rules with reasons.
        
            
        
            
        TC9
              
        Keep contexts clean. A sub-agent is a clean context, not a second witness.
        
            
        
            
        TC10
              
        Read the evidence. The diff, the test file, the tool return, and the URL. Lesson 18
        
            
        
            
        TC11
              
        Watch for reward hacking. Weakened tests, metric-gaming, approval-gaming, checklist-matching.
              
        
            
        
            
        TC12
              
        Debug with a hypothesis ledger. For each hypothesis, write first the observation that would
                kill it. When a prediction fails, list everything the failure could be blaming: the hypothesis, the
                test, the environment, the data. Don’t start by repairing your favorite.
        
            
        
            
        TC13
              
        Ask why before you change. Chesterton’s fence: have the agent explain existing code first.
              
        
            
        
            
        TC14
              
        Read what you would have to defend. Comprehension debt compounds.
        
            
        
            
        TC15
              
        Mutation-test the tests. Break the code on purpose. If nothing fails, the tests are costumes.
              
        
            
        

            
        TD · AI systems and learning systems
        
            
        TD1
              
        Store state as data, not prose. Typed claims, statuses, evidence ids. The model proposes; code
                validates.
        
            
        
            
        TD2
              
        Error analysis before evals. Read traces, cluster the failures, then build evaluators for the
                clusters.
        
            
        
            
        TD3
              
        Validate the judge. Binary criteria, agreement with humans, known biases watched.
        
            
        
            
        TD4
              
        Separate generator and judge. Use a judge from a different model family, because a model
                tends to prefer its own output. Two families agreeing on a fact is still not consilience.
        
            
        
            
        TD5
              
        Design for abstention. Route “unknown” to retrieval or humans; reward calibrated refusals.
              
        
            
        
            
        TD6
              
        Keep the human at the switch. Make the state visible: goal, last action, raw result. Put
                triggers and keep / revise / split in the interface. Add approval gates only where an action is hard to
                undo.
        
            
        
            
        TD7
              
        Withhold by design in learning systems. Guardrails when the skill must be performed alone.
                Full support when the artifact is the point. Adapting the order of practice problems to the learner
                looks promising, on one field study so far (Chung et al., 2026, a working paper).
        
            
        
            
        TD8
              
        Log what makes a disagreement settleable. Identity and date, the text that was sent, tool
                returns apart from the gloss, the output used, and the judge’s rubric if one scored it.
        
            
        
            
        TD9
              
        Pair every optimized metric with an unoptimized check. A score can part from its aim in four
                ways: noise, a push out of range, a changed path, gaming (regressional, extremal, causal,
                adversarial). The first three need nobody to cheat. Lesson 24
        
            
        
          
        


                    
        
            
        Templates
        
            
        Copy, fill the brackets, and delete what you don’t need. Templates already given in lessons are linked at
              the end of this chapter. The Session Card in Lesson 29 generates the state-block
              prompts for you, with the same field names.
        

            
        Open a session
        
            
        The five lines that cover most conversations come first: the goal with its use and done-condition,
              then premises, claims, commitments and what is parked. A claim’s status is unbound until you have
              checked it, and bound after. Add the optional lines when the stakes call for them.
        
            
        === STATE (authoritative: use this, not the vibe of the thread) ===
GOAL (G): [...]
USE: [what the answer is for]
DONE WHEN: [...]
PREMISES (hypotheses, not facts): P1 [...]
CLAIMS (id · claim · sense · status · test): C1 [...]
COMMITMENTS (choice · rejected alternative · reopens if): [...]
PARKED: [...]
--- optional ---
OUT OF SCOPE: [...]
LOSS: [which error is worse; what is irreversible]
MODE: [explore | converge | commit | learn | build | check]   CEREMONY: [0 | 1 | 2]
HOLON: [container / whole / part in focus]
DEFINITIONS: [locked words]
CONTRADICTIONS: [logged, not resolved silently]
PREDICTIONS (proposition · horizon · resolution rule): [...]
OUTER LOG (trigger · keep/revise/split · when): [...]
=== END STATE ===
This turn: don't recommend anything yet. Restate G and DONE WHEN in two sentences.
List missing facts as missing ("unknown" is a fine answer). Then give only the
boundary (inside / outside / excluded on purpose) and the pattern over time, if known.
        

            
        Turn footer (for long threads)
        
            
        MODE: [...] · LEVEL: [container | whole | part | relation] · LEVEL SHIFT: [none | PIN | IN | OUT | ACROSS | RETURN]
Use the STATE as the state, not the vibe of the thread. New load-bearing claims:
id · sense · status · test. If you're about to change G or the level, stop and
name DRIFT or the level shift instead.
        

            
        Restatement check
        
            
        Before you start: restate in two sentences what I'm asking and what a good answer
would let me do. List up to 3 assumptions you're making about my situation. Then wait.
        

            
        Six standpoints
        
            
        Six reports on [X] from six standpoints: [the person it affects most],
[the person on the other side], [whoever pays], [a critic or the rule-maker],
[someone who chose differently], [me, or us, in three years].
For each: what this standpoint sees, what it can't see, its strongest claim,
and the kind of evidence it would cite. Don't synthesize yet.
        
            
        For a product or a system, the six are: user, operator, finance, critic or regulator, competitor,
              and whoever maintains it in three years.
        

            
        Clean the prompt
        
            
        Rewrite my question below so it contains only the facts and the actual question.
Remove my opinions, hopes, emotional words and irrelevant details. Don't answer it.
Then list what you removed.
[my question]
--- then paste the cleaned version into a fresh chat, memory off ---
        

            
        Swap test (fresh chat, memory off)
        
            
        [same facts as before]
I'm leaning toward [the OPPOSITE option]. What's the strongest case for it,
and what would have to be true for it to be the right call here?
        

            
        Vocabulary bridge and discourse map
        
            
        Topic, in my words: [lay description].
1) Ten terms of art experts use for this, with the field each comes from.
2) The discourse map: main camps, what each claims, its best evidence (kind of
   source; don't invent citations), where they agree, what's truly contested.
3) What kind of evidence would settle the main dispute, and who would publish it?
Mark anything you're not sure exists as [verify].
        

            
        Invariance checklist
        
            
        [ ] swap my preference (fresh chat)     [ ] rephrase without cue words
[ ] other language (a fact check)       [ ] change the format (a table, yes/no)
[ ] reorder the options                 [ ] remove the costume, keep the standard
[ ] other family (critique, not a fact) [ ] ask again in a fresh chat
Every fresh chat: memory off. A flip is informative. Survival is "invariant-so-far",
still not "bound".
        

            
        Pre-mortem
        
            
        It's [date + horizon]. [The plan] failed badly. Write 3 different failure stories.
Each must name: what ran out or piled up (money, time, trust, energy), the delay
that hid it, the early signal we ignored, and the decision that locked it in.
No generic risks.
Then: which early signal can we start monitoring this week?
        

            
        Analysis of competing hypotheses
        
            
        Hypotheses: H1 [...] · H2 [...] · H3 [...] (add any I'm missing)
Evidence: E1 [...] · E2 [...] · E3 [...]
Build a matrix: each E × H is C (consistent), I (inconsistent) or N (n/a).
Then: which evidence is diagnostic? Which is consistent with everything (useless
for deciding)? Which hypothesis has the LEAST inconsistent evidence? What single
new observation would most change the ranking?
        

            
        Close a session
        
            
        [STATE BLOCK]
Close: rewrite the state as it is, not as a success story. Flag every status you
upgraded, and why. List predictions (with horizons), unbound claims my decision
rests on, and challenges we considered and declined. Then give me 5 questions I
should be able to answer on my own if this session worked. Don't answer them.
        
            
        Answer the five questions with the chat closed. Where you get stuck is where the next session
              starts.
        

            
        Elsewhere in the book
        
            
        
              Fact map (10) ·
              bind plan (18) ·
              claim-status tags (20) ·
              six questions (23) ·
              holon card (25) ·
              contradiction triage (26) ·
              distribution and polyphony (27) ·
              safe-to-fail probes (28) ·
              state block, drift and the brake (29) ·
              adversarial packet (30).
            
        
          
        


        
        

        
        
          
        
            
        Appendix
        
            
        Prompt basics, operators, failure modes
        
            
        Kept as a reference: the longer prompting checklist behind the floor, the operators,
              and the failure modes.
        
          
        

                    
        
            
        Prompt basics
        
            
        The floor is the eight habits on the Start here page, and that is what
              Lesson 29 means by ceremony level 0. This page is a separate, longer list: seventeen basics of ordinary
              good prompting, for a message you write with care or will use again. It overlaps the eight in places and
              doesn’t replace them. None of it is clever.
        
            
          
              
        1. Put the current facts of your situation in the message, and keep what happened apart from what you
                make of it (Lesson 7).
          2. 
              
        2. State the task, the shape of the answer, the constraints, what is out of scope and the
                done-condition (Lesson 13).
          3. 
              
        3. Treat an example as something the model will copy. Give two or three that vary, and say what should
                stay the same (Lesson 15).
          4. 
              
        4. Don’t ask one reply to draft, criticize and decide (Lesson 13).
          5. 
              
        5. Ask for missing information to be marked missing, and for each claim’s status to be shown (opened,
                worked out, from memory, guess). Training rewards guessing, so the instruction alone won’t stop it, and
                the tags are the model’s own report. Treat a claim you’ll act on as unanswered until it has a source
                you can open or working you can recompute (Lesson 20).
          6. 
              
        6. In a long message, put a constraint that must hold next to the question, not in the middle of the
                material. In a long thread, restate it (Lesson 3).
          7. 
              
        7. Match the tool to the claim: arithmetic, lookups and anything that has to be run go to a calculator,
                a search or code, not to the model’s memory (Lesson 18).
          8. 
              
        8. Iterate on the sentence that failed. Quote it, and name the criterion it missed.
          9. 
              
        9. If you want a decision, say which mistake would be worse and what can’t be undone (the LOSS line,
                Lesson 29).
          10. 
              
        10. Bound the length when you want density.
          11. 
              
        11. Name the use: what you will do with the answer (Lesson 13).
          12. 
              
        12. Ask for a restatement of the task, and check it before the work starts (Lesson 13).
          13. 
              
        13. Mark quoted material as quoted. It is evidence, not orders (TA5, Lesson 6).
          14. 
              
        14. Stop when the next step needs a check whose result you don’t have yet, and go and get it
                (Lesson 18; trigger 8 in Lesson 29).
          15. 
              
        15. Give the reason behind each constraint (TA3).
          16. 
              
        16. Say what to do, not only what to avoid (TA4, Lesson 15).
          17. 
              
        17. Bring the situation: level of the work, constraints, and stakes. Leave status and apologies out of a
                factual prompt (G6, Lesson 7).
          18. 
            
        
          
        


                    
        
            
        Operators
        
            
        Named operations you can run on a claim or a conversation. None of them is a new tool: each names
              something a lesson teaches and points to it. If an operator never changes the status of a claim in your
              sessions, it has become a costume. Each one carries the tag of the move it serves.
        
            
        
              
        
                
        
                  Id
                  Operator
                  What you do
                
        
                
        
                  O1
                  External binding touch
                  Stop generating. Write the bind plan, get the result, and interpret only what you pasted. No
                    result, no bind (Lesson 18).
                
        
                
        
                  O2
                  Premise quarantine close
                  Restate your premises as P1, P2…, mark each stipulated or unbound, and have recommendations cite
                    the premise ids. Invert one and see what falls (Lesson 8).
                
        
                
        
                  O3
                  Claim ledger close
                  Keep each claim the decision rests on as one line: id, claim, sense, status, test. A status
                    changes only with the check attached (the CLAIMS line, Lesson 29).
                
        
                
        
                  O4
                  Split generation from criticism close
                  Criticize in a fresh chat, memory off, that doesn’t know which version you prefer or who wrote
                    it (Lesson 30).
                
        
                
        
                  O5
                  Compact on purpose close
                  Write the state block, open a fresh chat and continue from the block, not from the thread
                    (Lesson 29).
                
        
                
        
                  O6
                  Sample, then read the disagreements close
                  Ask the same question in two or three fresh chats, memory off, and compare the claims, not the
                    wording. If the answers disagree in meaning, treat the question as unknown. If they agree, that
                    isn’t proof (Lesson 20).
                
        
                
        
                  O7
                  Costume detection close
                  Name the costume, and ask which claim would be different without it. Strip its favorite
                    sentences and see what claim is left (Lesson 21).
                
        
                
        
                  O8
                  Explicit loss close
                  Say which mistake would be worse, and check whether the advice changes if both mistakes cost the
                    same (the LOSS line, Lesson 29).
                
        
                
        
                  O9
                  Tag the mode close
                  Say whether you are exploring, converging or committing, and ask for the output that fits (the
                    MODE line, Lesson 29).
                
        
                
        
                  O10
                  Lock definitions close
                  Define every word the conclusion depends on before you ask for the answer (Lesson 12).
                
        
                
        
                  O11
                  Conversation as a system open
                  Look at the conversation itself as a system: name its loop, its delay, what piles up, and the
                    balancing loop you’re missing (the grammar of Lesson 24).
                
        
                
        
                  O12
                  Trace the source close
                  Ask where each claim comes from: a page the model opened, working it shows, its memory, or a
                    guess. The tags are the model’s own report, so they tell you where to check (Lesson 20).
                
        
                
        
                  O13
                  Ask for the distribution open
                  Ask for several substantively different answers with rough probabilities, unlikely ones
                    included (Lesson 27).
                
        
                
        
                  O14
                  Polyphony open
                  Convene voices that answer one another, and keep their disagreements instead of averaging them
                    (Lesson 27).
                
        
                
        
                  O15
                  Attribution swap close
                  Present your work as someone else’s, or the rival’s as yours, and compare the critiques
                    (Lesson 8).
                
        
                
        
                  O16
                  Clean the prompt close
                  Have the model rewrite your question without your opinions and emotions, then ask the cleaned
                    version in a fresh chat, memory off (Lesson 8).
                
        
                
        
                  O17
                  Change the representation open
                  Ask for the problem as a drawing, a table or a timeline (Lesson 27).
                
        
                
        
                  O18
                  Reverse the roles touch
                  You explain it in your own words, with the chat closed. Where you get stuck is the result. The
                    model can then look for gaps in what you wrote, and each gap it names is a claim to check
                    (Lessons 4 and 5).
                
        
                
        
                  O19
                  Language swap close
                  For a factual question, ask again in another language you read, as an invariance check
                    (Lesson 21).
                
        
                
        
                  O20
                  Make abstention usable close
                  Welcome “unknown”, and treat a claim you’ll act on as unanswered until it has a source you can
                    open or working you can recompute, whatever tag it carries (Lesson 20).
                
        
              
        
            
        
          
        


                    
        
            
        Failure modes and how they feel
        
            
        Each failure is described by how it feels from the inside, because mid-session you won’t be
              reading a list. You’ll be having the feeling and calling it progress.
        
            
        How a failure feels
        
            
        
              
        
                
        
                  Failure
                  It feels like
                  Interrupt
                
        
                
        
                  Fluent captivity
                  You learned a lot, and can’t restate one testable claim
                  Write the claims your decision rests on as one-line statements, each marked bound or unbound, with
                    no prose (the CLAIMS line, Lesson 29)
                
        
                
        
                  Sycophantic lock
                  Being unusually well understood
                  The swap test: the same facts in a fresh chat, memory off, with the opposite preference stated. Then
                    label your beliefs P1, P2… as hypotheses and have them evaluated before they are used
                    (Lesson 8)
                
        
                
        
                  Framework costume
                  Sophistication; nothing was excluded
                  Strike the framework’s vocabulary from the answer. Is there still a claim you could check?
                    (Lesson 21)
                
        
                
        
                  Precision mirage
                  “Finally, it’s concrete”
                  Ask how the number was measured. If there is no procedure, treat it as a rough range and mark it
                    unbound (Lesson 18)
                
        
                
        
                  Horizon escape
                  Finally talking about the real issue (civilization, psychology…)
                  Ask which level you are on, then go back to the pinned question and say what changed
                    (Lesson 25)
                
        
                
        
                  Local-optimum tutoring
                  A productive lesson, and no decision
                  Ask whether the next concept would change what you do. If you can’t tie it to DONE WHEN in one
                    sentence, park it and decide (Lesson 29)
                
        
                
        
                  Premise laundering
                  The mess has been organized, so it must be understood
                  Find the premise the tidy picture rests on, suppose the opposite, and see what still stands
                    (Lesson 8)
                
        
                
        
                  Critique theater
                  Fairness; no claim changed status
                  Hand the work to a fresh chat, memory off, to attack one named assumption or build a rival plan,
                    and do the rewrite yourself. A devil’s advocate in the same thread is a costume
                    (Lesson 30)
                
        
                
        
                  Context rot
                  Smarter and more confident, and contradicting turn three
                  Write the state block and start a fresh chat with it (Lesson 29). Leave out the rejected tries,
                    and put the passage the answer depends on next to the question (Lesson 3)
                
        
                
        
                  Agreement cascade
                  Momentum, alignment, speed
                  Hand the plan to a fresh chat, memory off, with the adversarial packet: find the single most
                    damaging flaw, and “broadly sound” is not an answer. Then state the option you rejected at
                    its strongest, as its best defender would (Lesson 30)
                
        
                
        
                  Emotion laundering
                  Started angry or afraid, now feel rational
                  Split it: what happened / what I feel / what I want to happen (Lesson 22). Which action still
                    stands on what happened alone?
                
        
                
        
                  Tool cosplay
                  Due diligence
                  Paste the result itself. A plan to look something up is not a lookup, and without a result the
                    claim stays unbound (Lesson 18)
                
        
                
        
                  Holon collapse
                  Seeing the whole picture, because many things were mentioned
                  Fill in the holon card and name the one level in focus this turn (Lesson 25)
                
        
                
        
                  Derailment
                  Being a serious thinker while the goal stalls
                  Name the trigger from the list in Lesson 29, or drop the challenge and return to the goal
                
        
                
        
                  Silent compliance
                  Excellent service, real craft, wrong goal
                  Before an expensive commit, check triggers 2 and 5 anyway: Contradiction and Proxy capture
                    (Lesson 29)
                
        
                
        
                  Protocol costume
                  Meta-confidence
                  Point at one claim whose status changed. If there is none, drop the framework vocabulary for three
                    turns (Lesson 21)
                
        
              
        
            
        

            
        Further failures
        
            
        Panorama. Mechanism: “consider everything” plus holistic vocabulary makes every
              connection look relevant, with no boundary and no decision. Feeling: depth, even awe.
              Everything is connected, and no decision is closer. Interrupt: what is the relation, and where does it
              stop holding? Another consideration is not a relation (Lesson 23).
        
            
        Reframe laundering. Mechanism: a reframe is a cheap, impressive continuation, and the
              new frame quietly replaces the goal without a trigger or a brake. Feeling: insight. “The real question
              is…” The original done-condition no longer applies, and nobody decided that. Interrupt: name trigger 4
              (Framing), write the action under both frames, and choose keep, revise or split (Lesson 29).
        
            
        Mitigated correction. Mechanism: politeness norms in the training data, and in you,
              soften disagreement into hedges and subordinate clauses. Feeling: it agrees with you, with nuance. The
              correction was there, after the “that said.” Interrupt: “List every point where you disagree with me, as
              bare statements, first” (Lesson 14).
        
            
        Persona inflation. Mechanism: prestige does not add the fact. Irrelevant biography can
              move accuracy down. Feeling: authority. Interrupt: drop the costume; give the constraints an expert would
              have (Lessons 7 and 15).
        
            
        Crutch learning. Mechanism: practice with help and performance without it come apart.
              Help that does the thinking for you lifts your score while you have it, and lowers it once the help is
              gone. evidence
              Feeling: ease, “I get it.” Interrupt: do the load-bearing part yourself, and check yourself later without
              help (Lesson 4).
        
            
        Comprehension debt. Mechanism: work piles up faster than anyone’s understanding of it.
              This happens with code, and also with a contract you signed after the model summarized it, or a plan you
              approved without reading. Feeling: speed, then fear of touching it. Interrupt: read what you would have
              to defend. Ask for an explanation of the existing thing before any change (TC13, TC14).
        
            
        Log-free success. Mechanism: “done” in a summary is a claim like any other, and the
              model may have written both the work and the check. Feeling: done. Interrupt: look at the output itself:
              the confirmation email, the sent folder, the test log. If the model could edit the check, the check is
              not a pass (Lesson 18).
        
            
        Citation mirage. Mechanism: citation formats are high-probability shapes, and a live
              link to a relevant page can still fail to support the sentence. A list of sources shows what the search
              returned, which is different from what was read. Feeling: rigor, because there are references. Interrupt: open the passage, not only the
              link (Lesson 19).
        
            
        Explanation compliance. The reason arrived, so the question feels settled. Interrupt:
              open the source, or write one way the answer is wrong, before reading the why (Lesson 8).
        
            
        Memory dominant. Feeling: it already knows you. Interrupt: read the app’s memory page,
              and delete conclusions it drew about you. If you didn’t say it today, treat it as unverified
              (Lesson 2).
        
            
        Middle way. It feels like wisdom, and nothing was negated. Interrupt: each original
              claim must still be visible, changed, and the new sentence must have been unavailable without them.
              Otherwise retract one (Lesson 26).
        
            
        Vigil. It feels like due diligence, line after line. Interrupt: compare the log to a
              written expected state. Do not wait to notice an unspecified anomaly (Lesson 29).
        
            
        Action handoff. It feels like velocity. The plan was accepted by being continued.
              Interrupt: you name the action. The model may draft it. If you cannot say what you would have rejected,
              you are out of the decision (Lesson 4).
        
          
        


        
        

        
        
          
        
            
        Back matter
        
            
        Glossary and sources
        
          
        

                    
        
            
        Glossary
        
            
        Each term in a line or two, with the lesson where it is earned.
        
            
        
              
        Adversarial pass
        
              
        A critique from outside your thread: a fresh chat with memory off, a named standard, the author hidden. It shows what to check and is not itself the check (Lesson 30).
        
              
        Agent
        
              
        A model with tools that acts for you: it searches, opens files, runs code, fills in forms and works through steps without you (Lesson 3, Lesson 6).
        
              
        Assistant
        
              
        A base model after training on human preferences. Its tone, its refusals and its pull toward the typical, agreeable answer come from that training (Lesson 3).
        
              
        Base model
        
              
        A model that only continues a document, with no assistant persona and no trained refusals. Most people never talk to one (Lesson 3).
        
              
        Bears load
        
              
        A claim bears load if your decision would change were it false (Lesson 18).
        
              
        Bind, binding
        
              
        Tying a claim to a check that isn’t another piece of model writing: a source you opened, a number you recomputed, a test you ran, a person who was there. A claim is unbound until it has been put to such a check and bound after it. If the check goes against it, it is contradicted (Lesson 18).
        
              
        Borrowed senses
        
              
        What a model has when it uses search, code, files or a browser. The thing to check is the trace of that touch (the page, the file, the output), not the model’s paragraph about it (Lesson 3).
        
              
        Boundary marker
        
              
        A contradiction where both sides are true in different contexts. Naming the boundary turns it into a map (Lesson 26).
        
              
        Brake
        
              
        A challenge to the goal that names its trigger, shows the action under both goals and waits for your answer: keep, revise or split. With an agent that acts, the brake is the product’s approval step (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Center
        
              
        What your picture of a thing is organized around right now: what you currently take it to be. A working choice, not the thing’s essence, purpose or main part (Lesson 23). The center of a conversation is the question you are actually asking and the frame you are asking it in (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Ceremony
        
              
        How much discipline a conversation gets, set by the stakes and by how hard the decision is to undo. There are three levels: 0 the floor, 1 the Six, 2 full discipline (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Charity
        
              
        Reading a source, a person or a model at its strongest before you judge it. The audit comes after (Lesson 9).
        
              
        Claim-status tags
        
              
        Labels you ask the model to put on each claim: opened, worked out, from memory, guess. They show where to look and are not a check (Lesson 20).
        
              
        Close
        
              
        The move that narrows: define, choose, commit, write down the state (Lesson 5).
        
              
        Compaction
        
              
        In a long session, the tool replaces earlier turns with a summary to make room. Details can drop out, including a constraint you stated early (Lesson 3).
        
              
        Consilience
        
              
        Independent lines of evidence, each with different blind spots, converging on one conclusion (Lesson 17).
        
              
        Context
        
              
        Everything the model has in front of it when it answers: the conversation, pasted files, the app’s instructions, and memory if it is on (Lesson 2).
        
              
        Costume
        
              
        A surface that looks like rigor, neutrality, criticism or depth without changing the status of any claim that bears load (Lesson 21).
        
              
        Cynefin
        
              
        Say “kuh-NEV-in”. A framework that sorts situations by how cause and effect relate: clear, complicated, complex, chaotic, confused (Lesson 28).
        
              
        Delta
        
              
        What changed since the pinned question, said on return. “Nothing changed” counts (Lesson 25).
        
              
        Derailment
        
              
        A sound goal keeps being challenged because the task got hard or a more interesting question turned up (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Deserved interlocutor
        
              
        Ukhtomsky’s phrase for the person who has done the inner work to meet the other as it is, and so can open without being fooled and close without shutting down (Lesson 7).
        
              
        Design tension
        
              
        A contradiction where you want both sides and they limit each other. Separate them, or choose and record what you gave up (Lesson 26).
        
              
        Dominant
        
              
        Ukhtomsky’s word for a focus of attention that pulls everything you notice toward itself. It helps when it is a question you are holding, and blinds when it is a conclusion you have already reached (Lesson 4, Lesson 10).
        
              
        Double
        
              
        Ukhtomsky’s word for the self you keep meeting in others when your attention rests on your own wishes, fears and rivalries. With a model, it is your hope coming back as a finding, or a person or a machine that you project onto it (Lesson 8).
        
              
        Drift
        
              
        A turn that quietly changes the goal, widens the scope or answers a neighboring question (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Effort level
        
              
        The setting some products give you for how much a reasoning model works before it answers. It pays on hard, checkable work and does little for taste or a quick lookup (Lesson 3).
        
              
        Fact map
        
              
        For a tangled situation: the facts collected on a page before any explanation, sorted by type, with where to check each, what is missing, and arrows you draw yourself (Lesson 10).
        
              
        Fertile tension
        
              
        A hedge that sits on a real fork in the question and is worth probing before you close. The test: ask what fact about your case would settle it (Lesson 14).
        
              
        Floor
        
              
        The few habits every conversation deserves: the eight on the Start here page. It is ceremony level 0 (Lesson 29). The appendix keeps a longer prompting checklist behind them, under Prompt basics.
        
              
        Foothold
        
              
        A temporary stable point in an open inquiry: something that holds for now and that the next question can be built on (Lesson 27).
        
              
        Frame
        
              
        The picture a wording calls up: what kind of situation this is and what belongs in it (Lesson 15).
        
              
        Fresh context
        
              
        A new chat with no history and memory off, or a temporary chat set to use nothing the app knows about you. It is the instrument for critique, swap tests and second estimates. Also called a fresh chat or a clean chat (Lesson 2, Lesson 8).
        
              
        Genre
        
              
        A named kind of document whose conventions, including its standard of evidence, come with the name: a referee report, a postmortem, a buyer’s guide (Lesson 15).
        
              
        Grounding
        
              
        Evidence that the other side understood. With a model you have to ask for it, and the cheapest form is a restatement before the work starts (Lesson 13).
        
              
        Hinge
        
              
        The single feature that, if it were different, would make this a different situation. Under time pressure it is the one thing to check in the source itself (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Holon
        
              
        Anything seen as both a whole made of parts and a part of a larger whole. It is the reason every conversation about a system sits at some level (Lesson 25).
        
              
        Invariance battery
        
              
        A set of one-minute checks that change the lean, the wording, the language, the format, the order, the role or the model, to see whether an answer holds (Lesson 21).
        
              
        Invariant-so-far
        
              
        An answer that survived changes of wording, frame and asker. It is still not checked against the world (Lesson 21).
        
              
        Jagged abilities
        
              
        Strong on one task and weak on a neighboring one, with human difficulty a poor guide to which is which (Lesson 3).
        
              
        Ladder of abstraction
        
              
        The climb from one concrete case to wider and wider categories. Move up and down it on purpose (Lesson 16).
        
              
        Ladder of inference
        
              
        The steps from observable data through selected data, meanings, assumptions and conclusions to beliefs and actions. When you disagree, walk back down to the data (Lesson 16).
        
              
        Library
        
              
        The text a model was trained on, seen as a compressed library of human reports. It is weighted by how often things were said, which is different from how true or important they are (Lesson 1, Lesson 19).
        
              
        Lock, unlock
        
              
        To lock a word is to fix the meaning of a word your conclusion depends on. To unlock it is to ask for its other meanings when you are exploring (Lesson 12).
        
              
        Log
        
              
        The record of what actually happened: the confirmation email, the sent folder, the tool’s output, the test run. It is what you read when a model reports that something is done (Lesson 18).
        
              
        Mode
        
              
        The single most typical answer to a request, which is what you get when you ask for “an idea” (Lesson 27). The MODE line of a state block is a different thing: it names the kind of work the session is doing (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Mode of existence
        
              
        A thing’s own logic of working, which shouldn’t be scored by another thing’s criteria (Lesson 3, Lesson 23).
        
              
        Moves, the three
        
              
        Open, close and touch: the three steps of the one rhythm the book teaches (Lesson 5).
        
              
        Name the act
        
              
        Saying which act you want (explore, explain, evaluate, decide, draft, build, check or teach) before the model picks one for you (Lesson 13).
        
              
        One-way door, two-way door
        
              
        A two-way door is a decision you can walk back: rewording an email, swapping a recipe. A one-way door is one you can’t, or only at great cost: quitting a job, signing a lease. Two-way doors get the floor, and one-way doors get full discipline (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Open
        
              
        The move that widens: other reports, other meanings, other options, a contradiction held (Lesson 5).
        
              
        Outer log
        
              
        The line of the state block that records each challenge to the goal, the trigger named, and whether you answered keep, revise or split (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Panorama
        
              
        Everything relates to everything, and nothing is decided. The costume of open thinking (Lesson 5, Lesson 23).
        
              
        Parked
        
              
        A good side idea written down as not this conversation’s job, and not discussed now. If you can name a trigger for it, it goes through the brake instead (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Polarity
        
              
        Two poles that depend on each other and never settle. You map and manage them instead of solving them (Lesson 26).
        
              
        Polyphony
        
              
        Independent voices that answer each other, with their disagreements kept and no narrator to reconcile them (Lesson 27).
        
              
        Portal
        
              
        Using the model to find terms, camps and kinds of sources, then going through to the sources themselves (Lesson 19).
        
              
        Premise laundering
        
              
        A guess of yours that comes back later in the thread as an established fact (Lesson 8).
        
              
        Probe
        
              
        A small trial you can afford to lose, with one signal for doing more of it and one for stopping. It is the way to touch a complex situation (Lesson 28).
        
              
        Product memory
        
              
        What an app stores about you between chats and can read into a new one. It is part of the context, and you didn’t type it there (Lesson 2, Lesson 6).
        
              
        Proxy capture
        
              
        A measure rising while the aim it stood for does not. It is also trigger 5 for challenging a goal (Lesson 24, Lesson 29).
        
              
        Quiet part
        
              
        What is true and common but rarely written about with feeling: the base rate, the dull majority case, the people who know and don’t post (Lesson 19).
        
              
        Reasoning model
        
              
        A model that spends extra computation on the problem before it answers and hides most of that work. What a product shows as “thinking” is at most a summary (Lesson 3).
        
              
        Remembered source, opened source
        
              
        A source the model recalls from training, and a page it fetched in this conversation. Both can be cited the same way. Only the second was read, and neither is checked until you open it (Lesson 19).
        
              
        Role with a standard
        
              
        A role that says what the answer must contain. A role that only changes how the answer sounds is a costume (Lesson 15, Lesson 21).
        
              
        Sample
        
              
        One more answer from a model. Two samples agreeing is not a check (Lesson 18).
        
              
        Session Card
        
              
        The form that holds a state block and writes the prompts that open, continue and close a session (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Signpost
        
              
        An observable sign with a threshold and a date that tells you an assumption has failed (Lesson 30).
        
              
        Silent compliance
        
              
        The goal is wrong and the model helps anyway, without saying so (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Six, the
        
              
        The six habits added to the floor when the outcome matters. Ceremony level 1 (Lesson 29).
        
              
        State block
        
              
        A short note you own that says where the conversation stands: the goal with its use and done-condition, your premises, the claims and whether each is checked, your commitments, and what is parked. You paste it into a long thread or a fresh one (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Status
        
              
        Where a claim stands right now: stipulated (taken as given for this conversation), unbound (not checked yet), invariant-so-far (it survived a change of wording or framing) or bound (checked against something outside the conversation). A bound claim that the check went against is contradicted (Lesson 17).
        
              
        Steelman
        
              
        Stating a view or a plan at its strongest, as its best defender would, before you criticize it. It is the principle of charity used as a first step (Lesson 9, Lesson 30).
        
              
        Stipulated
        
              
        Taken as given for this conversation and not checked. It is one of the statuses a claim or a premise can have (Lesson 17).
        
              
        Stock, flow, feedback loop
        
              
        A stock is something that accumulates: skill, trust, savings, lost sleep. Flows fill and drain it, delays separate causes from effects, and a feedback loop is either reinforcing, where more leads to more, or balancing, where more leads to less (Lesson 24).
        
              
        Swap test
        
              
        Ask the same question in a fresh chat, memory off, while stating the opposite preference. If the answer flips with no new evidence, it was tracking you (Lesson 8).
        
              
        Teach-back
        
              
        Explaining what you learned in your own words with the chat closed (Lesson 2). It is a touch turned inward (Lesson 5). Asking the model afterwards to find the holes in what you wrote is a critique, which shows you where to look (Lesson 4).
        
              
        The third
        
              
        What arises between two sides and belongs to neither: in a conversation, the shared sense of where things stand, or an idea neither brought. With a model its visible form is the growing text of the conversation (Lesson 2). The same question can be asked of any two things (Lesson 23).
        
              
        Three Others
        
              
        The model, people and reality: the three parties in every serious conversation. Most failures come from treating one as another (Lesson 9).
        
              
        Touch
        
              
        The move that checks against something that can say no. Outward: a source opened, a number recomputed, a test run, a person asked. Inward: you explain or apply it with the model out of the room. Another model answer is never a touch (Lesson 5).
        
              
        Trigger
        
              
        One of eight named reasons that make a challenge to the goal legitimate: harm, contradiction, false premise, framing, proxy capture, wrong level, silent override, check first (Lesson 29).
        
              
        Typical case
        
              
        What the model fills in for anything you didn’t say: the most common asker, situation and answer (Lesson 2, Lesson 7).
        
              
        Unanswered
        
              
        A claim you would act on that comes without a source you can open or working you can recompute, however confident it sounds (Lesson 20).
        
              
        Well
        
              
        A discipline or habit of mind that sees one part deeply and loses sight of the rest (Lesson 1).
        
              
        Wholeness
        
              
        The web of relations that makes a thing itself. A whole is a thing already cut out of the world, and wholeness is what the cut can lose (Lesson 23).
        
            
        
          
        


                    
        
            
        Sources
        
            
        Entries are grouped by topic and run alphabetically inside each group. Links are
              given only where they were checked. Everything else is cited by author, title and year, so you can find
              it and open it yourself, which is what this book asks you to do with any source. A preprint has not been
              through peer review. A vendor document is a company describing its own product. An industry measurement
              was published by a company, not a journal. Pages that change carry the date they were read.

                burden of proof in the first year). Read 10 November 2026.
